## Module 3 — Climate & Weather
## 1. Temperature
## 1. Introduction
Temperature is one of the most important climatic factors controlling crop growth, development,
flowering, maturity, yield, and geographical distribution.
In agriculture, temperature determines how quickly biological processes occur and whether a crop can
successfully complete its life cycle.
A simple relationship is:
Temperature→Physiological processes→Growth and development→Yield 
    
Temperature affects:
- seed germination
- root growth
- shoot growth
- photosynthesis
- respiration
- nutrient uptake
- water absorption
- transpiration
- flowering
- pollination
- fertilization
- fruit and seed development
- maturity
- dormancy
- senescence
- pest and disease development

- crop quality

2. What Is Temperature?
Temperature is a measure of the degree of hotness or coldness of a substance or environment.
In crop science, temperature generally refers to the thermal condition of the:
- air
- soil
- plant tissues
- crop canopy
- surrounding environment
The commonly used unit in agriculture is:
∘C 
Temperature is different from heat.
- Temperature describes thermal state.
- Heat is energy transferred because of a temperature difference.

3. Why Temperature Is Important in
Agriculture
Plants are living organisms whose biochemical reactions are temperature-dependent.
Temperature influences the rates of:
Enzyme activity→Metabolism→Growth→Development      
Every crop has a temperature range within which it can grow successfully.
Generally:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      
where:
- Tmin⁡T     = minimum temperature for a process
- ToptT     = optimum temperature

- Tmax⁡T     = maximum temperature for the process
Outside the suitable range, growth decreases and extreme temperatures can cause injury or death.

4. Cardinal Temperatures
The three most important temperatures for a crop process are called cardinal temperatures.

Minimum temperature
The lowest temperature at which a particular physiological process can proceed sufficiently.
Below this:
Growth rate→0  0
- r the process becomes extremely slow.

Optimum temperature
The temperature at which the particular process occurs at or near its maximum rate.

Maximum temperature
The highest temperature at which the process can proceed before severe inhibition or injury occurs.
Thus:
Tmin⁡→Topt→Tmax⁡  T     T      
The cardinal temperatures are process-specific. Germination, photosynthesis, flowering and grain
filling may have different temperature requirements.

5. Temperature Range of a Crop
A crop does not have one single temperature requirement throughout its entire life.
Different stages may require different temperatures.
For example:
Germination→Vegetative growth→Flowering→Grain/fruit filling→Maturity       
Each stage can have its own optimum temperature.

Therefore:
Crop temperature requirement changes with developmental stage.

6. Cool-Season and Warm-Season Crops
A broad agricultural classification is based on temperature preference.

Cool-season crops
Generally perform better under relatively cool conditions.
Examples include:
- wheat
- barley
- oats
- pea
- chickpea
- lentil
- many temperate vegetables

Warm-season crops
Generally require warmer conditions.
Examples include:
- rice
- maize
- sorghum
- pearl millet
- cotton
- groundnut
- soybean
- many tropical vegetables
This classification is broad rather than absolute.

7. Temperature and Seed Germination
Temperature strongly affects germination.
The general relationship is:
Temperature→Enzyme activity→Respiration→Reserve mobilization→Germination       
At temperatures that are too low:
- metabolic activity slows
- enzyme activity decreases
- germination becomes slow
- emergence may be delayed
- seedlings may become weak
At temperatures that are too high:
- membranes may be damaged
- enzymes can lose function
- respiration may become excessive
- seed viability may decline
- abnormal seedlings may develop

8. Temperature and Vegetative Growth
Temperature affects:
- cell division
- cell expansion
- leaf development
- root growth
- stem elongation
- branching
- tillering
- biomass accumulation

Within a suitable range:
Temperature↑⇒Growth rate↑    up to the optimum.
Beyond the optimum:
Temperature↑⇒Growth rate↓    because stress and metabolic limitations increase.

9. Temperature and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis is strongly temperature dependent.
The simplified process is:
6CO2+6H2O+Light→C6H12O6+6O26CO 2+6H 2O+   C 6H  12 
O 6+6O 2
Temperature influences:
- enzyme activity
- CO₂ fixation
- stomatal behavior
- electron transport
- membrane stability
- photorespiration
At temperatures above the optimum, photosynthesis may decline.
One reason is that high temperature can increase photorespiration in C3 plants and cause stomatal
closure under water stress.

10. Temperature and Respiration
Respiration is also temperature dependent:
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+EnergyC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
 
Within a suitable range:

Temperature↑⇒Respiration rate↑    But excessive temperature can cause severe stress and disrupt metabolism.
This creates an important agricultural relationship:
Net carbon gain≈Photosynthetic carbon gain−Respiratory carbon loss 
 -    
If respiration becomes excessively high while photosynthesis is inhibited, net biomass accumulation
can decrease.

11. Temperature and Transpiration
Temperature affects water loss from plants.
Higher temperature generally increases the atmospheric demand for water, especially when
accompanied by low humidity.
Thus:
Temperature↑→Atmospheric evaporative demand↑→Potential water loss↑ 
      
However, actual transpiration also depends on:
- humidity
- wind
- radiation
- stomatal conductance
- soil water
- leaf area
Therefore temperature alone does not determine transpiration.

12. Temperature and Water Requirement
Higher temperatures can increase crop water demand.
Under high temperature:
- transpiration can increase

- soil moisture can decline faster
- irrigation requirements may increase
- drought stress can develop more rapidly
A simplified relationship is:
High temperature + low soil moisture→greater heat/drought stress    

13. Temperature and Root Growth
Temperature affects:
- root elongation
- root branching
- root respiration
- nutrient uptake
- root microbial activity
Very low temperatures can slow root growth.
Very high temperatures can damage root tissues and increase respiration.
Soil temperature is especially important during:
- seed germination
- early establishment
- root development

14. Soil Temperature
Soil temperature is the temperature of the soil environment surrounding roots and seeds.
It influences:
- seed germination
- microbial activity
- nutrient mineralization
- root growth

- decomposition
- soil respiration
- water uptake
So:
Air temperature≠Soil temperature   
Both are important.

15. Temperature and Nutrient Availability
Temperature influences biological and chemical processes in soil.
It can affect:
- decomposition of organic matter
- microbial activity
- mineralization
- nutrient transformation
- root uptake
For example:
Organic matter→microbial activitymineral nutrients      
Temperature influences the rate of this biological activity.
However, nutrient availability is also controlled by:
- soil moisture
- pH
- aeration
- organic matter
- microbial communities
- soil texture

16. Temperature and Flower Initiation
Temperature can act as a signal controlling the transition from vegetative to reproductive growth.
Some plants require exposure to particular temperature conditions before flowering.
This phenomenon is particularly important in crops requiring vernalization.
Suitable cold exposure→Developmental competence→Flowering     

## 17. Vernalization
Vernalization is the induction or acceleration of flowering by exposure to an appropriate period of low
temperature in certain plants.
It is important in some:
- winter cereals
- biennial crops
- temperate crops
Examples include certain types of:
- wheat
- barley
- rye
- cabbage
- carrot
- beet
The exact temperature and duration depend on genotype.
Vernalization should not be confused with simply experiencing cold weather.

18. Temperature and Flowering
Temperature affects:
- flower initiation
- flower development

- flowering time
- anthesis
- pollen viability
- stigma receptivity
- flower retention
Extreme temperature around flowering can be particularly damaging.

19. High Temperature During Flowering
High temperature can cause:
- pollen sterility
- reduced pollen viability
- reduced stigma receptivity
- poor pollen tube growth
- flower abortion
- reduced fertilization
- reduced fruit/seed set
The result may be:
Heat stress during flowering→Poor reproductive success→Yield reduction     

20. Temperature and Pollination
Temperature can influence both plants and pollinating organisms.
Very high or low temperatures may affect:
- pollen release
- pollen viability
- stigma receptivity
- pollen tube growth
- insect activity

Therefore:
Suitable temperature→better pollination conditions    
while temperature extremes can reduce reproductive success.

21. Temperature and Fertilization
After pollination, pollen tubes must grow toward the ovule.
Temperature affects:
- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth
- gamete viability
- fertilization success
Extreme temperature during this stage can therefore reduce:
Seed set 
and:
Fruit set 

22. Temperature and Fruit/Seed Development
Temperature affects:
- cell division
- cell expansion
- carbohydrate accumulation
- protein synthesis
- oil accumulation
- starch deposition
- fruit size
- seed weight
For grain crops:

Temperature→Grain filling rate+Grain filling duration→Grain weight 
 +     

23. Temperature and Grain Filling
Grain filling is highly sensitive to temperature.
High temperature can:
- accelerate development
- shorten grain-filling duration
- increase respiration
- reduce assimilate accumulation
- decrease final grain weight
Thus:
High temperature→Faster development→Shorter filling period→Potentially lower grain weight         
The actual response depends on crop, genotype, water availability and timing.

24. Temperature and Maturity
Temperature influences how quickly a crop moves through developmental stages.
Higher temperatures can accelerate development within a suitable range.
Therefore:
Temperature→Development rate→Time to maturity     
But excessively high temperature can cause stress and abnormal development.

25. Heat Stress
Heat stress occurs when temperature becomes high enough, for long enough, to negatively affect plant
function.
It can cause:

- membrane instability
- protein damage
- enzyme dysfunction
- increased respiration
- reduced photosynthesis
- oxidative stress
- pollen sterility
- flower abortion
- accelerated senescence
- reduced yield

26. Heat Shock
A sudden exposure to unusually high temperature can cause heat shock.
Plants respond by activating protective mechanisms, including production of heat-shock proteins
(HSPs).
These proteins help protect and stabilize cellular proteins under stress.
Conceptually:
Heat stress→Cellular stress signals→Heat-shock response→Protective proteins      

27. Heat Stress and Reactive Oxygen Species
High temperature can disturb cellular metabolism and increase production of reactive oxygen species
(ROS).
Examples include:
- superoxide
- hydrogen peroxide
- hydroxyl radicals
Excessive ROS can damage:

- proteins
- lipids
- membranes
- nucleic acids
Plants therefore use antioxidant systems to control ROS.

28. Plant Responses to High Temperature
Plants may respond through:

Stomatal regulation
Stomata may close to reduce water loss.

Leaf rolling
Some crops roll leaves to reduce exposed surface area.

Increased antioxidant activity
Helps control oxidative stress.

Heat-shock proteins
Protect cellular proteins.

Changes in membrane composition
Help maintain membrane stability.

Root responses
Plants may modify root growth and water acquisition.

Earlier development
Some crops may accelerate developmental transitions under heat.

29. Low Temperature Stress
Low temperatures can also damage crops.
Effects include:

- slower germination
- reduced growth
- reduced enzyme activity
- impaired nutrient uptake
- membrane damage
- reduced photosynthesis
- delayed flowering
- chilling injury
- freezing injury

30. Chilling Stress
Chilling stress occurs at temperatures low enough to impair plant function but generally above the
freezing point of plant tissues.
It is particularly important for many warm-season crops.
Possible effects:
- membrane dysfunction
- reduced photosynthesis
- impaired metabolism
- poor germination
- slow growth

31. Freezing/Frost Stress
When plant tissues experience temperatures below their freezing tolerance, ice formation can cause
severe injury.
Freezing→Ice formation→Cellular damage     
Frost can damage:
- leaves
- flowers

- young shoots
- fruits
- buds
Young reproductive tissues are often particularly vulnerable.

## 32. Frost
Frost refers to conditions in which temperatures near the plant surface fall sufficiently low for frost or
freezing injury to occur.
Frost is especially important in:
- fruit orchards
- vegetables
- winter crops
- flowering crops
A crop can be damaged even when the standard air temperature measurement does not perfectly
represent the temperature at the plant surface.

33. Diurnal Temperature
Diurnal temperature variation is the difference between daytime and nighttime temperature.
For example:
- daytime: 30∘C30  C
- nighttime: 18∘C18  C
Then:
Diurnal range=30−18=12∘C =30-18=12  C
This temperature difference can influence:
- respiration
- carbohydrate accumulation
- flowering
- grain filling

- fruit quality

34. Day Temperature vs Night Temperature
Plants respond differently to day and night temperature.

High daytime temperature
May increase:
- transpiration
- heat stress
- respiratory losses

High nighttime temperature
Can increase:
- nighttime respiration
- carbon consumption
- developmental rate
Therefore:
Day temperature and night temperature can have different effects   

35. Night Temperature and Yield
High nighttime temperature can be particularly important in some crops because respiration continues
during darkness.
Conceptually:
High night temperature→Respiration↑→Carbon loss↑      If prolonged, this may reduce carbon available for growth and reproductive products.

36. Temperature and Crop Distribution
Temperature helps determine where crops can be successfully grown.
For example:
- tropical crops generally require warmer conditions
- temperate crops often require cooler conditions
- some crops require cold exposure for developmental reasons
Thus:
Climate suitability=f(temperature+water+other factors)  =
f( + + )  
Temperature is one of the major determinants of crop geography.

37. Temperature and Photoperiod Interaction
Temperature does not act independently of day length.
Flowering may depend on:
Temperature+Photoperiod+Genotype + + 
 
This is why the same variety may flower at different times under different environments.

38. Temperature and Crop Phenology
Phenology is the timing of developmental events.
Examples:
- germination
- emergence
- tillering
- flowering
- anthesis
- pod formation
- grain filling

- maturity
Temperature is one of the main environmental drivers of phenology.

39. Growing Degree Days (GDD)
Because crop development depends on accumulated temperature, agricultural scientists often use
growing degree days or thermal time.
A simplified formula is:
GDD=Tmean−Tbase  -T      
where:
- TmeanT     = mean daily temperature
- TbaseT     = base temperature below which development is assumed to be
negligible for the model.
Over multiple days:
GDDtotal=∑i=1n(Tmean,i−Tbase)   =    n 
(T   ,i -T    )  
More sophisticated crop models may impose upper temperature thresholds or use different equations.

40. Example of GDD
Suppose:
Tmean=25∘CT    =25  C
and:
Tbase=10∘CT    =10  C
Then:
GDD=25−10=15GDD=25-10=15
If this occurs for five days:
GDDtotal=15×5=75GDD    =15 5=75
This gives a measure of accumulated thermal time.

41. Why GDD Is Useful
GDD can help estimate:
- crop development
- flowering time
- maturity
- pest development
- irrigation scheduling in some systems
- harvest timing
- comparison of environments
However, GDD is a model/approximation and does not capture every biological response.
Water stress, photoperiod, nutrient status and extreme temperatures can also affect development.

42. Temperature and Pest Development
Temperature affects insects and other pests.
Within their suitable temperature range:
Temperature↑→Development rate↑    up to an optimum.
Temperature can influence:
- egg development
- larval development
- adult activity
- reproduction
- number of generations
Therefore crop temperature also affects pest pressure.

43. Temperature and Plant Diseases
Temperature influences:

- pathogen growth
- spore germination
- infection
- disease development
- host susceptibility
But disease development also depends strongly on:
- moisture
- humidity
- leaf wetness
- host resistance
- pathogen species
A useful conceptual framework is:
Disease=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment)  = f( + +
 )  
Temperature is one component of the environment.

44. Temperature and Weed Growth
Temperature affects:
- weed seed germination
- emergence
- growth
- flowering
- reproduction
Therefore temperature influences the timing and intensity of weed competition.

45. Temperature and Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration is the combined water loss from:
Evaporation+Transpiration +   

Temperature is an important component influencing atmospheric evaporative demand.
Higher temperature generally increases potential evapotranspiration when other conditions are suitable.
This is important for:
- irrigation planning
- drought management
- crop water requirement estimation

46. Temperature and Irrigation
During periods of high temperature:
- water demand may increase
- soil dries faster
- plants may experience heat + drought stress simultaneously
Therefore irrigation scheduling should consider:
Temperature+Rainfall+Soil moisture+Crop stage+Evapotranspiration  +
  +   +   +    

47. Temperature and Crop Quality
Temperature can influence:
- grain quality
- protein accumulation
- oil accumulation
- sugar concentration
- fruit color
- firmness
- aroma
- acidity
- fiber quality
Thus temperature affects not only yield, but also quality.

48. Temperature Effects at Different Crop
Stages
Crop stage
Important temperature effects
Seed
Germination and viability
Germination
Enzyme activity and emergence
Seedling
Root/shoot growth
Vegetative
Biomass, leaf area, tillering
Flower initiation Developmental transition
Flowering
Anthesis and reproductive success
Pollination
Pollen viability and pollen tube growth
Fertilization
Reproductive success
Grain/fruit filling Dry matter accumulation
Maturity
Development rate
Senescence

Post-harvest

49. Temperature and Crop Yield
Yield can be viewed conceptually as:
Yield=f(Genotype,Temperature,Water,Nutrition,Light,Management,…)  =
f( , , , , , ,
 )  
Temperature can influence yield through:
Germination→Growth→Flowering→Pollination→Grain/Fruit Set→Filling 
     A temperature stress at a sensitive stage can have a disproportionately large effect on final yield.

50. Temperature Stress + Other Stresses
Temperature rarely acts alone.

Heat + drought
Heat+Water shortage→Severe crop stress +    

Cold + waterlogging
Can reduce root metabolic activity and oxygen availability.

Heat + high humidity
Can increase disease risk for some pathogens while reducing evaporative cooling.

Heat + nutrient stress
Can reduce nutrient uptake and increase physiological stress.
Therefore:
Crop response=f(multiple interacting stresses)  = f( )  

51. Temperature Measurement
Agricultural temperature observations may include:

Air temperature
Measured using appropriate meteorological instruments and standardized exposure.

Soil temperature
Measured at specified soil depths.

Canopy temperature
Measured using:
- infrared thermometers
- thermal sensors
- thermal cameras

Leaf temperature
Can differ from air temperature because of:
- transpiration
- radiation
- wind
- humidity

52. Air Temperature vs Canopy Temperature
These are not necessarily identical.
A well-watered transpiring crop can sometimes maintain leaf temperatures below surrounding air
temperature.
Under drought:
Stomatal closure→Transpiration↓→Cooling↓→Canopy temperature↑ 
      Therefore canopy temperature can provide information about plant water status in some applications.

53. Temperature Inversions
Normally, air temperature tends to decrease with increasing altitude in the lower atmosphere.
Under certain conditions, especially clear and calm nights, a temperature inversion can develop in
which colder air is near the surface and warmer air occurs above it.
This matters in agriculture because it can influence:
- frost risk
- air mixing
- orchard microclimate
- dispersion of materials in the atmosphere

## 54. Microclimate
A microclimate is the climate condition in a small localized environment.
Examples:
- under a crop canopy
- near soil surface
- inside a greenhouse
- inside an orchard
- beneath mulch

Temperature inside the canopy may differ from temperature measured in an open weather station.
Factors include:
- canopy density
- shading
- soil moisture
- wind
- radiation
- plant height

55. Temperature in Protected Cultivation
In greenhouses and polyhouses, temperature can be managed using:
- ventilation
- shading
- evaporative cooling
- heating
- thermal screens
- irrigation/fogging systems
- structural design
The goal is not simply maximum temperature control but maintaining a suitable crop environment.

56. Temperature Management in Agriculture
Farmers can modify crop exposure to temperature through:

Crop selection
Choose crops and varieties suited to local temperature conditions.

Sowing date
Adjust planting so sensitive developmental stages avoid temperature extremes.

Irrigation
Water can reduce heat stress partly through transpiration when plants have adequate water.

Mulching
Can moderate soil temperature and conserve soil moisture.

Shade
Can reduce excessive radiation and canopy temperature in some situations.

Windbreaks
Can modify local microclimate and reduce wind-related stress.

Protected cultivation
Allows greater control of temperature.

Variety selection
Choose varieties with appropriate heat/cold tolerance and maturity characteristics.

57. Heat Avoidance vs Heat Tolerance
Plants can cope with heat in different ways.

Heat avoidance
The plant reduces exposure to high temperature.
Examples:
- early flowering
- altered leaf orientation
- transpiration cooling

Heat tolerance
The plant maintains physiological function despite high temperature.
Examples:
- heat-stable proteins
- membrane stability
- antioxidant protection

- reproductive tolerance

58. Acclimation vs Adaptation
These terms are different.

Acclimation
A reversible physiological adjustment made by an individual plant in response to environmental
conditions.

Adaptation
A heritable characteristic that becomes associated with populations through evolutionary processes.
Therefore:
Acclimation=individual adjustment =   
Adaptation=heritable population-level characteristic =   

59. Temperature and Climate Change
Long-term changes in temperature can affect agriculture through:
- changes in growing season
- heat-wave frequency
- frost patterns
- crop suitability
- flowering dates
- pest populations
- disease patterns
- irrigation demand
- yield stability
A particularly important concern is not only average temperature but also:
Temperature extremes   
such as heat waves and unusual cold events.

60. Temperature and Growing Season
The growing season is broadly the period during which environmental conditions allow crop growth.
Temperature influences:
- beginning of growth
- developmental rate
- flowering
- maturity
- crop duration
Changes in temperature can therefore alter the timing of agricultural operations.

61. Temperature and Crop Calendar
The crop calendar depends partly on temperature.
For example:
Temperature conditions→Sowing window→Crop establishment→Flowering→Harvest window          
However, crop calendars also depend on:
- rainfall
- irrigation
- soil
- photoperiod
- variety
- local farming systems

63. Temperature: The Complete Agricultural
Relationship
You can remember the entire topic through this chain:
Temperature→Enzyme Activity→Metabolism→Photosynthesis/
Respiration→Growth→Development→Flowering→Pollination/
Fertilization→Filling→Maturity→Yield & Quality             
And temperature interacts with:
Temperature+Water+Light+Nutrition+Genotype+Management  +
  +   +   +   +    
to determine crop performance.

64. Final Conceptual Summary
The five most important ideas
1. Every crop and developmental process has a temperature range

Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡T    <T    <T    
2. Temperature controls the rate of development
Temperature→Development rate→Crop duration   3. Extreme temperatures cause stress
Heat→Heat stress  Cold→Chilling/Frost stress 
 4. Temperature is particularly important during sensitive stages
Especially:
- germination
- flowering
- pollination
- fertilization
- grain/fruit filling
5. Temperature affects both yield and quality
Temperature→Growth + Development + Reproduction + Maturity→Yield + Quality     

One-line memory
Temperature is the thermal regulator of crop life—from germination to harvest—
because it controls the rate of physiological and developmental processes and
determines whether those processes occur within a safe range.

Minimum Temperature
## 1. Introduction
Minimum temperature is the lowest temperature at which a particular biological or
developmental process can occur at a meaningful rate.
In agriculture, minimum temperature is important because every crop and every developmental process
has a lower thermal limit below which normal activity becomes extremely slow or stops.
The basic relationship is:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      

where:
- Tmin⁡T     = minimum temperature
- ToptT     = optimum temperature
- Tmax⁡T     = maximum temperature
However, an important point is that minimum temperature is process-specific. The minimum
temperature for seed germination may not be the same as the minimum temperature for flowering,
photosynthesis, or grain filling.

## 2. Definition
Minimum temperature
The minimum temperature or base temperature (TbT b) is the lower temperature threshold below
which a particular crop developmental process is assumed to have negligible or essentially zero
progress.
In crop-growth models:
T<Tb⇒development rate≈0  0  
At temperatures above the base temperature, development generally begins and increases toward an
- ptimum, although the exact relationship is not always linear.

3. Minimum Temperature vs Base Temperature
These terms are closely related but should not always be treated as perfectly identical.

Minimum temperature
Can refer generally to the lowest suitable temperature for a particular biological process.

Base temperature
A specific threshold used in crop-development calculations, especially thermal-time or growingdegree-day models.
Thus:
Base temperature=model-defined lower developmental threshold  =
   
For practical crop science, the two are often discussed together.

4. Why Minimum Temperature Is Important
Minimum temperature influences:
- seed germination
- seedling emergence
- root growth
- shoot growth
- leaf expansion
- tillering
- branching
- photosynthesis
- respiration
- flowering
- reproductive development
- grain filling
- maturity
- pest and disease development
Therefore:
Tmin⁡→determines whether development can proceed   

5. Minimum Temperature Is Not the Same as
the Coldest Temperature
This distinction is important.
Minimum temperature for a process is a biological threshold.
Daily minimum air temperature is a weather observation—the lowest air temperature recorded
during a particular day.
For example:

- Daily minimum temperature = 8∘C8  C
- Crop base temperature = 5∘C5  C
The crop may still accumulate some thermal time.
Therefore:
Weather minimum temperature≠biological minimum temperature   

6. Minimum Temperature and Germination
Seeds require a suitable temperature range for germination.
Conceptually:
T<Tmin⁡→very slow or negligible germination    
As temperature increases toward the optimum:
Germination rate↑  until excessive temperature begins to inhibit germination.

Effects of temperatures below the minimum
- slow water metabolism after imbibition
- reduced enzyme activity
- slow reserve mobilization
- reduced respiration
- delayed radicle emergence
- delayed seedling emergence

7. Minimum Temperature and Enzyme Activity
Plant metabolism depends on enzymes.
At very low temperatures:
Low temperature→enzyme activity↓→metabolic reactions↓ 
     
This is one reason crop growth slows under cold conditions.

8. Minimum Temperature and Respiration
Respiration continues at low temperatures, but its rate generally decreases.
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+energyC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
 
At low temperature:
Respiration rate↓  This can reduce energy availability for active growth.
However, extremely low temperature can also cause cellular injury, particularly in crops that are not
cold tolerant.

9. Minimum Temperature and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis is also temperature dependent.
At temperatures below the suitable range:
- enzyme reactions slow
- membrane processes can be affected
- carbon fixation decreases
- growth slows
Therefore:
T<Topt→Photosynthetic capacity may decline     
The exact response differs among crops and temperatures.

10. Minimum Temperature and Root Growth
Low temperature can reduce:
- root elongation
- root branching
- root respiration

- nutrient uptake
- water absorption
This is especially important during early crop establishment.
Low soil temperature→Root activity↓→Seedling growth↓ 
     

11. Minimum Soil Temperature
For seeds and roots, soil temperature can be more relevant than air temperature.
A seed planted in cold soil may germinate slowly even if daytime air temperature becomes relatively
warm.
So:
Seed germination≈f(soil temperature)   f( )  
particularly during early establishment.

12. Minimum Temperature and Seedling
Emergence
Low temperature can cause:
- delayed emergence
- slow root growth
- weak seedlings
- prolonged exposure to soil pathogens
- uneven crop stand
Therefore, sowing time should consider whether soil temperature is suitable for the crop.

13. Minimum Temperature and Vegetative
Growth
Low temperatures can slow:

- cell division
- cell expansion
- leaf development
- stem elongation
- branching
- tillering
Consequently:
Low temperature→Growth rate↓     

14. Minimum Temperature and Crop Duration
When temperature remains close to or below the developmental base temperature, crop development
slows.
Therefore:
Lower temperature→Development rate↓→Crop duration↑ 
     
This is one reason crops may take longer to reach maturity under cooler conditions.

15. Thermal Time and Minimum Temperature
Minimum/base temperature is particularly important in growing degree day (GDD) calculations.
A simplified daily formula is:
GDD=Tmean−Tb  -T b  
where:
- TmeanT     = daily mean temperature
- TbT b = base temperature
If:
Tmean=20∘CT    =20  C
and:
Tb=5∘CT b=5  C

then:
GDD=20−5=15GDD=20-5=15
Thus the crop accumulates approximately 15 degree-days for that day under this simple model.

16. What Happens Below the Base
Temperature?
Suppose:
Tb=10∘CT b=10  C
and:
Tmean=7∘CT    =7  C
The simple GDD equation gives:
GDD=7−10=−3GDD=7-10=-3
But we normally do not treat this as negative crop development.
A simple approach is:
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)  -T b)  
Therefore:
GDD=max⁡(0,7−10)=0GDD= (0,7-10)=0
This means no thermal time is accumulated under the simplified model.

17. Minimum Temperature and Vernalization
Minimum temperature should not be confused with vernalization.
Some crops actually require exposure to low temperatures to trigger or accelerate flowering.
For example, certain winter cereals require a period of appropriate cold exposure.
Thus:
Low temperature can sometimes be a developmental signal   
rather than simply a stress.

18. Minimum Temperature and Flowering
Low temperatures can affect flowering in two different ways.

Appropriate low temperature
May promote flowering in vernalization-responsive crops.

Excessively low temperature
May cause:
- delayed development
- flower damage
- reduced pollen viability
- delayed anthesis
- poor fertilization
Therefore:
Beneficial cold exposure≠damaging cold stress 
  

19. Minimum Temperature and Pollination
Temperature around flowering can influence:
- pollen viability
- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth
- stigma receptivity
- pollinator activity
If temperatures become too low for a crop's reproductive process:
Pollination success↓  which can ultimately reduce:
Fruit set/seed set↓  20. Minimum Temperature and Grain Filling
Low temperatures during grain filling can slow:
- starch accumulation
- protein accumulation
- dry matter deposition
- grain development
Consequently:
Low temperature→Grain filling rate↓     
However, the exact effect depends on crop and genotype.

21. Minimum Temperature and Maturity
Development toward maturity is temperature-dependent.
When temperatures remain low:
Development rate↓→Days to maturity↑     
Therefore temperature influences crop duration and harvest timing.

22. Chilling Stress
A temperature can be above freezing but still be damaging to a crop.
This is called chilling stress.
It is particularly important for many warm-season crops.
Effects can include:
- membrane dysfunction
- reduced photosynthesis
- slow growth
- poor germination
- reduced nutrient uptake

- reproductive injury
Thus:
Low temperature≠only freezing injury   

23. Freezing Stress
When plant tissues reach temperatures at which ice formation occurs, severe cellular injury may result.
Freezing→Ice formation→Cellular damage     
Possible damage:
- membrane rupture
- tissue death
- leaf injury
- flower injury
- fruit injury
- shoot death
The critical temperature depends strongly on species, cultivar, tissue, developmental stage and
exposure duration.

24. Frost and Minimum Temperature
Frost is related to very low temperatures near the surface.
Frost risk is especially important for:
- flowering fruit trees
- vegetables
- young seedlings
- sensitive crops
A weather station's minimum air temperature does not always exactly equal the temperature
experienced by a particular plant tissue.
Microclimate matters.

25. Duration Matters
Temperature stress is not determined by temperature alone.
Two situations can be different:

Short exposure
2∘C for a short period2  C  

Long exposure
2∘C for many hours2  C  
The biological effects can differ substantially.
Therefore:
Temperature stress=f(temperature+duration+developmental stage)  =
f( + + )  

26. Minimum Temperature and Crop Stage
Sensitivity varies throughout the crop life cycle.
Stage

Effect of low temperature
Seed
Slow germination
Germination
Reduced metabolic activity
Seedling
Slow growth/injury
Vegetative
Reduced biomass accumulation
Flower initiation May delay or alter flowering
Flowering
Possible reproductive injury
Pollination
Reduced pollen performance
Fertilization
Reduced reproductive success
Grain filling
Slower filling
Maturity
Delayed development

27. Minimum Temperature and Different Crops
The exact minimum/base temperature differs among crops and varieties.
Broadly:

Crop group
General temperature tendency
Wheat/barley/oats Relatively cool-adapted
Chickpea/lentil/pea Relatively cool-season
Rice
Warm-season
Maize
Warm-season
Sorghum
Warm-season
Pearl millet
Warm-season
Cotton
Warm-season
Groundnut
Warm-season
These are broad classifications; the actual thresholds depend on genotype and developmental stage.

28. Why There Is No Universal Minimum
Temperature
It would be incorrect to say:
“The minimum temperature for all crops is X°C.”
There is no universal value because minimum temperature depends on:
- crop species
- cultivar/genotype
- developmental stage
- physiological process
- acclimation
- duration of exposure
- soil vs air temperature
- water availability
- photoperiod
- nutritional status
Therefore:
Tmin⁡=f(crop+genotype+process+stage) =f( + +
 + )  

29. Minimum Temperature and Crop
Distribution
The lower thermal limit contributes to determining where a crop can be cultivated.
A crop may require:
- sufficiently warm conditions for germination
- suitable temperature for vegetative growth
- appropriate temperature for flowering
- suitable conditions for maturity
Therefore:
Crop suitability≈f(minimum temperature+maximum temperature+water+other climate factors)
   f( + +
 + )  

30. Minimum Temperature and Sowing Date
Farmers often select sowing dates so that early crop development occurs under suitable temperatures.
If sowing occurs too early:
Cold soil→Slow germination→Delayed emergence    If sowing occurs too late:
the crop may encounter excessive heat during later sensitive stages.
Thus temperature is an important factor in determining the crop calendar.

31. Minimum Temperature and Pest/Disease
Development
Minimum temperature also influences organisms associated with crops.
For some insects and pathogens:
T<Tb→development slows substantiallyT<T b  As temperature rises into their suitable range, development may accelerate.

Thus temperature affects the timing of:
- pest emergence
- pest reproduction
- pathogen development
- disease epidemics

32. Minimum Temperature and Growing Season
The growing season can be influenced by the period during which temperature remains suitable for
crop development.
Conceptually:
Temperature above developmental threshold→development can proceed    
Temperature falling below the threshold can slow or stop development in thermal-time models.

34. Minimum vs Maximum Temperature
These define opposite limits.
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      

Below minimum
Development↓  Around optimum
Development↑  Above optimum
Development↓  Extreme high or low temperature
Stress↑  35. Minimum Temperature and Crop Models
Crop simulation models use temperature thresholds to estimate development.
A simplified model may assume:
r(T)= 0,T≤Tbk(T−Tb),Tb<T<Toptreduced rate,T≥Toptr(T)=   0, & T T b\\ k(T-T b),
& T b<T<T    \\  , & T T      
where r(T)r(T) is developmental rate.
Real crops are often more complex, so sophisticated models use nonlinear temperature-response
functions.

36. Minimum Temperature and Climate Risk
Changes in nighttime minimum temperature can be agriculturally important.
For example:

Higher minimum/night temperature
May increase:
- respiration
- water demand
- heat stress
- developmental rate

Lower minimum temperature
May increase:
- chilling risk

- frost risk
- delayed development
Therefore monitoring minimum temperature, not just maximum temperature, is important.

37. Minimum Temperature and Night
Temperature
Daily minimum temperature often occurs during the late night or early morning under typical
conditions.
Night temperature influences:
- respiration
- recovery from daytime heat
- flowering
- reproductive processes
- crop development
A high minimum temperature can therefore be an important heat-stress indicator.

38. Temperature Inversion and Minimum
Temperature
On clear, calm nights, the ground can lose heat rapidly.
This can result in:
Surface cooling→Cold air near ground→Frost risk    This is especially relevant in:
- orchards
- low-lying fields
- frost-sensitive crops

39. Managing Low-Temperature Risk
Possible agricultural strategies include:

Selecting suitable varieties
Choose cultivars with appropriate cold tolerance.

Adjusting sowing dates
Avoid exposing sensitive stages to severe cold.

Irrigation
Under specific frost-management conditions, water application can provide thermal protection through
heat release during freezing, but it must be properly managed.

Mulching
Can modify soil temperature.

Protected cultivation
Structures can reduce exposure to cold.

Wind machines
Used in some orchard frost-management systems under suitable inversion conditions.

Site selection
Avoiding frost-prone locations can reduce risk.

40. Important Formula: Thermal Time
The simplest thermal-time model is:
TT=∑i=1nmax⁡(0,Tmean,i−Tb)   n  (0,T   ,i -T b)  
where:
- TTTT = accumulated thermal time
- Tmean,iT   ,i  = mean temperature on day ii
- TbT b = base temperature
This helps estimate developmental progress.

However, temperatures above an upper threshold may need special treatment in more sophisticated
models.

## 41. Example
Suppose a crop has:
Tb=8∘CT b=8  C
and the mean temperatures for three days are:
15∘C,12∘C,6∘C15  C, 12  C, 6  C
Daily thermal contributions:
15−8=715-8=7 12−8=412-8=4 6−8<0⇒06-8<0 0
Therefore:
TT=7+4+0=11TT=7+4+0=11
So the crop accumulates approximately:
11 degree-days  
under this simple model.

42. Key Distinctions
Minimum temperature vs base temperature
Closely related concepts, but base temperature is specifically a developmental-model threshold.

Minimum temperature vs minimum daily temperature
One is a biological threshold; the other is a weather measurement.

Minimum temperature vs chilling temperature
The minimum developmental threshold does not necessarily equal the temperature at which chilling
injury begins.

Minimum temperature vs frost temperature
Frost injury depends on tissue temperature, duration, acclimation and other factors, not simply one
universal air-temperature number.

44. Final Takeaway
The most important idea is:
Minimum temperature is the lower thermal threshold below which a particular crop process becomes e
xtremely slow or is treated as having negligible development.   
It controls the lower thermal boundary for processes such as:
Germination→Emergence→Vegetative growth→Flowering→Grain/Fruit filling→Maturity        
And in crop modelling:
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)  -T b)  

Remember:
Minimum temperature tells us how cold is too cold for a particular developmental
process—not simply how cold the weather is.

Maximum Temperature
## 1. Introduction
Maximum temperature is the upper thermal limit associated with a particular biological or
developmental process beyond which the process becomes increasingly inhibited and, at sufficiently
high temperatures, plant tissues may suffer heat injury.
In crop science, maximum temperature is commonly considered together with minimum and optimum
temperatures:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      
where:
- Tmin⁡T     = minimum/base temperature
- ToptT     = optimum temperature
- Tmax⁡T     = maximum temperature
A very important point is that maximum temperature is process-specific. The maximum temperature
for seed germination, photosynthesis, flowering, or grain filling may be different.

## 2. Definition
Maximum temperature
The maximum temperature for a biological process is the upper temperature limit beyond which that
process can no longer proceed normally and begins to decline sharply or becomes severely inhibited.
In practical crop science:
T>Tmax⁡→severe inhibition and/or heat injury    
However, maximum temperature should not be interpreted as one universal temperature at which every
crop immediately dies.

3. Maximum Temperature vs Daily Maximum
Temperature
These are two different concepts.

Maximum biological temperature
A temperature threshold related to a particular plant process.

Daily maximum temperature
The highest air temperature recorded during a particular day.
Therefore:
Biological Tmax⁡≠daily weather maximum T      
For example, a weather station may record a daily maximum of 40∘C40  C, but the temperature
experienced by a leaf may be different because of:
- transpiration
- radiation
- wind
- humidity
- canopy structure
- soil water availability

4. Cardinal Temperature Relationship
The three cardinal temperatures describe the general temperature-response range:
Tmin⁡→Topt→Tmax⁡  T     T      

Below Tmin⁡T    
Development becomes very slow or negligible.

Around ToptT    
The process operates efficiently.

Above ToptT    
The process begins to decline.

Near or above Tmax⁡T    
Severe physiological stress and injury can occur.

5. Why Maximum Temperature Matters in
Agriculture
High temperature affects almost every important crop process:
- seed germination
- seedling establishment
- photosynthesis
- respiration
- transpiration
- water use
- root growth
- leaf development
- flowering
- pollen viability
- fertilization
- fruit set
- grain filling
- maturity
- senescence
- crop quality
- post-harvest storage
The overall relationship can be represented as:
Excess temperature→Heat stress→Physiological damage→Yield/quality loss      

6. Maximum Temperature and Heat Stress
Heat stress occurs when temperature becomes sufficiently high to disrupt normal plant physiological
processes.
It can cause:
- enzyme dysfunction
- membrane instability
- protein damage
- excessive respiration
- reduced photosynthesis
- oxidative stress
- water loss
- reproductive failure
- premature senescence

7. Temperature Response Curve
Crop processes often have a response similar to:
Process rate
│
│
/\
│
/
\
│
/
\
│
/
\
│ /
\ 
│
└──────────────────────────────────── Temperature
Tmin
Topt
Tmax

The general pattern is:
Low temperature→slow process    
Optimum temperature→maximum/near-maximum process rate 
  
High temperature→declining process rate    

8. Maximum Temperature and Seed
Germination
Seeds require an appropriate temperature range for germination.
At excessively high temperatures:
- membranes may be damaged
- enzymes may lose function
- respiration can become excessive
- stored reserves may be depleted abnormally
- embryo viability may decline
- germination percentage may decrease
Therefore:
T>Topt→Germination rate↓      
and at sufficiently extreme temperatures:
T>Tmax⁡→Germination may fail    

9. Maximum Temperature and Seedling Growth
Young seedlings are often vulnerable to heat.
High temperature can cause:
- rapid water loss
- leaf scorching
- root damage
- reduced photosynthesis
- poor establishment
- growth reduction
If soil also becomes very hot:
High soil temperature→Root stress→Water/nutrient uptake problems 
  10. Maximum Temperature and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis is particularly sensitive to excessive temperature.
6CO2+6H2O+light→C6H12O6+6O26CO 2+6H 2O+   C 6H  12 O 6+6O 2
High temperature can reduce photosynthesis through:
- enzyme instability
- reduced Rubisco performance
- membrane damage
- impaired electron transport
- stomatal closure
- increased photorespiration in C3 plants
- oxidative stress
Therefore:
High temperature→Photosynthesis↓     

11. Maximum Temperature and Stomata
Under heat and water stress, plants may close their stomata.
The sequence can be:
High temperature→Water loss↑→Water stress→Stomatal closure 
     
Stomatal closure reduces water loss but also restricts CO₂ entry.
Therefore:
Stomatal closure→CO2 availability↓→Photosynthesis↓   CO 2     
This creates a trade-off between water conservation and carbon fixation.

12. Maximum Temperature and Respiration
Respiration generally increases with temperature within a suitable range.

C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+EnergyC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
 
At high temperatures:
Respiration↑   
until severe stress disrupts metabolism.
This can consume more of the carbon produced by photosynthesis.
Therefore:
High temperature→Photosynthesis↓+Respiration↑→Net carbon gain↓ 
  +      
This is one important mechanism by which heat reduces biomass accumulation.

13. Maximum Temperature and Transpiration
Higher temperature generally increases atmospheric demand for water.
If sufficient water is available:
Temperature↑→Transpiration demand↑    Transpiration can provide evaporative cooling.
Thus:
Water availability→Transpiration→Leaf cooling     
But if soil water becomes limiting, stomata may close and cooling capacity can decline.

14. Heat + Drought Stress
One of the most important agricultural combinations is:
High temperature+water shortage +   
The sequence can be:
Heat→Evaporative demand↑→Water loss↑      while:

Drought→Water availability↓    Together:
Heat + drought→strong physiological stress    

15. Maximum Temperature and Root Growth
High soil temperature can affect:
- root elongation
- root branching
- root respiration
- membrane stability
- nutrient uptake
- root water uptake
Extreme temperatures can damage root tissues.
Thus:
High soil temperature→Root function↓     

16. Maximum Temperature and Flower
Initiation
Temperature can influence the timing of reproductive development.
Excessive temperature may:
- alter developmental signals
- accelerate development
- disturb reproductive timing
- interact with photoperiod
- cause stress before flowering
Therefore temperature can change the timing and success of the transition from vegetative to
reproductive growth.

17. Maximum Temperature During Flowering
Flowering is often one of the most temperature-sensitive stages.
High temperature can cause:
- flower abortion
- reduced pollen viability
- pollen sterility
- reduced stigma receptivity
- reduced pollen tube growth
- reduced fertilization
- reduced fruit/seed set
Therefore:
Heat during flowering→Reproductive failure→Yield reduction 
   

18. Maximum Temperature and Pollen
Pollen can be particularly sensitive to heat.
Excessive temperature may affect:
- pollen formation
- pollen viability
- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth
Therefore:
High temperature→Pollen viability↓→Fertilization↓       
This can be especially important when heat occurs around anthesis.

19. Maximum Temperature and Fruit Set
Successful fruit set requires:
Flowering→Pollination→Pollen tube growth→Fertilization→Fruit set      High temperature can disrupt several steps.
Therefore:
Heat stress→Fruit set↓     
in sensitive crops and conditions.

20. Maximum Temperature and Grain Filling
High temperature during grain filling can be particularly harmful.
It can:
- increase respiration
- shorten grain-filling duration
- reduce photosynthesis
- accelerate senescence
- reduce starch accumulation
- reduce final grain weight
Conceptually:
High temperature→Grain filling duration↓     
and:
Grain weight↓   
if assimilate accumulation is insufficient.

21. Maximum Temperature and Maturity
High temperature can accelerate development.

Therefore:
Higher temperature→Development rate↑→Earlier maturity 
    
within the normal physiological range.
But if temperature becomes excessive:
Extreme heat→Stress→Abnormal development / yield loss     
So faster maturity does not necessarily mean better productivity.

22. Heat-Induced Early Senescence
Excessive temperature can accelerate senescence.
Possible sequence:
Heat stress→oxidative stress→chlorophyll loss→photosynthesis↓→senescence 
     Premature senescence can shorten the period during which leaves provide assimilates to developing
grain, fruit or seed.

23. Maximum Temperature and Crop Yield
Yield is influenced by several processes:
Yield=f(growth+reproduction+filling+duration)  = f( +
 + + )  
High temperature can reduce yield by affecting several of these simultaneously:
Heat→ Photosynthesis↓Respiration↑Water demand↑Pollen viability↓Fruit/seed set↓Grain filling↓ d      \\  
 \\   \\   \\  
 \\    

24. Maximum Temperature and Crop Quality
Heat can affect:

- grain weight
- grain composition
- protein accumulation
- oil accumulation
- fruit firmness
- sugar concentration
- color
- aroma
- fiber properties
Therefore:
Heat stress can affect both quantity and quality   

25. Heat Shock
A sudden exposure to unusually high temperature can trigger a heat-shock response.
Plants produce heat-shock proteins (HSPs) and activate protective mechanisms.
Simplified:
Sudden heat→Stress signaling→Heat-shock response→Protein protection 
    
Heat-shock proteins can help maintain proper protein folding and reduce cellular damage.

26. Heat Stress and Reactive Oxygen Species
High temperature can disturb cellular metabolism and increase reactive oxygen species (ROS).
Examples include:
- superoxide
- hydrogen peroxide
- hydroxyl radicals
Excess ROS can damage:

- membranes
- proteins
- lipids
- DNA
- chloroplasts
- mitochondria
Plants therefore use antioxidant systems such as:
- superoxide dismutase
- catalase
- peroxidases
- non-enzymatic antioxidants

27. Membrane Damage
Cell membranes are sensitive to temperature stress.
Excessive heat can:
Heat→Membrane fluidity/disruption→Cellular dysfunction     
This can interfere with:
- ion transport
- photosynthesis
- respiration
- enzyme localization
- cell integrity

28. Maximum Temperature and Enzymes
Enzymes have temperature-dependent activity.
Initially:
Temperature↑→Reaction rate↑    But beyond the optimum:
Temperature↑→Enzyme dysfunction↑    At extreme temperatures, proteins can lose their functional structure.

29. Maximum Temperature and Photoinhibition
Under excessive heat and strong radiation, the photosynthetic apparatus can become damaged or
downregulated.
This can result in:
- reduced photosynthetic efficiency
- damage to photosystem components
- increased oxidative stress
Heat and high light often interact, making the stress stronger than either factor alone.

30. Maximum Temperature and Pest
Development
Temperature affects insects and other pests.
Within their suitable range:
Temperature↑→Development rate↑    up to a species-specific optimum.
Beyond their upper thermal limits:
Extreme heat→Survival/reproduction↓    Thus, high temperature does not automatically mean pest populations increase. The response depends
- n the specific pest and environmental conditions.

31. Maximum Temperature and Plant Diseases
Temperature influences pathogen development, but disease depends on several factors.
Disease=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment)  = f( + +
 )  
Temperature interacts with:
- humidity
- leaf wetness
- rainfall
- host susceptibility
- pathogen species
Therefore a higher temperature can either increase or decrease disease risk depending on the particular
pathosystem.

32. Maximum Temperature and
Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration is:
ET=Evaporation+Transpiration +   
Higher temperature generally increases atmospheric evaporative demand.
Therefore:
High temperature→ET demand↑→crop water requirement may increase   ET    
Actual evapotranspiration also depends on:
- solar radiation
- humidity
- wind
- soil moisture
- crop characteristics.

33. Maximum Temperature and Irrigation
During hot periods, irrigation may help maintain plant water status where irrigation is available and
appropriately managed.
Water supports:
Root uptake→Xylem transport→Transpiration→Cooling     But irrigation cannot eliminate all heat damage, particularly when temperatures are extremely high or
reproductive tissues are directly heat-sensitive.

34. Maximum Temperature and Canopy
Temperature
Air temperature and plant temperature are not necessarily identical.
A transpiring plant can sometimes maintain a leaf temperature below air temperature.
Under drought:
Stomatal closure→Transpiration↓→Evaporative cooling↓→Leaf temperature↑ 
      Therefore canopy temperature can be useful as an indicator of plant water/heat stress.

35. Maximum Temperature and Microclimate
The temperature experienced by a crop depends on its microenvironment.
Factors include:
- canopy density
- plant height
- soil moisture
- shading
- wind
- radiation
- humidity

- crop architecture
Therefore:
Measured air temperature≠necessarily leaf temperature 
  

36. Maximum Temperature and Day/Night
Temperature
Maximum temperature generally represents the daytime thermal extreme, while minimum temperature
- ften occurs near the night/early morning.
Both are important.

High daytime temperature
Can increase:
- heat stress
- transpiration
- evaporative demand
- photorespiration

High nighttime temperature
Can increase:
- respiration
- carbon loss
- developmental rate

37. Diurnal Temperature Range
The difference between daily maximum and minimum temperature is called the diurnal temperature
range.
DTR=Tmax⁡,daily−Tmin⁡,daily  -T   ,    
Example:
Tmax⁡=38∘CT    =38  C Tmin⁡=22∘CT    =22  C

Therefore:
DTR=38−22=16∘CDTR=38-22=16  C

38. Maximum Temperature and Growing
Degree Days
Growing degree day calculations primarily estimate development using temperatures above a base
threshold.
A simple form is:
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)GDD= (0,T    -T b)
However, this simple equation does not fully represent heat stress.
A crop can accumulate thermal time while simultaneously suffering damage from excessive
temperature.
Therefore:
Thermal time≠complete measure of heat stress   
More advanced models may include upper temperature thresholds.

39. Upper Temperature Threshold in Crop
Models
A more realistic temperature-development model may use:
r(T)= 0,T≤Tbincreasing,Tb<T<Toptdecreasing,Topt<T<Tmax⁡0 or severe inhibition,T≥Tmax⁡r(T)=
  0,&T T b\\  ,&T b<T<T    \\
 ,&T    <T<T    \\ 0 ,&T T    
 
where r(T)r(T) represents developmental rate.
This reflects the fact that development is not simply proportional to temperature indefinitely.

40. Duration Above the Critical Temperature
Heat stress depends on both temperature intensity and exposure duration.

For example:
40∘C for 15 minutes40  C  
may have a different effect from:
40∘C for 10 hours40  C  
Therefore:
Heat stress=f(temperature magnitude+duration+plant stage)  =
f( + + )  

41. Sensitive Growth Stages
Not every crop stage has equal heat sensitivity.
Commonly important stages include:
- flowering
- anthesis
- pollination
- fertilization
- early grain/seed formation
- grain filling
Thus the same temperature can have different consequences at different stages.

42. Crop Heat Tolerance
Crops differ in their ability to withstand high temperatures.
Heat tolerance may involve:
- efficient transpiration
- deeper roots
- membrane stability
- antioxidant capacity
- heat-shock proteins
- pollen tolerance

- efficient recovery after stress
- altered flowering time
Genotype therefore matters:
Heat response=f(genotype+environment+stage)  = f( +
 + )  

43. Heat Avoidance
Some plants avoid severe heat rather than simply tolerating it.
Mechanisms include:
- earlier flowering
- altered leaf angle
- leaf rolling
- transpiration cooling
- canopy architecture
- changing developmental timing
This is called heat avoidance.

44. Heat Tolerance
Heat tolerance means maintaining physiological function under high temperature.
Mechanisms can include:
- heat-shock proteins
- antioxidant defense
- membrane stability
- protective osmolytes
- stable photosynthetic machinery
- reproductive tolerance
Therefore:
Heat avoidance≠heat tolerance   

45. Management of High Temperature
Agricultural strategies can reduce heat risk.

Adjust sowing date
Schedule sensitive stages away from expected extreme heat.

Select heat-tolerant varieties
Use cultivars suited to local temperature conditions.

Irrigation
Maintain adequate soil water where irrigation is available.

Mulching
Can reduce soil heating and conserve moisture.

Shade
Can reduce radiation and canopy temperature in suitable systems.

Protected cultivation
Provides greater environmental control.

Maintain soil health
Good soil structure and organic matter can support water availability and root function.

Windbreaks
Can modify microclimate in appropriate situations.

46. Heat Stress and Crop Water Use
High temperature can create a feedback loop:
Temperature↑→Water demand↑→Soil water↓→Stomatal closure→Cooling↓→Leaf temperature↑ d              
This is why heat waves combined with drought can be particularly damaging.

47. Maximum Temperature and Climate Change
Agricultural effects of warming are not determined only by changes in average temperature.
Important factors include:
- frequency of heat waves
- duration of heat waves
- nighttime temperature
- extreme maximum temperatures
- timing of heat events
- interaction with drought
Therefore:
Climate risk≠average temperature alone    

48. Maximum Temperature and Crop Calendar
If high-temperature events become more frequent or occur earlier/later, they can alter:
- sowing windows
- flowering periods
- irrigation requirements
- maturity dates
- harvest timing
Farmers may therefore adapt through:
- changed sowing dates
- different varieties
- altered crop choices
- irrigation management

- protected cultivation

49. Maximum Temperature and Post-Harvest
Products
Temperature remains important even after harvest.
High temperatures can accelerate:
- respiration
- water loss
- microbial growth
- quality deterioration
- ripening
- senescence
For harvested horticultural products:
High temperature→Respiration↑→Storage life↓       
This is one reason cooling and appropriate storage conditions are important.

50. Maximum Temperature vs Heat Wave
Maximum temperature
A temperature measurement or upper thermal threshold.

Heat wave
A period of unusually high temperatures, generally defined relative to local climatology and using a
specified duration and threshold.
Thus:
One hot day≠necessarily a heat wave   
A heat wave involves persistence and unusually high temperatures according to the relevant definition.

51. Important Terms
Term
Meaning
Maximum temperature Upper temperature limit for a biological process
Daily maximum
Highest air temperature observed during a day
Optimum temperature Temperature/range near best process performance
Heat stress
Physiological stress caused by excessive temperature
Heat shock
Response to sudden high-temperature exposure
Chilling
Stress from low, non-freezing temperatures
Heat tolerance
Ability to maintain function under high temperature
Heat avoidance
Reducing exposure to damaging heat
Canopy temperature
Temperature of crop canopy
Diurnal range
Daily maximum minus daily minimum
Thermal time
Accumulated temperature used for development
Heat wave
Period of unusually high temperatures

55. Final Takeaway
Maximum temperature is the upper thermal limit beyond which a particular crop process becomes incre
asingly inhibited and, under sufficiently extreme or prolonged heat, plant tissues can suffer injury.
   

The most important agricultural principle is:
High temperature→Heat stress→ Photosynthesis↓Respiration↑Water demand↑Pollen viability↓Fruit/
seed set↓Grain filling↓Senescence↑→Potential yield and quality loss 
   \\
  \\   \\   \\
  \\   \\     
Remember: the maximum temperature is not one universal temperature for all crops. It depends on
the crop, variety, physiological process, developmental stage, exposure duration, and
environmental conditions.

Optimum Temperature
## 1. Introduction
Optimum temperature is the temperature or temperature range at which a particular physiological,
biochemical, or developmental process of a plant occurs at or near its maximum efficiency or rate,
without significant temperature stress.
It is one of the three important cardinal temperatures used to describe plant temperature responses:
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T     
where:
- Tmin⁡T     = minimum/base temperature
- ToptT     = optimum temperature
- Tmax⁡T     = maximum/upper temperature threshold
The most important point is that there is no single optimum temperature for every crop or every
stage.

## 2. Definition
Optimum temperature
The optimum temperature is the temperature or temperature range under which a particular plant
process operates at its highest or near-highest rate and efficiency.
For example, a crop may have an optimum temperature range for:
- germination

- root growth
- vegetative growth
- photosynthesis
- flowering
- pollination
- grain filling
- fruit development
These optimum temperatures may be different.

3. Cardinal Temperatures
Plant processes generally respond to temperature according to three major thresholds:
Tmin⁡→Topt→Tmax⁡  T     T      

Below Tmin⁡T    
Development is very slow or negligible.

Near ToptT    
Development and physiological activity are most favorable.

Above ToptT    
The process begins to decline.

Near or above Tmax⁡T    
Severe heat stress and injury can occur.
A simplified response curve is:
Process
rate

│

│
/

│

│ /
\ 
│
└────────────────────────────────────── Temperature
Tmin
Topt
Tmax

4. Optimum Temperature Is a Range, Not
Always One Number
In real agriculture, it is often more useful to think about an optimum temperature range rather than
- ne exact temperature.
For example:
Topt=20−25∘CT    =20-25  C
could represent a favorable range for a particular process.
This is because plant responses are influenced by:
- genotype
- developmental stage
- duration of exposure
- soil moisture
- humidity
- radiation
- nutrient availability
- photoperiod
- acclimation
Therefore:
Topt=f(crop+process+stage+environment)  =f( + +
 + )  

5. Optimum Temperature vs Optimum Crop
Temperature
This distinction is important.

Optimum temperature for a process
Temperature at which a specific process performs best.

Optimum temperature for a crop
A broader range of temperatures that allows the crop to perform well throughout a particular
developmental period.
For example, the optimum temperature for:
- seed germination
- vegetative growth
- flowering
- grain filling
may all be different.
Thus:
Topt,germination≠Topt,flowering≠Topt,grain filling    T      T      

6. Why Optimum Temperature Is Important
Optimum temperature influences:
- germination
- emergence
- enzyme activity
- respiration
- photosynthesis
- root growth
- shoot growth
- leaf expansion
- tillering
- branching
- flowering
- pollination
- fertilization
- fruit set
- grain filling

- maturity
- biomass accumulation
- yield
- crop quality
Therefore:
Optimum temperature→efficient physiological activity→better growth and development     

7. Optimum Temperature and Seed
Germination
Seeds require appropriate temperature for successful germination.
At temperatures below optimum:
Germination rate↓  As temperature approaches the optimum:
Germination rate↑  Beyond the optimum:
Germination rate↓  Therefore:
Temperature→enzyme activity→reserve mobilization→radicle emergence 
    

8. Optimum Temperature and Enzyme Activity
Plant metabolism depends heavily on enzymes.
As temperature rises within the favorable range:
Molecular movement↑→enzyme reaction rate↑    But beyond the optimum:

Excess heat→enzyme dysfunction→reaction rate↓     Thus:
Optimum temperature≈favorable temperature for coordinated biochemical reactions    

9. Optimum Temperature and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis converts light energy into chemical energy:
6CO2+6H2O+light→C6H12O6+6O26CO 2+6H 2O+   C 6H  12 O 6+6O 2
Temperature affects:
- enzyme activity
- CO₂ fixation
- electron transport
- membrane stability
- stomatal behavior
- photorespiration
At an appropriate temperature:
Photosynthetic rate→high   
Too low:
Photosynthesis↓  Too high:
Photosynthesis↓  10. Optimum Temperature and Respiration
Respiration provides usable energy:
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+EnergyC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
 
Temperature influences respiration strongly.
Within a suitable range:

Respiratory activity↑  But excessively high temperature can cause metabolic imbalance and cellular damage.
The agricultural objective is therefore not simply to maximize respiration.
Plants need a balance between:
PhotosynthesisandRespiration     

11. Optimum Temperature and Net Biomass
A simplified concept is:
Net biomass gain≈Photosynthetic carbon gain−Respiratory and other carbon losses   -    
At favorable temperatures, photosynthesis, respiration, growth and carbon allocation operate in a
relatively balanced manner.
This supports:
- leaf development
- stem growth
- root growth
- reproductive development
- yield formation.

12. Optimum Temperature and Root Growth
Root growth requires suitable temperatures for:
- cell division
- cell expansion
- respiration
- nutrient uptake
- membrane function
At unfavorable low temperature:
Root growth↓  At excessive temperature:

Root stress↑  Therefore:
Topt→efficient root growth and resource uptake     

13. Optimum Temperature and Vegetative
Growth
During vegetative growth, suitable temperature supports:
- leaf expansion
- stem elongation
- branching
- tillering
- root growth
- canopy development
The relationship can be represented as:
Temperature→Cell division + Cell expansion→Vegetative growth 
   

14. Optimum Temperature and Leaf
Development
Leaves are major photosynthetic organs.
Suitable temperature promotes:
- leaf initiation
- leaf expansion
- chlorophyll formation
- stomatal function
- photosynthetic activity
This contributes to canopy development and light interception.

15. Optimum Temperature and Canopy
Development
A healthy crop canopy captures solar radiation.
One important measurement is the Leaf Area Index (LAI):
LAI=Total one-sided leaf areaGround area  
     
Suitable temperature helps maintain appropriate leaf development.
However, maximum leaf area is not automatically desirable.
Too much canopy can result in:
- self-shading
- poor air movement
- higher humidity
- disease risk
- excessive respiratory losses.
The goal is an efficient canopy, not simply the largest canopy.

16. Optimum Temperature and Flower
Initiation
Temperature can influence the transition from vegetative to reproductive development.
Vegetative meristem→Flower initiation→Flower development    Some crops also have specific temperature requirements associated with vernalization.
Therefore:
Temperature+photoperiod+genotype→flowering response  +
  +    

17. Optimum Temperature and Flowering
Suitable temperature during flowering is important for:

- flower development
- anthesis
- pollen viability
- stigma receptivity
- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth
- fertilization
- fruit/seed set
If temperature moves too far above or below the favorable range:
Reproductive success↓   

18. Optimum Temperature and Pollination
Successful pollination requires functional pollen and receptive stigmas.
The sequence is:
Pollen release→Pollen transfer→Pollen hydration→Pollen germination→Pollen tube growth      Temperature influences every stage.
Therefore:
Topt→favorable pollen-pistil interaction     

19. Optimum Temperature and Fertilization
After pollination, the pollen tube must reach the ovule.
Suitable temperature supports:
- pollen tube growth
- gamete function
- fertilization
- early embryo development

Thus:
Suitable temperature→Pollination success→Fertilization→Seed/fruit set 
   20. Optimum Temperature and Grain Filling
Grain filling involves accumulation of:
- starch
- protein
- oils
- other storage compounds
Suitable temperature supports efficient:
Source→Phloem→Developing grain     
where source tissues include leaves and sometimes stems.

21. Optimum Temperature and Fruit
Development
In fruit crops, appropriate temperature supports:
- cell division
- cell expansion
- carbohydrate accumulation
- pigment formation
- flavor development
- appropriate maturation
Temperature interacts with:
- light
- water
- nutrition

- genotype.

22. Optimum Temperature and Maturity
Temperature influences developmental rate.
Under suitable temperatures:
Development→proceeds normally   If temperature is too low:
Development rate↓  If temperature becomes excessively high:
Development may accelerate initially but stress increases 
Thus optimum temperature helps maintain an appropriate balance between developmental speed and
physiological function.

23. Optimum Temperature and Crop Duration
Crop duration depends partly on accumulated thermal conditions.
A simplified thermal-time equation is:
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)  -T b)  
where:
- TmeanT     = mean temperature
- TbT b = base temperature.
But thermal time alone does not describe all crop responses.
Very high temperatures can cause stress even while thermal time is accumulating.
Therefore:
Thermal accumulation≠guaranteed optimum growth 
  

24. Optimum Temperature and Water Use
Temperature influences evapotranspiration.
Generally:
Temperature↑→Atmospheric water demand↑    Within an appropriate range, plants can maintain:
- transpiration
- cooling
- nutrient transport
- photosynthesis.
But high temperature combined with water shortage can produce severe stress.

25. Optimum Temperature and Transpiration
Transpiration provides several functions:
- evaporative cooling
- water movement
- nutrient transport
- maintenance of plant water relations.
The pathway is:
Soil water→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere      A suitable temperature allows effective regulation of this system.

26. Optimum Temperature and Nutrient Uptake
Temperature affects:
- root activity
- membrane transport
- nutrient diffusion

- microbial activity
- enzyme activity.
Therefore unsuitable temperatures can reduce nutrient uptake even when nutrients are present in the
soil.

27. Optimum Temperature and Soil
Microorganisms
Soil microorganisms are also temperature sensitive.
Suitable temperatures support processes such as:
- organic matter decomposition
- nutrient mineralization
- nitrogen transformations
- microbial symbioses
For example, biological nitrogen fixation by rhizobia depends on suitable environmental conditions.
Thus:
Soil temperature→Microbial activity→Nutrient availability     

28. Optimum Temperature and Crop Yield
Yield results from the interaction of many processes:
Growth+Reproduction+Filling+Maturity→Yield  +  +
  +    
Maintaining temperatures near appropriate ranges during sensitive stages can support these processes.
For grain crops, a simplified relationship is:
Grain yield≈Plants/area×Grains/plant×Average grain weight      
Temperature can influence each component.

29. Optimum Temperature Is Stage-Specific
A crop does not necessarily have one optimum temperature throughout its entire life.
For example:
Crop stage
Temperature concern
Germination
Suitable temperature for rapid, uniform germination
Seedling
Suitable root and shoot growth
Vegetative growth Canopy and biomass development
Flower initiation Developmental signaling
Flowering
Reproductive development
Pollination
Pollen and stigma function
Fertilization
Pollen tube and gamete function
Grain filling
Storage-product accumulation
Maturity
Appropriate developmental progression
Therefore:
Topt changes with developmental stage and process      

30. Cool-Season and Warm-Season Crops
Agricultural crops are broadly grouped according to their temperature preferences.

Cool-season crops
Examples include:
- wheat
- barley
- oats
- peas
- chickpea
- lentil
- many temperate vegetables
They generally perform better under relatively cooler conditions.

Warm-season crops
Examples include:

- rice
- maize
- sorghum
- pearl millet
- cotton
- groundnut
- soybean
They generally require warmer conditions.
This does not mean that each group has one universal optimum temperature.

31. Optimum Temperature and C3/C4 Plants
Temperature response also differs between photosynthetic pathways.

C3 crops
Examples:
- wheat
- rice
- soybean
- potato
C3 photosynthesis is generally more affected by high-temperature-induced photorespiration.

C4 crops
Examples:
- maize
- sugarcane
- sorghum
- pearl millet
C4 plants often have strong adaptation to warm, high-light conditions.
But C4 does not mean unlimited heat tolerance.
Extreme temperatures can damage both C3 and C4 crops.

32. Optimum Temperature and Photoperiod
Temperature and day length often interact.
Flowering can depend on:
Temperature+Photoperiod+Genotype + + 
 
This interaction explains why the same crop variety can flower at different times under different
climates.

33. Optimum Temperature and Vernalization
Some crops require exposure to a period of relatively low temperature to acquire flowering
competence.
This process is called vernalization.
Therefore, temperature can act not only as a growth factor but also as a developmental signal.
Appropriate cold exposure→Vernalization→Flowering competence     

34. Optimum Temperature vs Heat Stress
These should not be confused.

Optimum temperature
Supports efficient physiological function.

Heat stress
Occurs when temperature exceeds the crop's tolerance range sufficiently to impair normal function.
Topt→efficient function    
T≫Topt→heat stress

37. Factors That Change the Optimum
Temperature
The optimum is influenced by:

Genotype
Different cultivars can have different thermal responses.

Developmental stage
Seed, seedling, flowering and grain filling can differ.

Acclimation
Plants can adjust their physiology after exposure to different temperatures.

Water availability
A well-watered plant can cool itself more effectively through transpiration.

Humidity
Temperature and humidity together affect atmospheric water demand.

Radiation
High solar radiation can increase leaf temperature.

Wind
Wind changes boundary-layer conditions and heat exchange.

Nutrition
Nutritional status can influence temperature tolerance.

38. Air Temperature vs Plant Temperature
The temperature recorded by a weather station is generally air temperature.
But plant tissues experience their own temperature.
A transpiring leaf may be cooler than surrounding air.
Conversely, under drought and high radiation:
Stomatal closure→Transpiration↓→Leaf cooling↓      and leaf temperature may rise.
Thus:
Tair≠Tleaf≠Tsoil   T     T      

39. Optimum Temperature and Microclimate
Crop temperature depends on the field microclimate.
Important factors include:
- canopy density
- soil moisture

- shade
- wind
- humidity
- radiation
- row spacing
- plant height
Therefore two fields experiencing the same reported air temperature may expose plants to somewhat
different thermal conditions.

40. Optimum Temperature and Agriculture
Management
Farmers can try to maintain crop temperatures within favorable ranges by:
- selecting appropriate sowing dates
- choosing suitable cultivars
- irrigation
- mulching
- shading where appropriate
- protected cultivation
- proper plant density
- maintaining soil moisture
- windbreaks
- selecting suitable planting locations

41. Example: Temperature and Crop Growth
Suppose a hypothetical crop has:
Tmin⁡=8∘CT    =8  C Topt=22∘CT    =22  C Tmax⁡=35∘CT    =35  C
Then conceptually:

5∘C5  C
Development≈negligible  15∘C15  C
Development occurs but is slower 

22∘C22  C
Near optimum 

30∘C30  C
Development may still occur but begins declining 

40∘C40  C
Severe heat stress likely 
This is a conceptual example, not a universal crop threshold.

42. Optimum Temperature and Growing Degree
Days
The concept of optimum temperature is different from the base temperature used in GDD.

Base temperature
Used to determine when development begins contributing thermal time.
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)GDD= (0,T    -T b)

Optimum temperature
Temperature around which developmental or physiological performance is most favorable.

Maximum temperature
Upper threshold beyond which development is severely restricted or injury can occur.
Therefore:
Tb≠Topt≠Tmax⁡   T      

43. Temperature and Developmental Rate
A simplified model can be represented as:
r(T)= 0,T≤Tmin⁡↑,Tmin⁡<T<Topt↓,Topt<T<Tmax⁡severe inhibition,T≥Tmax⁡r(T)=   0, &
T T    \\  , & T    <T<T    \\  , & T    
<T<T    \\  , & T T      
where r(T)r(T) is the developmental rate.
Real crop responses are often nonlinear and process-specific.

44. Optimum Temperature and Heat Waves
A crop can experience stress even if its average temperature appears acceptable.
For example, several days of extreme heat during flowering can cause reproductive damage even if the
seasonal average is near the crop's preferred range.
Thus:
Temperature average alone≠complete measure of crop thermal stress   
The timing, intensity and duration of temperature extremes matter.

45. Optimum Temperature and Climate
Climate suitability depends on whether the crop receives appropriate temperatures throughout its
growth cycle.
A suitable agricultural climate generally requires appropriate:
- minimum temperatures
- maximum temperatures
- day/night temperatures
- duration of favorable temperatures
- seasonal pattern
- temperature during sensitive stages.
