52. Exam-Oriented Summary
Rainfall distribution means the way rainfall is distributed over time and space, including amount,
frequency, intensity, duration, onset, cessation, wet spells and dry spells.
Its importance comes from the fact that:
Crop water requirement changes with crop stage   
Therefore, the same annual rainfall can produce very different agricultural outcomes depending on
when and how it occurs.

Major components
1. Amount — total rainfall.
2. Timing — when rainfall occurs.
3. Frequency — how often it rains.
4. Intensity — how rapidly rain falls.
5. Duration — how long rainfall lasts.
6. Onset — beginning of rainy season.
7. Cessation — end of rainy season.
8. Wet spells — periods of frequent rainfall.
9. Dry spells — periods of little/no rainfall.

10.Spatial distribution — variation from one location to another.

53. Final Takeaway
The most important equation-like concept is:
Agricultural value of rainfall≠f(total rainfall alone)   f( )  
Instead:
Agricultural rainfall availability=f(amount,timing,frequency,intensity,duration,crop stage,soil,ET)
  = f(  ,  ,  ,
 ,  ,  ,  ,   )  
In simple words:
Rainfall distribution tells us not just how much rain a crop receives, but whether the
rain arrives at the right time, in the right amount and intensity, and in a pattern that
allows the soil and crop to use it effectively.
This is why a region with moderate but well-distributed rainfall can sometimes support crops
better than a region receiving a larger total amount of rainfall that arrives in a few extreme
events.

Humidity
## 1. Introduction
Humidity is the amount of water vapour present in the atmosphere. It is an important agricultural
weather factor because it affects:
- transpiration
- evapotranspiration
- water requirements
- stomatal behavior
- photosynthesis
- plant growth
- flowering and pollination
- disease development
- pest activity
- seed drying

- fruit quality
- post-harvest storage.
A simple agricultural relationship is:
Humidity→Atmospheric water demand→Transpiration→Crop water status→Growth and yield       
Humidity is closely related to temperature, rainfall, wind, solar radiation and evapotranspiration.

2. What Is Humidity?
Humidity refers to the presence of water vapour in the air.
Water exists in the atmosphere as:
- water vapour
- liquid droplets
- ice crystals.
Humidity specifically concerns the water vapour component.
Water vapour enters the atmosphere mainly through:
- evaporation from soil and water bodies
- transpiration from plants
- evapotranspiration from agricultural land.
Thus:
Evaporation+Transpiration→Atmospheric water vapour +
    

3. Humidity vs Relative Humidity
These terms are related but not identical.

Humidity
A general term describing atmospheric water vapour.

Relative humidity
The amount of water vapour in the air relative to the maximum amount the air could hold at that
temperature, expressed as a percentage.

RH=Actual water vapour contentSaturation water vapour content×100        100  
For example:
RH=70%RH=70\%
means the air has about 70% of the water-vapour amount associated with saturation at that temperature.

4. Why Temperature Matters
Relative humidity depends strongly on temperature.
Warm air can contain more water vapour before becoming saturated than cold air.
Therefore, if the actual amount of water vapour remains constant:

Temperature increases
RH↓  Temperature decreases
RH↑  This is why relative humidity commonly changes substantially between daytime and nighttime.

5. Types/Measures of Atmospheric Humidity
Several measures are used.

5.1 Absolute Humidity
Absolute humidity is the mass of water vapour present per unit volume of air.
AH=mvV  V   
where:
- mvm v = mass of water vapour
- VV = volume of air.
Typical unit:
g/m3g/m 3

6. Specific Humidity
Specific humidity is the mass of water vapour per unit mass of moist air.

q=mvmmoist air  m       
It is commonly expressed in:
g/kgg/kg
- r as a dimensionless ratio.

7. Mixing Ratio
The mixing ratio is the mass of water vapour relative to the mass of dry air.
r=mvmd  m d   
where:
- mvm v = mass of water vapour
- mdm d = mass of dry air.

8. Relative Humidity
Relative humidity is the most commonly used humidity measure in agricultural weather observations.
RH=eaes×100  e s   100  
where:
- eae a = actual vapour pressure
- ese s = saturation vapour pressure at the same temperature.
Thus:
RH=100%RH=100\%
means the air is saturated with respect to water at that temperature.

## 9. Saturation
Air is said to be saturated when it contains the maximum water vapour possible at a particular
temperature and pressure under the relevant conditions.
At saturation:
RH≈100% 
If air is cooled sufficiently, it may reach saturation.
This leads to the dew point.

10. Dew Point Temperature
The dew point temperature is the temperature to which air must be cooled, at approximately constant
pressure and water-vapour content, for saturation to occur.
At dew point:
RH≈100% 
If the surface or air cools below the dew point, condensation may occur.
This can produce:
- dew
- fog
- cloud droplets
- leaf wetness.

11. Humidity and Dew Formation
The sequence is:
Air cooling→RH increases→Saturation→Condensation→Dew       
Dew can increase the duration of leaf wetness, which is important for some plant diseases.

12. Relative Humidity During the Day
A typical daily pattern is:

Morning
Temperature relatively low:
RH↑RH Afternoon
Temperature relatively high:
RH↓RH Night
Temperature falls:
RH↑RH The exact pattern depends on weather conditions.

13. Humidity and Plant Transpiration
Humidity has a major influence on transpiration.
Plants lose water primarily through stomata.
When surrounding air is dry:
Water vapour gradient↑→Transpiration↑     
When surrounding air is humid:
Water vapour gradient↓→Transpiration↓     
assuming other conditions remain similar.

14. Humidity and Water Vapour Gradient
Water moves from regions of higher water-vapour concentration/potential toward lower atmospheric
water-vapour conditions.
Inside a leaf, air spaces are generally close to saturation.
If outside air is dry:
Leaf air→Atmosphere  has a strong vapour gradient.
Therefore:
Low humidity→high atmospheric drying demand    

15. High Humidity
High humidity generally means the air is relatively moist.
Potential effects include:

- lower atmospheric evaporative demand
- reduced transpiration under otherwise similar conditions
- slower drying
- increased leaf wetness
- favorable conditions for some diseases.
But high humidity does not automatically mean that the plant has sufficient soil water.
This is an important distinction.
High atmospheric humidity≠adequate root-zone water 
  

16. Low Humidity
Low humidity means the atmosphere is relatively dry.
Potential effects include:
- increased transpiration
- increased evapotranspiration
- faster soil-water depletion
- greater plant water stress
- stomatal closure
- reduced photosynthesis under severe stress.
The sequence can be:
RH↓→VPD↑→transpiration demand↑→water loss↑ 
      

17. Vapor Pressure Deficit — VPD
For agriculture, Vapor Pressure Deficit (VPD) is often more informative than relative humidity alone.
VPD represents the difference between the saturation vapour pressure and actual vapour pressure.
VPD=es−ea 
where:
- ese s = saturation vapour pressure
- eae a = actual vapour pressure.

Using relative humidity:
VPD=es(1−RH100)  100  )  

18. Why VPD Is Important
VPD represents the atmospheric drying demand on plants.

High VPD
Atmospheric drying demand↑   
Potentially:
- transpiration increases
- plant water loss increases
- stomata may close under water stress
- irrigation demand may increase.

Low VPD
Atmospheric drying demand↓   
Potentially:
- transpiration decreases
- drying slows
- leaf wetness may persist.

19. Relative Humidity vs VPD
This distinction is very important.
Relative Humidity
VPD
Expressed as %
Usually expressed in kPa
Relative measure of atmospheric moisture Atmospheric drying demand
Strongly temperature-dependent
Combines temperature and humidity
100% means saturation
0 kPa approximately corresponds to saturation
Useful for general weather description
Often useful for crop water-stress analysis
A key point is:
Same RH at different temperatures can mean different VPD   
Therefore, agricultural water-demand analysis often considers temperature + humidity together.

20. Humidity and Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration is:
ET=E+T 
where:
- EE = evaporation
- TT = transpiration.
Low humidity generally increases atmospheric demand for water and can increase ET when other
conditions are favorable.
High humidity generally reduces atmospheric drying demand.
Therefore:
RH↓→VPD↑→ET demand↑   

21. Humidity and Irrigation
Humidity affects irrigation requirements indirectly through evapotranspiration.
Under dry atmospheric conditions:
ET demand↑  which can increase crop water requirements.
Under humid conditions:
ET demand↓  assuming other environmental factors remain similar.
Thus irrigation scheduling should consider:
- rainfall
- soil moisture
- temperature
- humidity/VPD
- solar radiation
- wind
- crop stage.

22. Humidity and Soil Water
Consider two environments with the same soil moisture.

Environment A
Low humidity + high temperature.
ET↑ET Soil water may decline rapidly.

Environment B
High humidity + lower atmospheric demand.
ET↓ET Soil water may decline more slowly.
Therefore:
Soil-water depletion≠rainfall alone    

23. Humidity and Stomata
Stomata regulate:
- CO₂ entry
- water-vapour loss.
Under high atmospheric demand:
VPD↑VPD plants may regulate stomatal opening to limit excessive water loss.
This can create:
High VPD→stomatal regulation/closure→CO2 entry↓→photosynthesis↓ 
  CO 2     
The response varies among crops and environmental conditions.

24. Humidity and Photosynthesis
Humidity affects photosynthesis mainly through its effects on plant water status and stomatal
conductance.

A simplified chain is:
Low RH→VPD↑→water loss↑→stomatal closure→CO2 entry↓→photosynthesis↓ 
 VPD     CO 2    However, extremely high humidity can also affect plant function through altered transpiration, nutrient
transport and disease pressure.

25. Humidity and Root Water Uptake
Root water uptake depends on the water-potential gradient between soil, plant and atmosphere.
A simplified pathway is:
Soil→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere       
Atmospheric demand contributes strongly to the driving force for water movement.
High VPD can increase water demand, but if soil water cannot supply the demand, the plant can
experience water stress.

26. Humidity and Nutrient Transport
Transpiration contributes to xylem flow and therefore influences transport of some nutrients from roots
to shoots.
Under suitable conditions:
Transpiration↑→xylem flow↑→mass flow of some nutrients↑      But excessive atmospheric demand can cause plant water stress and impair physiological function.
Therefore, more transpiration is not always better.

27. Humidity and Plant Growth
Moderate humidity
Often supports balanced water relations when combined with suitable temperature and soil moisture.

Very low humidity
Can cause:

- rapid water loss
- leaf rolling
- stomatal closure
- wilting
- reduced growth.

Very high humidity
Can cause:
- excessive tissue moisture
- reduced drying
- increased disease risk
- weak/translucent growth under certain protected environments.

28. Humidity and Seed Germination
Humidity of the atmosphere is less directly important than moisture around the seed.
The key requirement is:
Seed water availability   
However, atmospheric humidity can influence:
- drying of the seedbed
- seed moisture
- storage conditions.
Therefore:
Atmospheric RH≠soil moisture   

29. Humidity and Seed Storage
Relative humidity is extremely important in seed storage.
High humidity can increase seed moisture content.
Higher seed moisture can accelerate:
- respiration
- aging

- fungal growth
- insect problems
- loss of viability.
Therefore seed storage generally requires appropriate control of:
Seed moisture+temperature+RH + +   

30. Humidity and Flowering
Humidity can affect reproductive processes indirectly through:
- plant water status
- pollen behavior
- stigma condition
- disease pressure
- pollinator activity.
Very high humidity can promote certain diseases during flowering.
Very low humidity can increase plant water stress.
The actual effect is crop-specific.

31. Humidity and Pollination
Humidity can affect:
- pollen release
- pollen hydration
- pollen viability
- stigma receptivity
- pollinator activity.
For example, in some crops, excessively wet conditions can interfere with pollen dispersal.
Therefore:
Humidity + temperature + rainfall + wind→pollination environment    

32. Humidity and Disease
This is one of the most important agricultural effects of humidity.
Many fungal and bacterial pathogens require favorable combinations of:
- humidity
- temperature
- leaf wetness
- host susceptibility.
High humidity can prolong leaf wetness and create favorable microclimates.
A simplified relationship:
High humidity+leaf wetness+suitable temperature→higher risk for some diseases  +   +     
This does not mean every disease increases with high humidity.

33. Leaf Wetness
Leaf wetness means that water is present on the leaf surface.
It can come from:
- rainfall
- dew
- fog
- irrigation
- condensation.
Leaf wetness duration is often more directly related to disease development than RH alone.
Therefore:
RH≠leaf wetness   
but high RH can favor longer periods of leaf wetness.

34. Humidity and Fungal Diseases
Many fungal pathogens are favored by combinations of:

High humidity+leaf wetness+appropriate temperature + +   
Potential consequences include increased risk of certain:
- leaf spots
- blights
- mildews
- rots.
Disease response is always pathogen- and crop-specific.

35. Humidity and Bacterial Diseases
High humidity and leaf wetness can also support infection and spread of some bacterial diseases.
Rain splash may additionally move bacterial cells between plant tissues.
Thus:
Rainfall+humidity+leaf wetness + + 
can form an important disease environment.

36. Humidity and Insect Pests
Humidity can influence:
- insect survival
- reproduction
- development
- fungal pathogens of insects
- population dynamics.
However, the response varies substantially among insect species.
Therefore there is no universal rule that high humidity always increases or decreases insect
populations.

37. Humidity and Weeds
Humidity can influence:

- seed moisture
- weed growth
- transpiration
- disease development in weeds.
But soil moisture and temperature are generally more direct factors controlling weed germination.

38. Humidity and Fruit Crops
Humidity influences:
- transpiration
- fruit water relations
- disease development
- fruit cracking in some situations
- post-harvest quality.
High humidity can reduce water loss but may increase fungal/rot risks.
Low humidity can increase:
- water loss
- shriveling
- wilting.

39. Humidity and Vegetables
Fresh vegetables contain substantial water and continue to lose water after harvest.
Low humidity can cause:
- wilting
- shriveling
- weight loss.
Very high humidity can favor:
- condensation
- fungal/bacterial decay.

Therefore post-harvest management seeks a suitable balance between moisture retention and disease
prevention.

40. Humidity and Post-Harvest Storage
Humidity is critical after harvest.

Low humidity
Water loss↑  Potentially causing:
- shriveling
- wilting
- weight loss.

High humidity
Condensation/decay risk↑  Potentially causing:
- fungal growth
- bacterial decay
- reduced shelf life.
Therefore:
Correct RH is crop and commodity specific   

41. Humidity in Protected Cultivation
Humidity management is particularly important in:
- greenhouses
- polyhouses
- net houses
- growth chambers.
Inside protected structures:
Transpiration+Evaporation→humidity buildup  +    Poor ventilation can therefore cause high humidity.

42. Managing Excess Humidity in Greenhouses
Methods include:
- ventilation
- appropriate plant spacing
- controlled irrigation
- avoiding unnecessary leaf wetting
- drainage
- heating/dehumidification where appropriate
- maintaining air movement.
The objective is:
Reduce excessive humidity and leaf wetness   
without creating excessive water stress.

43. Managing Low Humidity
Under very dry atmospheric conditions, management may include:
- appropriate irrigation
- mulching
- wind reduction
- shade where appropriate
- protected cultivation
- maintaining adequate soil moisture.
The goal is not simply to increase atmospheric humidity but to maintain an appropriate plant water
balance.

44. Humidity and Wind
Wind strongly interacts with humidity.

Dry air + strong wind can greatly increase atmospheric drying demand.
Conceptually:
Low RH+high wind→rapid water loss +    
Wind also removes the humid boundary layer around leaves.
This can increase transpiration.

45. Humidity and Temperature Together
Humidity should rarely be interpreted without temperature.
For example:
RH=50%RH=50\%
does not tell us the same atmospheric condition at:
20∘C20  C
and:
40∘C40  C
because saturation vapour pressure changes with temperature.
Therefore:
Temperature + RH→VPD    
provides a more useful measure of atmospheric drying demand.

46. Humidity and Rainfall
Rainfall directly adds moisture to the atmosphere and soil.
After rainfall:
Atmospheric moisture↑  and often:
RH↑RH especially if temperature remains relatively low.
But rainfall and humidity are not the same thing.

Rainfall=water arriving at the surface =   
Humidity=water vapour in the atmosphere =   

47. Humidity and Cloud Formation
When moist air cools toward saturation:
RH→100%RH 100\%
condensation can occur if appropriate conditions are met.
This contributes to formation of:
- clouds
- fog
- dew.
Thus humidity is a fundamental component of atmospheric precipitation processes.

48. Humidity and Microclimate
The humidity experienced by a crop can differ from the humidity measured at a standard weather
station.
Within a crop canopy:
- leaves reduce air movement
- transpiration adds water vapour
- shading changes temperature
- dense vegetation can maintain high humidity.
Therefore:
Canopy microclimate≠open-air weather conditions   

49. Humidity Within a Crop Canopy
Dense canopies can create:
Wind↓  Humidity↑  Leaf wetness duration↑  This can increase the risk of certain diseases.

Therefore plant density and canopy architecture influence humidity.

50. Humidity and Crop Canopy
A dense canopy can:
- trap moisture
- reduce air movement
- increase humidity
- prolong leaf wetness.
Appropriate spacing and canopy management can improve air circulation.
Thus:
Canopy architecture→microclimate→humidity→disease environment      

51. Humidity and Crop Water Requirement
Crop water requirement is affected by atmospheric demand.
A simplified relationship is:
ETc=Kc×ET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration
- KcK c = crop coefficient
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
Humidity is one of the meteorological variables contributing to ET0ET 0, along with:
- temperature
- radiation
- wind.

52. Humidity and Drought Stress
Low humidity can intensify drought stress.

Consider:
Low soil moisture+high atmospheric demand  +  
This can produce:
Rapid plant water stress   
Therefore drought is not simply a rainfall problem.
It is influenced by:
Water supply−Atmospheric water demand -   

53. Humidity and Heat Stress
High temperature combined with low humidity can create very high atmospheric demand.
For example:
High temperature+Low RH→VPD↑  +    VPD This can increase:
- transpiration demand
- canopy water loss
- stomatal closure
- heat stress.
Therefore heat stress should be considered together with humidity.

54. Humidity and Nighttime Conditions
At night:
- temperature often decreases
- RH often increases
- transpiration may decrease.
If temperature reaches the dew point:
Condensation→dew  Long nighttime leaf wetness can increase the risk of certain diseases.

55. Measurement of Humidity
Humidity can be measured using instruments such as:

Hygrometer
Measures atmospheric humidity.

Psychrometer
Uses dry-bulb and wet-bulb temperatures to estimate humidity.

Electronic humidity sensors
Modern weather stations commonly use electronic sensors.
Agricultural weather stations often measure:
- air temperature
- relative humidity
- rainfall
- wind
- solar radiation.

56. Psychrometer Principle
A psychrometer generally contains:
- dry-bulb thermometer
- wet-bulb thermometer.
The difference between the two temperatures helps estimate atmospheric humidity.
When air is dry:
Evaporation from wet bulb↑  so:
Tdry−Twet↑T    -T     When air is humid:
Evaporation↓  and the temperature difference becomes smaller.
At saturation:

Tdry≈TwetT     T    

57. Important Humidity Relationships
Relative humidity
RH=eaes×100  e s  100  

VPD
VPD=es−ea 

VPD using RH
VPD=es(1−RH100)  100  )  

Evapotranspiration
ET=E+T 

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

58. Humidity Through the Crop Life Cycle
Crop stage

Importance of humidity
Seed
Influences drying/storage conditions
Germination
Atmospheric RH less important than soil moisture
Seedling
Influences transpiration and disease
Vegetative growth
Strong influence on transpiration and microclimate
Flower initiation
Indirect effects through water status
Flowering
Can influence pollen/stigma environment and disease
Pollination
Can influence pollen/pollinator conditions
Fruit/grain formation Influences water relations and disease
Maturity
Influences drying and disease
Harvest
Affects drying conditions
Post-harvest
Major factor in weight loss and storage decay

59. Low vs Moderate vs High Humidity
Condition
Typical agricultural implications
Very low humidity High atmospheric drying demand, rapid water loss
Moderate humidity Often favorable when temperature and soil moisture are suitable
High humidity
Lower drying demand but potentially greater leaf wetness/disease risk

Condition
Typical agricultural implications
Near saturation
Very low VPD, condensation/dew possible
Persistent high RH Poor drying and greater risk of some diseases
Remember that the actual effect depends on temperature, crop, soil moisture, wind and disease
conditions.

60. Humidity vs Rainfall
Humidity
Rainfall
Water vapour in atmosphere
Water falling to surface
Atmospheric variable
Precipitation event
Measured as RH, specific humidity, etc. Usually measured in mm
Influences transpiration
Supplies water to soil/surface
Strongly related to VPD
Influences soil moisture
Can exist without rain
Often increases atmospheric moisture

61. Humidity vs Soil Moisture
This is another important distinction.

Humidity
Water vapour in the atmosphere.

Soil moisture
Water present in the soil.
A field can have:
High RH + dry soil 
- r:
Low RH + wet soil 
Therefore:
Atmospheric humidity≠soil-water availability   

62. Humidity and Agricultural Decision-Making
Humidity information can help with:
- irrigation scheduling

- disease forecasting
- greenhouse management
- ventilation
- spraying decisions
- seed storage
- post-harvest storage
- drought assessment
- evapotranspiration estimation.
It should normally be interpreted together with:
Temperature + rainfall + wind + radiation + soil moisture   

63. Complete Agricultural Relationship
The major pathway is:
Temperature+Humidity→VPD→Atmospheric drying demand→Transpiration→Plant water status→St
- matal behavior→Photosynthesis→Growth and yield  +  
        
A second pathway is:
High RH+Leaf wetness+Suitable temperature→Disease-favorable microclimate  +   +     

64. Key Points to Remember
1. Humidity = water vapour in the atmosphere.
2. Relative humidity (RH) expresses atmospheric moisture relative to saturation.
3. RH is strongly influenced by temperature.
4. VPD is often more useful than RH for understanding atmospheric drying demand.
5. Low RH generally means greater atmospheric drying demand.
6. High RH generally reduces evaporative demand.
7. High humidity can prolong leaf wetness.

8. High humidity + suitable temperature can favor some diseases.
9. Low humidity can increase water stress, especially at high temperature.
10.Humidity affects irrigation requirements through evapotranspiration.
11.Atmospheric humidity is not the same as soil moisture.
12.Humidity is especially important in greenhouses and post-harvest storage.

65. Final Takeaway
The central concept is:
Humidity describes the moisture condition of the atmosphere, and it strongly influences how rapidly cr
- ps lose water to the atmosphere.   
The most important agricultural relationship is:
Temperature + Humidity→VPD→Transpiration/ET→Crop water status      
At the same time:
High RH+Leaf wetness+Suitable temperature→greater risk of some plant diseases  +   +     
So, from an agricultural perspective, humidity is not simply “how wet the air feels.” It is a major
environmental factor controlling plant water loss, evapotranspiration, microclimate, disease
conditions and post-harvest quality.

Relative Humidity (RH)
## 1. Introduction
Relative Humidity (RH) is the ratio of the actual amount of water vapour present in the air to the
maximum amount of water vapour the air could hold at the same temperature, expressed as a
percentage.
In agriculture, RH is an important weather parameter because it affects:
- transpiration
- evapotranspiration
- crop water requirement

- stomatal behavior
- photosynthesis
- plant water stress
- pollination
- disease development
- pest activity
- dew and leaf wetness
- seed storage
- fruit and vegetable quality
- greenhouse conditions.
The basic relationship is:
RH=Actual vapour pressureSaturation vapour pressure×100        100  

2. Simple Meaning
Suppose air at a particular temperature can hold a maximum of 20 units of water vapour, but it
currently contains 10 units.
Then:
RH=1020×100=50%RH=  20  100=50\%
So:
RH=50% 
This means the air is at about half of its saturation vapour pressure at that temperature.

## 3. Definition
Relative humidity is the ratio of the actual vapour pressure of water in the air to the saturation
vapour pressure of water at the same temperature, multiplied by 100.
RH=eaes×100  e s  100  
where:
- RHRH = relative humidity (%)
- eae a = actual vapour pressure

- ese s = saturation vapour pressure.

4. Why Is It Called "Relative"?
It is called relative because the amount of water vapour is compared with the amount required for
saturation at that particular temperature.
This is extremely important.
The atmosphere's capacity to hold water vapour changes with temperature.
Therefore:
RH depends on both moisture content and temperature   

5. Relative Humidity Is Not the Same as
Absolute Humidity
Absolute humidity
Amount of water vapour per unit volume of air:
AH=mvVAH=  V 
Usually expressed as:
g/m3g/m 3

Relative humidity
Comparison of actual vapour pressure with saturation vapour pressure:
RH=eaes×100RH=  e s  100
Thus:
Absolute humidity = actual amount   
Relative humidity = actual amount relative to saturation   

6. Relative Humidity and Saturation
When:
RH=100%RH=100\%
the air is approximately saturated with respect to water at that temperature.

At saturation:
ea=ese a=e s
Therefore:
RH=eses×100=100%RH=  e s  100=100\%
If:
RH<100%RH<100\%
the air is not saturated.

7. Can Relative Humidity Be Greater Than
100%?
Under ordinary weather observations, RH is generally reported from approximately:
0%−100%0\%-100\%
Values slightly above 100% can occur temporarily in highly controlled or supersaturated atmospheric
conditions, but for normal agricultural interpretation:
RH≈100%=saturated air  

8. Relative Humidity and Temperature
This is one of the most important concepts.
Suppose the actual amount of water vapour remains constant.

Temperature increases
The saturation vapour pressure increases.
Therefore:
RH↓ 

Temperature decreases
The saturation vapour pressure decreases.
Therefore:
RH↑ 
This is why RH often increases during nighttime and decreases during the afternoon.

9. Daily Variation of Relative Humidity
A typical clear day may show:
Night

→ RH high

Early morning → RH very high
Morning

→ RH decreases

Afternoon

→ RH lowest

Evening

→ RH increases

Night

→ RH high

This pattern is not universal because clouds, rainfall, wind and local conditions can modify it.

10. Relative Humidity and Dew Point
When air cools to its dew-point temperature, it reaches saturation.
Thus:
Tair→Tdew point→RH≈100%   T      RH 100\%  
Further cooling can cause condensation.
This can produce:
- dew
- fog
- cloud droplets
- surface moisture.

11. Relative Humidity and Transpiration
Plants continuously lose water vapour through their aerial surfaces, particularly through stomata.
The surrounding atmosphere influences how strongly water vapour is pulled away from the leaf.

Low RH
RH↓RH Generally:
Atmospheric drying demand↑  and:
Transpiration↑  provided stomata and water supply allow it.

High RH
RH↑RH Generally:
Atmospheric drying demand↓  and:
Transpiration↓  under otherwise similar conditions.

12. Relative Humidity and VPD
For crop physiology, Vapor Pressure Deficit (VPD) is often more informative than RH alone.
VPD=es−ea 
Since:
RH=eaes×100RH=  e s  100
we can write:
VPD=es(1−RH100)  100  )  
where:
- ese s = saturation vapour pressure
- eae a = actual vapour pressure.

13. Why VPD Is Important
VPD describes the atmospheric demand for water.

High VPD
Atmospheric drying demand↑   
Potential consequences:
Transpiration demand↑  Water loss↑  Stomatal closure may increase 

Low VPD
Atmospheric drying demand↓   
Potentially:
Transpiration demand↓  14. RH Alone Can Be Misleading
Suppose two environments both have:
RH=50%RH=50\%
but one is:
20∘C20  C
and another is:
40∘C40  C
Their saturation vapour pressures are very different.
Therefore their VPD values are also different.
This gives an important agricultural principle:
Same RH≠same atmospheric drying demand   
because temperature matters.

15. Relative Humidity and Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration is:
ET=E+T 
where:
- EE = evaporation
- TT = transpiration.
Low RH generally increases atmospheric demand for evaporation and transpiration.
Therefore:
RH↓→VPD↑→ET demand↑RH  VPD  ET 
 High RH generally has the opposite effect.

16. Relative Humidity and Crop Water
Requirement
Crop water requirement depends partly on atmospheric demand.
A simplified crop evapotranspiration relationship is:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration
- KcK c = crop coefficient
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
RH is one of the weather variables influencing ET0ET 0.
Other important variables include:
- temperature
- solar radiation
- wind speed.

17. RH and Soil Moisture
Relative humidity describes the atmosphere, while soil moisture describes the water available in soil.
Therefore:
RH≠soil moisture   
A field can have:

High RH + dry soil
Possible during humid weather after prolonged lack of rainfall.

Low RH + wet soil
Possible after irrigation or rainfall followed by hot, dry weather.
Therefore both atmospheric and soil conditions must be considered for irrigation.

18. RH and Plant Water Stress
Low RH can increase atmospheric water demand.
If the soil cannot supply enough water:
RH↓→VPD↑→water loss demand↑→plant water stress↑RH  VPD     Under severe stress:
Stomatal closure→CO2 entry↓→photosynthesis↓   CO 2    19. RH and Stomata
Stomata control the exchange of:
- CO₂
- water vapour
- other gases.
When atmospheric demand becomes high, plants can reduce stomatal opening.
Conceptually:
RH↓→VPD↑→stomatal regulation   
The actual response depends on crop, genotype, soil water and other environmental conditions.

20. RH and Photosynthesis
Relative humidity affects photosynthesis mainly through plant water status and stomatal conductance.
A simplified chain is:
RH↓→VPD↑→water loss↑→stomatal closure→CO2 uptake↓→photosynthesis↓    CO 2     
This becomes especially important when soil moisture is also limited.

21. RH and Nutrient Transport
Transpiration contributes to the movement of water and dissolved minerals through the xylem.

Under appropriate conditions:
Transpiration↑→xylem flow↑    However, excessive atmospheric demand can cause water stress and reduce physiological performance.
Therefore:
More transpiration is not always better   

22. RH and Germination
Relative humidity of the atmosphere is not the primary requirement for seed germination.
The critical factor is water availability to the seed.
The basic process is:
Seed→Imbibition→Metabolic activation→Radicle emergence     Therefore:
RH≠seed moisture   
However, RH can affect seed drying and storage.

23. RH and Seed Storage
Relative humidity is extremely important during seed storage.
High RH can increase seed moisture.
Higher seed moisture can increase:
- respiration
- deterioration
- fungal growth
- insect activity
- loss of viability.
Therefore seed storage depends on appropriate:
Temperature+Seed moisture+RH + +   

24. RH and Flowering
Relative humidity can influence flowering indirectly through:
- plant water status
- flower health
- pollen behavior
- disease conditions.
Very dry atmospheric conditions can increase water stress.
Very humid conditions can favor some pathogens.

25. RH and Pollination
Pollination can be influenced by atmospheric conditions.
RH may affect:
- pollen release
- pollen hydration
- pollen viability
- stigma condition
- pollinator activity.
However, the response differs among crop species.
A better conceptual model is:
RH+temperature+wind+rainfall→pollination environment +
 +    

26. RH and Disease Development
This is one of the most important agricultural applications of RH.
High RH can create favorable conditions for certain pathogens, especially when combined with
suitable:
- temperature
- leaf wetness
- host susceptibility.

Thus:
High RH+Leaf wetness+Suitable temperature→increased risk of some diseases  +   +     
Examples can include certain fungal and bacterial diseases.
However, high RH does not mean that every plant disease will increase.

27. Relative Humidity vs Leaf Wetness
These are related but different.

Relative humidity
Water vapour condition of the air.

Leaf wetness
Liquid water present on the leaf surface.
Leaf wetness may come from:
- rainfall
- dew
- fog
- condensation
- irrigation.
Therefore:
RH≠leaf wetness   
High RH can favor prolonged leaf wetness, especially near saturation.

28. RH and Dew
At night, temperature often decreases.
As temperature approaches the dew point:
RH→100%RH 100\%
Further cooling may produce:
Condensation→Dew  Dew increases leaf wetness duration and can influence disease development.

29. RH and Plant Diseases
A simplified disease environment can be represented as:
Temperature+RH+Leaf wetness+Pathogen+Susceptible host→Disease development  +   +   +   +     
This is more realistic than assuming humidity alone causes disease.

30. RH and Greenhouse Agriculture
Relative humidity is especially important inside:
- greenhouses
- polyhouses
- net houses
- controlled-environment agriculture facilities.
Plants continuously release water vapour through transpiration.
Therefore:
Transpiration→water vapour accumulation→RH↑    RH Poor ventilation can cause excessive humidity.

31. Problems With Excessively High RH in
Greenhouses
Potential problems include:
- condensation
- prolonged leaf wetness
- fungal disease
- bacterial disease
- reduced transpiration

- altered nutrient transport
- poor drying.
Management may include:
- ventilation
- air circulation
- appropriate plant spacing
- irrigation management
- drainage
- humidity control systems where appropriate.

32. Problems With Very Low RH
Very low RH can lead to:
- excessive transpiration
- rapid water loss
- leaf wilting
- stomatal closure
- reduced photosynthesis
- increased irrigation demand.
Management can include:
- maintaining adequate soil moisture
- mulching
- reducing excessive wind exposure
- suitable shading where appropriate
- protected cultivation.

33. RH and Wind
Wind removes the humid air surrounding leaves.
Therefore:

Low RH+strong wind→greater atmospheric drying demand +    
Wind can therefore amplify the effects of low humidity.

34. RH and Temperature Together
Consider:
RH=40%RH=40\%
At a low temperature, the atmospheric water-vapour content may be relatively low.
At a high temperature, the same RH can correspond to a much higher actual water-vapour pressure and
a different VPD.
Therefore:
RH should be interpreted together with temperature   
This is why weather stations normally record both.

35. Morning vs Afternoon RH
A common agricultural weather pattern is:
Time
Temperature
RH
Early morning Lower
Higher
Late morning Increasing
Decreasing
Afternoon
Highest
Often lowest
Evening
Decreasing
Increasing
Night
Lower
Often high
Cloud cover, rainfall and wind can change this pattern.

36. RH and Post-Harvest Management
RH strongly influences the storage quality of fresh agricultural products.

Very low RH
Water loss↑  leading to:
- wilting

- shriveling
- weight loss.

Excessively high RH
Can increase:
- condensation
- fungal growth
- bacterial decay.
Thus:
Commodity-specific RH management   
is important.

37. RH and Fruits
For fruits, RH affects:
- water loss
- firmness
- shriveling
- disease
- shelf life.
A balance is required between:
preventing water loss 
and:
preventing condensation/decay 

38. RH and Vegetables
Leafy vegetables are particularly sensitive to water loss.
Low RH can rapidly cause:
Water loss↑→wilting   High RH can help reduce water loss but excessive moisture can promote decay.

39. RH and Crop Canopy
Inside dense crop canopies:
Wind speed↓  and:
Transpiration→water vapour addition  which can increase local humidity.
Thus:
Dense canopy→higher canopy RH→longer leaf wetness→potential disease risk      
This is one reason crop spacing affects disease management.

40. RH and Microclimate
The RH measured at a standard weather station may differ from the humidity experienced within the
crop canopy.
Microclimate depends on:
- canopy density
- plant height
- soil moisture
- wind
- radiation
- temperature
- transpiration.
Therefore:
Weather-station RH≠exact canopy RH   
in all situations.

41. Measuring Relative Humidity
Common instruments include:

Hygrometer
Instrument used to measure humidity.

Psychrometer
Uses dry-bulb and wet-bulb temperatures.

Electronic humidity sensor
Common in modern automatic weather stations.

42. Psychrometer Principle
A psychrometer has:
- dry-bulb thermometer
- wet-bulb thermometer.
The wet bulb is cooled by evaporation.

Dry air
More evaporation:
Tdry−Twet↑T    -T     Humid air
Less evaporation:
Tdry−Twet↓T    -T     Saturated air
Tdry≈TwetT     T    
and:
RH≈100%RH 100\%

43. Relative Humidity Categories
These are useful conceptual categories rather than universal agricultural thresholds:
RH condition
General implication
Very low RH Strong atmospheric drying demand
Low RH
Greater transpiration demand
Moderate RH Often moderate atmospheric demand

RH condition
General implication
High RH
Lower atmospheric drying demand
Near 100%
Saturation; condensation may occur
The exact favorable RH range depends on the crop, temperature and growth stage.

44. RH and Crop Water Management
An irrigation decision should not be based on RH alone.
A better approach considers:
Rainfall+Soil moisture+Temperature+RH/VPD+Wind+Radiation+Crop stage  +
  +   +   +   +   +
   
This provides a much better estimate of actual crop water demand.

45. RH and Drought
Drought involves both water supply and atmospheric demand.
For example:
Low rainfall+low RH+high temperature  +   +  
can produce strong water stress.
Thus:
Drought stress≠rainfall deficit alone    

46. RH and Heat Stress
A hot atmosphere with low RH can create high VPD.
T↑+ RH↓→VPD↑→atmospheric water demand↑   
If soil water is insufficient:
Water stress↑  which can compound heat stress.

47. Important Formula Set
Relative humidity
RH=eaes×100  e s  100  

Actual vapour pressure from RH
ea=esRH100  100   

Vapor Pressure Deficit
VPD=es−ea 

VPD from RH
VPD=es(1−RH100)  100  )  

Evapotranspiration
ET=E+T 

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

48. Complete Relationship With Crop Growth
The major agricultural pathway can be summarized as:
Temperature+RH→VPD→Atmospheric drying demand→Transpiration→Plant water status→Stomatal
behavior→CO2 uptake→Photosynthesis→Growth→Yield  +  
       2      
A separate pathway is:
High RH+Leaf wetness+Suitable temperature→favorable conditions for some diseases  +   +     

49. Important Differences
RH vs humidity
Humidity is the general concept of atmospheric water vapour.

Relative humidity specifically expresses atmospheric moisture relative to saturation.

RH vs VPD
RH=relative atmospheric moisture   
VPD=atmospheric drying demand   

RH vs rainfall
RH=water vapour in air   
Rainfall=water falling to the surface   

RH vs soil moisture
RH=atmospheric condition   
Soil moisture=water available in soil =   

RH vs leaf wetness
RH=water vapour condition   
Leaf wetness=liquid water on leaf surface =   

50. Exam-Ready Answer
Relative humidity is the ratio of actual water vapour pressure in the atmosphere to the saturation
vapour pressure at the same temperature, expressed as a percentage.
RH=eaes×100  e s  100  
It varies strongly with temperature. When temperature rises while actual moisture remains constant,
RH generally decreases; when temperature falls, RH generally increases.
In agriculture, RH affects transpiration, evapotranspiration, crop water requirement, stomatal
behavior, photosynthesis, plant water stress, flowering and pollination conditions, disease
development, greenhouse microclimate, seed storage and post-harvest quality.
Low RH generally increases atmospheric drying demand, while high RH reduces it. The combination
- f temperature and RH can be expressed more directly through Vapor Pressure Deficit (VPD):
VPD=es(1−RH100)  100  )  
Therefore, RH should not be interpreted alone; it should be considered together with temperature,
rainfall, wind, radiation, soil moisture and crop stage.

Final concept
Relative Humidity = How close the air is to water-vapour saturation at its current temperature.
   
And agriculturally:
RH + Temperature → VPD → Transpiration/ET → Crop Water Status   

Sunlight
## 1. Introduction
Sunlight is the electromagnetic radiation received from the Sun that provides the primary energy
source for most crop production.
For agriculture, sunlight is important because it controls:
- photosynthesis
- plant growth
- biomass production
- flowering
- photoperiodic responses
- seed and fruit development
- crop temperature
- evapotranspiration
- drying of crops
- crop yield and quality.
The fundamental agricultural relationship is:
Sunlight→Photosynthesis→Biomass→Economic yield      
However, sunlight is not simply "light." It has different wavelengths, intensity, duration and energy,
and plants respond differently to each.

2. What Is Sunlight?
Sunlight is electromagnetic radiation emitted by the Sun and reaching Earth's surface.
It includes different regions of the electromagnetic spectrum, particularly:
- ultraviolet (UV)
- visible radiation
- infrared (IR).
For crop production, the most important portion is photosynthetically active radiation (PAR).

3. Sunlight and Solar Radiation
The terms are related but not exactly identical.

Sunlight
The radiation from the Sun that reaches and illuminates Earth's surface.

Solar radiation
The broader scientific term for electromagnetic energy emitted by the Sun and received by Earth.
In agriculture, solar radiation measurements are commonly used to quantify the energy available to
crops.

4. Why Sunlight Is Essential for Crops
Green plants use sunlight to convert carbon dioxide and water into carbohydrates through
photosynthesis.
The simplified equation is:
6CO2+6H2O+light→C6H12O6+6O2   C 6H  12 
O 6+6O 2  
The chemical energy stored in carbohydrates ultimately supports:
- plant growth
- respiration
- roots
- stems
- leaves

- flowers
- fruits
- seeds
- grains
- tubers
- storage organs.
Therefore:
Sunlight is the primary external energy source for crop biomass production   

5. Components of Sunlight Relevant to
Agriculture
Sunlight can be considered in terms of:
## 1. Intensity
## 2. Duration
3. Quality / wavelength
4. Daily radiation
5. Seasonal radiation
6. Spatial distribution
These factors interact with:
- temperature
- water availability
- CO₂
- nutrients
- crop architecture.

6. Light Intensity
Light intensity refers to how much light energy is available at a particular location and time.
Higher light intensity can increase photosynthesis when light is limiting.

But this relationship is not unlimited.
A typical response is:
Low light→Photosynthesis increases with light    
until a saturation region is reached.
Beyond that:
More light⇏proportionally more photosynthesis 
   
Extremely strong radiation can even cause photoinhibition and stress.

7. Light Duration
Light duration refers to the length of time during which plants receive daylight.
For example:
Long day→more potential daily light exposure  Short day→less potential daily light exposure  But plant responses depend on both light intensity and duration.

8. Sunshine Duration
Sunshine duration is the amount of time during which the received solar radiation is sufficiently
strong to be considered bright sunshine according to a measurement criterion.
It is different from total daylight duration.
For example:
Daylight duration≠actual bright sunshine duration   
Clouds may reduce solar radiation while daylight still exists.

## 9. Photoperiod
Photoperiod is the duration of light and darkness within a 24-hour cycle.
It is particularly important for plant development and flowering.

Plants can respond to:
Day length / night length   
rather than simply the total amount of sunlight energy.

## 10. Photoperiodism
Photoperiodism is the physiological response of plants to the relative duration of light and darkness.
Major categories include:

Short-day plants
Flower when day length is below a critical photoperiod, or more precisely when the required night
duration is sufficiently long.
Examples include some varieties of:
- rice
- soybean
- sorghum
- chrysanthemum.

Long-day plants
Flower when day length exceeds a critical photoperiod.
Examples include:
- wheat
- barley
- spinach
- some varieties of oats.

Day-neutral plants
Flowering is not strongly controlled by photoperiod within the normal range.
Examples include some varieties of:
- tomato
- cucumber
- sunflower.
Crop responses are genotype-dependent.

11. Light Quality
Sunlight contains different wavelengths.
Important regions include:
Region
Approximate wavelength
Agricultural significance
UV
<400 nm Stress, signaling, protective responses
Visible
 400–700 nm Major photosynthetic radiation
Near infrared
>700 nm Plant structure, temperature and signaling interactions
The exact boundaries can vary by scientific convention.

12. Photosynthetically Active Radiation (PAR)
PAR is the portion of radiation approximately between:
400−700 nm  
that plants can use for photosynthesis.
PAR is one of the most important concepts in crop science.
PAR→photosynthesis→biomass production    

13. Photosynthetic Photon Flux Density (PPFD)
PAR can be measured in terms of the number of photosynthetically useful photons arriving at a surface.
This is called:
Photosynthetic Photon Flux Density (PPFD).
Common unit:
μmol m−2s−1 s  -1  
PPFD is especially important in:
- greenhouses
- vertical farms
- controlled-environment agriculture
- plant growth chambers.

14. Daily Light Integral (DLI)
DLI represents the total amount of photosynthetically active photons received by a unit area during an
entire day.
It is commonly expressed as:
mol m−2day−1 day  -1   
Conceptually:
DLI=∫dayPPFD dt  PPFD\,dt  
Thus:
Instantaneous light→PPFD  PPFD
while:
Daily accumulated light→DLI  DLI

15. Light Intensity vs DLI
These should not be confused.

## Ppfd
How much photosynthetic photon flux is arriving right now.

DLI
How much photosynthetic photon flux accumulated over an entire day.
For example:
A greenhouse can have moderate PPFD for many hours or high PPFD for a short period and potentially
achieve different DLI values.

16. Light Compensation Point
The light compensation point is the light intensity at which photosynthetic carbon fixation balances
respiratory carbon loss.
Conceptually:
Photosynthesis=Respiration =   
Therefore:
Net carbon gain≈0  0

Below the compensation point:
Respiration>Photosynthesis > 
Above it:
Photosynthesis>Respiration > 
under the relevant conditions.

17. Light Saturation Point
As light intensity increases, photosynthesis eventually reaches a region where additional light produces
little or no further increase.
This is the light saturation point.
Conceptually:
Light↑→Photosynthesis↑→Saturation      
Beyond saturation, additional light may not increase photosynthesis and can sometimes cause
photoinhibition.

18. Photosynthetic Light Response
A simplified response can be represented as:
Photosynthesis
│
│

 

│

 /

│

 /

│

 /

│

 /

│

 /

│ /
└────────────────────────── Light
Low

Saturation

The actual curve differs among:
- species

- varieties
- leaves
- developmental stages
- temperature
- CO₂
- water status.

## 19. Photoinhibition
Photoinhibition occurs when excessive light reduces photosynthetic efficiency or damages the
photosynthetic apparatus.
A simplified sequence is:
Excess radiation→photosynthetic stress→ROS generation→photosystem damage/
inhibition     Photosystem II is particularly important in photoinhibition.
Plants have protective mechanisms such as:
- non-photochemical quenching
- antioxidant systems
- pigment-based energy dissipation
- repair of photosynthetic proteins.

20. Sunlight and Chlorophyll
Chlorophyll absorbs light energy.
Major photosynthetic pigments include:
- chlorophyll aa
- chlorophyll bb
- carotenoids.
These pigments capture energy used in photosynthesis.
A simplified sequence is:

Photon→Pigment excitation→Electron transport→ATP+NADPH→Carbon fixation     ATP+NADPH   

21. Sunlight and Photosynthesis
The overall process can be divided into:

Light reactions
Occur in the thylakoid membranes.
Produce:
- ATP
## • Nadph
- oxygen from water splitting.

Calvin cycle
Occurs in the chloroplast stroma.
Uses:
- CO₂
- ATP
## • Nadph
to produce carbohydrate precursors.
Thus:
Sunlight→ATP/NADPH→CO2 fixation→carbohydrates     2    

22. Sunlight and Crop Biomass
More effective light interception generally provides more potential energy for photosynthesis.
Conceptually:
Light interception→photosynthesis→carbon gain→biomass 
    
But this depends on:
- water

- nutrients
- temperature
- CO₂
- leaf health.
Therefore:
More sunlight alone does not guarantee more yield   

23. Sunlight and Leaf Area
The crop canopy determines how efficiently sunlight is intercepted.
A key parameter is:
LAI=Total one-sided leaf areaGround area  
     
where LAI = Leaf Area Index.
As LAI increases:
Light interception↑  up to an appropriate canopy density.
Beyond that, excessive self-shading can reduce photosynthesis of lower leaves.

24. Canopy Light Distribution
A crop canopy contains:
- sunlit leaves
- shaded leaves.
Upper leaves receive more direct radiation.
Lower leaves receive less radiation because light is absorbed and scattered by upper leaves.
Therefore:
Canopy architecture→light distribution→whole-canopy photosynthesis     

25. Sunlight and Crop Architecture
Important architectural characteristics include:
- leaf angle
- leaf orientation
- leaf size
- plant height
- branching
- plant density
- canopy thickness.
A well-structured canopy can distribute light more effectively.
This is important for crops such as:
- wheat
- rice
- maize
- soybean
- cotton.

26. Sunlight and Plant Density
If plants are too widely spaced:
Light interception↓  If plants are too densely planted:
Self-shading↑  Therefore an optimum plant population aims for effective canopy light interception without excessive
competition.

27. Sunlight and Shade
Shading reduces available radiation.
Moderate shade may be beneficial for some shade-tolerant crops.

Excessive shade can cause:
- reduced photosynthesis
- reduced biomass
- elongated weak stems
- reduced flowering
- lower yield.
Shade-loving and shade-tolerant crops can respond differently from sun-loving crops.

## 28. Etiolation
When plants develop under insufficient light, they may exhibit etiolation.
Typical features include:
- elongated stems
- pale/yellow appearance
- reduced chlorophyll
- poorly developed leaves.
The plant is essentially attempting to reach a better light environment.

29. Sunlight and Stem Growth
Light influences stem elongation.
Under low light:
Stem elongation↑  in many plants.
This is associated with shade-avoidance responses.
Plants may develop:
- longer internodes
- altered leaf angles
- increased height.

30. Sunlight and Flowering
Sunlight affects flowering through two distinct mechanisms:

Energy supply
Photosynthesis supplies carbohydrates needed for reproduction.

Photoperiodic signaling
Day/night duration can act as a developmental signal.
Thus:
Light quantity≠photoperiod signal   
This distinction is extremely important.

31. Sunlight Quantity vs Photoperiod
Sunlight quantity
How much radiation/energy the plant receives.

Photoperiod
How long the plant experiences light and darkness.
A plant can receive strong light for a short duration or weaker light for a longer duration.
The two can produce different physiological responses.

32. Sunlight and Flower Initiation
Photoperiod can trigger flowering responses in photoperiod-sensitive plants.
The simplified sequence is:
Day/night duration→photoreceptor signaling→flowering pathways→flower initiation     The response depends on:
- species
- cultivar
- developmental stage.

## 33. Phytochrome
Phytochrome is a major plant photoreceptor involved in sensing red and far-red light.
It helps regulate:
- seed germination
- shade responses
- stem elongation
- flowering
- photomorphogenesis.
It exists in interconvertible forms commonly described as:
Pr↔PfrPr Pfr
Red and far-red light influence the balance between these forms.

34. Other Light Receptors
Plants also have photoreceptors such as:

Cryptochromes
Respond primarily to blue/UV-A wavelengths.

Phototropins
Important in:
- phototropism
- stomatal responses
- chloroplast movement.

UVR8
Involved in responses to UV-B radiation.
Thus plants can detect not just the amount of light, but its spectral composition.

35. Sunlight and Phototropism
Phototropism is directional growth in response to light.
A common example is shoot growth toward light.

The simplified concept is:
Directional light→auxin redistribution→differential cell elongation→shoot bending      

36. Sunlight and Stomata
Light generally promotes stomatal opening in many plants, particularly through blue-light signaling.
Open stomata allow:
CO2→leafCO 2 but also:
H2O→atmosphereH 2O Therefore sunlight creates an important trade-off:
CO2 uptake↔water loss  2    

37. Sunlight and Transpiration
Sunlight affects transpiration in several ways.
Radiation can:
- increase leaf temperature
- drive evaporation
- promote stomatal opening under suitable conditions.
Therefore:
Solar radiation→evaporative demand→transpiration     
The actual response depends strongly on humidity, wind and soil moisture.

38. Sunlight and Evapotranspiration
Solar radiation is one of the major drivers of evapotranspiration.
ET=f(radiation,temperature,humidity,wind,…) , ,
 , , )  
High solar radiation generally increases energy available for evaporation and transpiration.

39. Sunlight and Temperature
Solar radiation provides energy that can heat:
- leaves
- soil
- crop canopy
- surrounding air.
Therefore:
Solar radiation→surface energy→temperature    
But air temperature and solar radiation are not the same variable.

40. Sunlight and Heat Stress
Strong radiation combined with high air temperature and high VPD can increase heat and water stress.
A simplified relationship is:
Strong radiation+high temperature+high VPD→heat/water stress risk↑  +
  +     This can cause:
- stomatal closure
- reduced photosynthesis
- membrane stress
- oxidative stress.

41. Sunlight and C3 Plants
C3 plants initially fix CO₂ into a three-carbon compound.
Examples include:
- rice
- wheat
- soybean
- potato

- many vegetables.
Their photosynthesis can be strongly affected by:
- temperature
- CO₂
- light
- photorespiration.

42. Sunlight and C4 Plants
C4 plants have specialized carbon-concentrating mechanisms.
Examples include:
- maize
- sugarcane
- sorghum
- pearl millet.
C4 crops often have high photosynthetic productivity under:
- high light
- high temperature
- suitable water conditions.
But high light does not eliminate the effects of drought or heat stress.

43. Sunlight and CAM Plants
CAM plants separate carbon fixation partly by time.
They commonly open stomata at night and reduce daytime water loss.
A classic example is:
Pineapple  
This adaptation improves water-use efficiency under dry conditions.

44. Sunlight and Crop Yield
Yield depends on how effectively crops:

1. intercept radiation,
2. convert radiation into biomass,
3. allocate biomass to economic organs.
Conceptually:
Yield=Radiation interception×Radiation-use efficiency×Harvest allocation  =
     
This is a conceptual framework rather than a universal exact equation.

45. Radiation Use Efficiency
Radiation Use Efficiency (RUE) describes how efficiently intercepted radiation is converted into
biomass.
Conceptually:
RUE=Biomass producedIntercepted photosynthetically active radiation        
Units depend on the chosen radiation measurement.
RUE varies with:
- crop
- temperature
- water stress
- nutrients
- CO₂
- disease
- developmental stage.

46. Sunlight and Source-Sink Relations
Leaves act primarily as sources when they produce and export assimilates.
Developing:
- seeds
- grains
- fruits

- tubers
- roots
can act as sinks.
Thus:
Sunlight→Photosynthesis→Assimilates→Sinks      

47. Sunlight and Grain Filling
During grain filling:
Photosynthesis→carbohydrates→grain    Insufficient radiation can reduce assimilate production.
If low radiation persists:
Grain filling↓→grain weight↓    This is particularly important during cloudy periods.

48. Sunlight and Fruit Development
Adequate radiation supports:
- photosynthesis
- carbohydrate production
- fruit growth
- coloration
- sugar accumulation in many fruits.
However, excessive direct radiation combined with high temperatures can cause sunburn or heat injury
in susceptible fruits.

49. Sunlight and Fruit Quality
Sunlight can influence:
- sugar accumulation

- pigmentation
- color
- flavor
- dry matter.
The effect varies strongly by crop and fruit.

50. Sunlight and Oilseed Crops
Oilseed crops require photosynthetic assimilates for:
- seed development
- oil accumulation.
During seed filling:
Photosynthesis→assimilates→seed→oil accumulation     Insufficient radiation can affect seed filling depending on crop and environmental conditions.

51. Sunlight and Fiber Crops
In crops such as cotton:
Photosynthesis→biomass→reproductive development→fiber production 
   Radiation affects canopy development and boll formation.

52. Sunlight and Seed Germination
Some seeds respond to light during germination.
They can be:
- positively photoblastic
- negatively photoblastic
- relatively insensitive to light.
This is regulated by photoreceptors, particularly phytochrome.

Therefore:
Light requirement for germination is crop/species-specific   

53. Sunlight and Seedling Development
Adequate light allows seedlings to transition toward autotrophic growth.
The process is:
Seed reserves→seedling emergence→leaf development→photosynthesis→independent carbon gain t Seed reserves      Insufficient light can cause weak, elongated seedlings.

54. Sunlight and Nutrient Use
Photosynthesis supplies carbon skeletons and energy needed for plant metabolism.
Therefore nutrient utilization is closely connected to photosynthetic activity.
For example:
Nitrogen→chlorophyll/proteins→photosynthetic machinery    and:
Sunlight→photosynthetic energy   Thus light and nutrients interact strongly.

55. Sunlight and Water Availability
Sunlight cannot be considered independently of water.
High radiation with adequate water:
Photosynthesis↑  High radiation with severe water stress:
Stomatal closure→CO2 uptake↓   CO 2  Therefore:

High sunlight+low water≠high productivity + 
  

56. Cloudy Weather
Clouds reduce incoming solar radiation.
Therefore:
Cloud cover↑→Radiation↓    Potential effects include:
- lower photosynthesis
- lower crop temperature
- lower evapotranspiration
- slower drying.
However, diffuse light from clouds can sometimes penetrate crop canopies more effectively than direct
light, so the relationship between cloudiness and whole-canopy photosynthesis is not simply "cloudy =
bad."

57. Direct vs Diffuse Radiation
Direct radiation
Travels directly from the Sun.

Diffuse radiation
Scattered by:
- clouds
- aerosols
- atmospheric molecules.
Diffuse radiation can penetrate deeper into some crop canopies.
Therefore:
Radiation quality within canopy≠radiation above canopy    

58. Sunlight and Season
Sunlight varies seasonally because of:
- day length
- solar angle
- atmospheric conditions
- cloud cover
- Earth's seasonal position.
Thus crop growth conditions differ between seasons.

59. Sunlight and Latitude
Day length and solar angle vary with latitude and season.
Therefore:
Latitude→day length/solar geometry→radiation pattern     
This contributes to geographical differences in crop adaptation.

60. Sunlight and Time of Day
Solar radiation generally changes throughout the day.
Typical pattern:
Sunrise → radiation increases
↓
midday
↓
radiation maximum
↓
afternoon
↓
sunset

Clouds and atmospheric conditions can alter this pattern.

61. Sunlight and Crop Seasons
Different crops have different light requirements.

Kharif crops
Often grow during a period with substantial solar radiation but also substantial cloud cover and rainfall
in monsoon regions.

Rabi crops
Experience a different combination of:
- day length
- solar angle
- temperature
- cloudiness.
Therefore crop growth depends on the combined seasonal environment rather than sunlight alone.

62. Sunlight and Greenhouse Management
Protected cultivation allows some control over the radiation environment.
Methods include:
- shading nets
- thermal screens
- reflective materials
- artificial lighting
- greenhouse orientation
- glazing selection.
The goal is:
Provide sufficient light without excessive heat load   

63. Artificial Light
In controlled environments, artificial lights can supplement sunlight.
Important characteristics include:

- intensity
- duration
- spectrum
- photoperiod.
LED lighting is widely used because its spectrum and intensity can be controlled.
The relevant measurements can include:
## Ppfdppfd
and:
## Dlidli
64. Sunlight Management in Agriculture
Farmers can manage crop exposure through:

Plant spacing
Controls canopy density.

Row orientation
Can influence light distribution within the canopy.

Pruning
Improves light penetration in some horticultural crops.

Training
Changes canopy architecture.

Shade nets
Reduce excessive radiation.

Protected cultivation
Allows greater environmental control.

Variety selection
Choose cultivars adapted to local light and climate conditions.

65. Row Orientation
Row orientation can influence how sunlight reaches crop leaves.
It affects:
- morning exposure
- afternoon exposure
- canopy shading
- temperature
- photosynthetic efficiency.
The optimal orientation is not universal; it depends on:
- latitude
- crop architecture
- season
- planting geometry.

66. Sunlight and Weed Competition
Weeds compete with crops for sunlight.
A dense crop canopy can reduce the amount of light reaching weeds:
Crop canopy↑→light at soil surface↓→some weed growth↓      Therefore rapid canopy development can contribute to weed suppression.

67. Sunlight and Shade Avoidance
Plants can detect changes in red-to-far-red radiation caused by neighboring plants.
When competition is detected, some plants respond by:
- increasing stem elongation
- changing leaf angle
- altering branching
- accelerating reproductive development in some species.

This is called a shade-avoidance response.

68. Sunlight and Senescence
Adequate light helps maintain photosynthetic activity.
Insufficient light can accelerate loss of photosynthetic function in shaded leaves.
Therefore:
Low canopy light→photosynthesis↓→leaf aging/senescence may increase 
   The actual response depends on species and canopy conditions.

69. Sunlight and Harvest
Sunlight affects:
- crop drying
- seed/grain moisture loss
- fruit quality
- field drying.
After harvest, solar radiation can also be used for drying agricultural products, but excessive heating
can reduce quality.

70. Sunlight and Post-Harvest Drying
Solar drying uses:
Solar radiation→heating→water evaporation     
But drying must be controlled to prevent:
- overheating
- uneven drying
- contamination
- quality deterioration.

74. Sunlight vs Temperature
These are closely related but different.

Sunlight
Provides electromagnetic energy.

Temperature
Measures the thermal state of the environment or object.
Sunlight can increase temperature, but temperature is also affected by:
- wind
- humidity
- cloud cover
- soil properties
- longwave radiation.
Therefore:
Sunlight≠temperature   

75. Sunlight vs Photoperiod
This is another crucial distinction.
Sunlight quantity=how much radiation =   
Photoperiod=how long light/darkness lasts =   
A plant can respond to photoperiod even when the total radiation received is not the primary signal.

76. Sunlight vs Solar Radiation vs PAR
Solar radiation
Broad electromagnetic energy from the Sun.

Sunlight
Solar radiation reaching the surface.

PAR
Approximately the 400–700 nm portion relevant to photosynthesis.
Therefore:
PAR⊂solar radiation
