83. Complete Heat-Stress Mechanism
The complete agricultural pathway can be summarized as:
High temperature↓VPD and evaporative demand↑↓Water loss↑↓Stomatal closure↓CO2 uptake↓↓Photos
ynthesis↓  & \\ & \\ &  \\ & \\ &  \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ &CO 2  \\ & \\
&   
At the same time:
Heat→protein/membrane stress→ROS↑→cellular damage    ROS   
And during reproduction:
Heat→pollen viability↓→fertilization↓→seed/fruit set↓         
Together:

Heat stress→physiological damage+reproductive damage+shortened filling period→yield/quality loss\
boxed     +   +
    

84. Exam-Ready Definition
Heat stress is the physiological condition caused by exposure of a plant to temperatures above its
- ptimal range for sufficient intensity and duration to disrupt normal physiological, biochemical,
reproductive and developmental processes. Heat stress can reduce photosynthesis, increase
respiratory and water losses, damage membranes and proteins, increase oxidative stress, reduce
pollen viability and fertilization, shorten grain-filling duration, accelerate senescence and
ultimately reduce crop yield and quality.

85. Final Takeaway
Remember heat stress through six major effects:
HEAT=Water loss+Photosynthesis decline+Protein/membrane damage+Reproductive failure+Faster de
velopment+Yield/quality loss  =   +   +   +   +   +    
The most important relationship is:
High Temperature+Duration+Sensitive Growth Stage→Heat Stress  +
  +     
And the agricultural objective is not simply to lower air temperature, but to maintain:
Suitable tissue temperature+adequate water status+photosynthetic activity+reproductive success     +   +   +
   
So, in one sentence:
Heat stress occurs when excessive temperature overwhelms a plant's ability to
regulate temperature, water balance, metabolism and cellular stability, ultimately
impairing growth, reproduction, yield and quality.

Cold Stress
## 1. Introduction
Cold stress is the physiological stress experienced by a plant when temperature falls below the range
required for normal growth, metabolism, development, or reproduction.

It is one of the major abiotic stresses affecting crops.
The basic relationship is:
Low temperature→metabolic disruption→growth impairment→reproductive/yield loss      
Cold stress is broader than frost. It includes both non-freezing low-temperature stress (chilling) and
freezing/frost stress.

## 2. Definition
Cold stress is the physiological and biochemical disturbance caused by exposure of a plant to
temperatures below its optimal range for a sufficient intensity and duration.
A useful conceptual model is:
Cold stress=f(temperature,duration,crop,genotype,developmental stage)  =
f( , , , , )  
There is therefore no single temperature that defines cold stress for every crop.

3. Cold Stress vs Frost
This is one of the most important distinctions.

Cold stress
A broad term covering harmful low-temperature conditions.

Chilling stress
Low-temperature stress that occurs without substantial freezing of plant tissues.

Freezing/frost stress
Low-temperature stress in which ice formation occurs or freezing conditions cause injury.
Conceptually:
Cold stress=Chilling stress+Freezing/frost stress  =   +
   
Although exact scientific classifications can vary by crop and context.

4. Chilling Stress
Chilling stress occurs when temperature is low enough to interfere with physiological processes but is
generally above the temperature at which extensive tissue freezing occurs.
It can affect:
- germination
- root growth
- photosynthesis
- membrane function
- enzyme activity
- nutrient uptake
- flowering
- reproductive development.
Tropical crops are often particularly sensitive to chilling.

5. Freezing Stress
Freezing stress occurs when temperatures are sufficiently low for ice formation in or around plant
tissues.
The basic mechanism is:
Low temperature→ice formation→cell dehydration/membrane damage→tissue injury      
This overlaps strongly with the frost topic.

6. Cold Stress and Cardinal Temperatures
Every crop and physiological process has temperature limits.
Tmin⁡<Topt<Tmax⁡ <T    <T      
where:
- Tmin⁡T     = lower threshold
- ToptT     = optimum range

- Tmax⁡T     = upper threshold.
When temperature falls below the appropriate lower range:
T<ToptT<T    
growth and metabolic rates generally decline.
At sufficiently low temperatures, injury occurs.

7. Cold Stress Is Process-Specific
The minimum temperature for:
- germination
- photosynthesis
- root growth
- flowering
- pollen development
- grain filling
may all be different.
Therefore:
Tcold threshold=f(crop+process+genotype+stage)   = f( +
 + + )  

8. Why Cold Stress Is Harmful
Temperature affects almost every biochemical reaction in a plant.
Cold can alter:
- enzyme activity
- membrane fluidity
- photosynthesis
- respiration
- water transport
- nutrient uptake
- hormone balance

- gene expression
- flowering
- pollen development
- seed development.

9. Basic Mechanism of Cold Stress
A simplified sequence is:
Temperature↓→enzyme activity↓+membrane changes+metabolic imbalance→ROS imbalance→growt
h/reproductive injury    +   +      
Under freezing conditions:
Cold→ice formation→dehydration→cell damage      

10. Cold Stress and Enzyme Activity
Most enzymes function within a particular temperature range.
As temperature decreases:
Reaction rate↓   
Consequently:
- metabolism slows
- nutrient assimilation slows
- growth slows
- cellular repair slows.
At extremely low temperatures, some enzymes may become severely inhibited.

11. Cold Stress and Membranes
This is a fundamental mechanism.
Plant cell membranes contain lipids whose physical properties depend on temperature.
Cold can cause membrane lipids to become more rigid.

Conceptually:
Cold→membrane fluidity↓→transport/function↓       
Severe cold can impair:
- ion transport
- membrane proteins
- photosynthetic membranes
- mitochondrial membranes.

12. Membrane Lipid Adaptation
Plants can adjust membrane lipid composition to maintain membrane function.
A greater proportion of unsaturated fatty acids can help maintain membrane fluidity under cold
conditions.
This is called:
Homeoviscous adaptation  
Simplified:
Cold→membrane rigidification risk→lipid adjustment→fluidity maintenance     13. Cold Stress and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis can be reduced by cold because:
- enzyme activity decreases
- electron transport is affected
- stomatal conductance may decline
- chloroplast function is impaired
- carbon fixation slows.
Therefore:
Cold→photosynthesis↓     

14. Cold and Photosystem Damage
When cold reduces carbon fixation while light absorption continues, the plant may experience an
imbalance between light energy capture and its ability to use that energy.
This can increase oxidative stress and damage photosynthetic machinery, particularly under strong
light.
Thus:
Cold+strong light→photoinhibition risk↑ +     

15. Cold Stress and Respiration
Respiration generally slows as temperature decreases.
T↓→respiration rate↓   
However, during severe stress, normal metabolic balance can be disrupted.
In cold-sensitive tissues, energy production may become insufficient to support growth and repair.

16. Cold Stress and Root Growth
Cold soil can strongly affect roots.
Potential effects include:
- reduced root elongation
- reduced branching
- reduced nutrient uptake
- reduced water uptake
- reduced root respiration.
Thus:
Cold soil→root growth↓→water/nutrient uptake↓       

17. Cold Stress and Nutrient Uptake
Nutrient uptake depends on:
- root activity

- membrane transport
- diffusion
- microbial activity
- soil processes.
Cold can reduce several of these.
Therefore:
Cold→root activity↓→nutrient uptake↓       
Nutrient deficiency-like symptoms may therefore appear even when nutrients are present in the soil.

18. Cold Stress and Germination
Germination requires:
- water
- oxygen
- viable seed
- appropriate temperature.
Cold can slow:
- imbibition-related metabolism
- enzyme activity
- reserve mobilization
- radicle emergence
- seedling growth.
Thus:
Cold→germination rate↓     
and often:
Time to emergence↑  19. Cold Stress and Seedling Establishment
Cold can cause:

- slow emergence
- weak seedlings
- poor root growth
- poor nutrient uptake
- increased susceptibility to some diseases
- uneven crop establishment.
Therefore:
Cold→poor establishment→uneven crop stand     

20. Cold Stress and Seedling Vigor
Seedling vigor depends partly on how quickly a seedling establishes its root and shoot systems.
Cold slows:
- cell division
- cell expansion
- enzyme activity
- root development.
Therefore:
Cold→seedling vigor↓     

21. Cold Stress and Vegetative Growth
During vegetative growth, cold can reduce:
- leaf expansion
- stem elongation
- branching
- tillering
- root growth
- biomass accumulation.
Consequently:
Cold→growth rate↓     

22. Cold Stress and Cell Division
Cell division and cell expansion are temperature-dependent.
Low temperature can slow:
Cell division 
and:
Cell expansion 
Therefore plant growth becomes slower.

23. Cold Stress and Water Relations
Cold can reduce:
- root water uptake
- membrane transport
- hydraulic conductivity.
Even when soil contains water:
Cold soil→root water uptake↓     
The plant can therefore experience a type of physiological water stress.

24. Cold-Induced Water Stress
A plant may have sufficient soil water but still have difficulty absorbing it when roots are cold.
This can lead to:
- wilting
- reduced stomatal conductance
- reduced photosynthesis.
Therefore:
Cold soil→water uptake↓→plant water status↓       

25. Cold Stress and Flower Initiation
Temperature is one of the major environmental signals controlling flowering.
Cold can either:
- delay flowering
- promote flowering after a required cold exposure
- damage developing reproductive structures.
This depends on the crop.

## 26. Vernalization
Some plants require prolonged exposure to relatively low temperatures to become competent to flower.
This process is:
Vernalization  
Examples include many winter cereals such as certain wheat and barley types.
Important distinction:
Vernalization≠cold stress   
Vernalization is a beneficial developmental response to specific cold exposure, whereas cold stress is
harmful exposure.

27. Vernalization vs Cold Stress
Feature
Vernalization
Cold stress
Nature
Developmental requirement
Stress condition
Effect
Can promote flowering
Causes physiological damage
Duration
Usually prolonged, specific exposure Variable
Outcome
Reproductive transition
Growth/yield impairment
Crop response Genotype-specific
Genotype- and stage-specific

28. Cold Stress During Flowering
Cold during flowering can interfere with:
- pollen development
- pollen release

- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth
- stigma receptivity
- fertilization.
Thus:
Cold during flowering→fertility↓→seed/fruit set↓       

29. Cold and Pollen
Low temperatures can reduce:
- pollen viability
- pollen germination
- pollen tube growth.
This can result in poor fertilization.

30. Cold and Fertilization
If pollen is nonviable or pollen tube growth is impaired:
Pollination⇏successful fertilization    Therefore:
Cold→pollen/stigma dysfunction→fertilization↓      

31. Cold Stress and Fruit/Grain Set
Poor fertilization can lead to:
- flower abortion
- fruit abortion
- grain abortion
- reduced seed number.
Thus:

Cold→reproductive failure→yield components↓      

32. Cold Stress and Grain Filling
Low temperature during grain filling can slow:
- starch synthesis
- protein accumulation
- assimilate transport
- enzyme activity.
Therefore:
Cold→grain filling rate↓     
and potentially:
grain weight↓  33. Cold Stress and Maturity
Low temperature can delay crop development.
Therefore:
Cold→development rate↓→maturity delayed      
This can extend crop duration.

34. Cold Stress and GDD
Growing Degree Days measure accumulated thermal time:
GDD=max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb)  -T b)  
If temperatures remain close to or below the base temperature:
GDD≈0GDD 0
Therefore crop development accumulates thermal time slowly.

35. Cold Stress and Crop Duration
Cold can extend the time required to reach:
- flowering
- grain filling
- maturity.
Conceptually:
Cold→thermal accumulation↓→development slows→crop duration↑        

36. Cold Stress and Senescence
Cold can alter senescence depending on crop and severity.
Mild cold may delay developmental progression, while severe stress can cause:
- chlorophyll damage
- oxidative stress
- tissue injury
- premature senescence.
Therefore cold does not always simply "delay senescence."

37. Cold Stress and Reactive Oxygen Species
Cold can disturb electron transport and metabolism.
This may increase:
Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS)  
including:
- superoxide
- hydrogen peroxide
- hydroxyl radicals.
Excess ROS can damage:
- membranes

- proteins
- pigments
- DNA.

38. Antioxidant Defense
Plants respond using antioxidant systems.
Important enzymes include:
- superoxide dismutase (SOD)
- catalase (CAT)
- ascorbate peroxidase (APX)
- glutathione-related enzymes.
Non-enzymatic antioxidants include:
- ascorbate
- glutathione
- carotenoids
- tocopherols.
Thus:
Cold→ROS↑→antioxidant defense↑   ROS    

39. Osmotic Adjustment
Cold-tolerant plants can accumulate compatible solutes such as:
- soluble sugars
- proline
- raffinose-family oligosaccharides
- certain amino acids.
These can help:
- stabilize membranes
- protect proteins

- maintain cellular water relations.

40. Cold Stress Proteins
Plants produce various stress-associated proteins during cold exposure.
These can include:
- cold-responsive proteins
- dehydrins
- antifreeze-related proteins
- protective chaperones.
These help protect cells during low-temperature exposure.

41. Antifreeze Proteins
Some plants produce antifreeze proteins that can interact with ice crystals and limit their growth.
This contributes to freezing tolerance in certain species.
However, antifreeze proteins are only one part of the much larger freezing-tolerance system.

42. Cold Acclimation
Cold acclimation is the physiological adjustment that increases a plant's tolerance after exposure to
non-lethal low temperatures.
The sequence can be:
Moderate cold→gene/metabolic changes→protective adjustments→greater freezing tolerance      

43. Cold Hardening
Cold hardening is often used similarly to cold acclimation, especially when discussing increased
tolerance to freezing temperatures.
Plants may:
- modify membrane lipids
- accumulate sugars

- increase antioxidants
- produce protective proteins
- alter gene expression.

## 44. Deacclimation
When a plant experiences warm temperatures after acclimation:
Warm conditions→cold tolerance↓     
This is called deacclimation.
A subsequent cold event can therefore cause greater injury.

45. Cold Stress and Membrane Lipids
This is one of the most important adaptations.
Cold tends to make membranes more rigid.
Plants can increase membrane unsaturation:
Unsaturated fatty acids↑→membrane fluidity maintained 
   
This is particularly important in chloroplast and cellular membranes.

46. Cold Stress and Hormones
Cold stress interacts with:
- ABA
- ethylene
- jasmonates
- salicylic acid
- auxin
- cytokinins
- gibberellins.
Hormones regulate:

- stomatal responses
- growth
- metabolism
- stress signaling
- flowering
- senescence.

47. Cold Stress and ABA
Abscisic acid (ABA) can participate in stress signaling and stomatal regulation.
The pathway can be represented as:
Cold signal→stress signaling→ABA and other pathways→protective responses      
The exact response depends on tissue and species.

48. Cold Stress and Photoperiod
Photoperiod and temperature interact in determining crop development.
Thus:
Photoperiod+Temperature+Genotype→phenology  +   +
    
A cold period can delay development even if day length is favorable.

49. Cold Stress and Wind
Wind can increase cooling of exposed plant surfaces.
The effect depends on:
- air temperature
- wind speed
- humidity
- radiation

- plant water status.
In cold conditions, strong wind can increase convective heat loss.

50. Wind Chill vs Plant Temperature
"Wind chill" is primarily a measure of how cold conditions affect the perceived cooling of exposed
- bjects or people.
It should not automatically be interpreted as a separate air temperature experienced by plants.
Plant tissue temperature depends on the complete energy balance.

51. Cold Stress and Humidity
Humidity affects the rate of heat and water exchange.
Cold + high humidity may promote:
- condensation
- leaf wetness
- fungal disease risk.
Cold + dry conditions may increase desiccation risk under certain conditions.

52. Cold Stress and Disease
Cold conditions can influence pathogen development and plant defense.
Some pathogens thrive under cool, wet conditions.
Thus:
Cold+high humidity+leaf wetness→some disease risks↑  +   +
     
But pathogen responses are species-specific.

53. Cold Stress and Soil Microorganisms
Low soil temperature can slow:
- microbial activity
- decomposition

- nutrient mineralization
- biological nitrogen cycling.
Therefore:
Cold soil→microbial activity↓→nutrient availability dynamics change 
    

54. Cold Stress and Nitrogen
Cold can reduce plant nitrogen uptake and soil nitrogen mineralization.
Therefore crops may show symptoms resembling nutrient deficiency even where soil nutrient levels are
not necessarily low.

55. Cold Stress in Tropical Crops
Tropical and subtropical crops can be particularly sensitive to chilling.
Examples include:
- rice
- maize
- soybean
- cotton
- many tropical vegetables.
The exact tolerance varies greatly among cultivars.

56. Cold Stress in Temperate Crops
Many temperate crops have mechanisms for cold acclimation.
Examples include:
- wheat
- barley
- rye
- oats
- some temperate vegetables.

Some winter varieties can tolerate much lower temperatures after acclimation than non-acclimated
plants.

57. Winter vs Spring Crops
Winter-type crops
Often planted before winter and exposed to cold during early development.
Some require vernalization.

Spring-type crops
Usually have lower or different cold requirements and are planted to develop during warmer
conditions.
This distinction is especially important in cereals.

58. Cold Stress and Frost-Sensitive Crops
Tropical crops can suffer severe damage from temperatures that temperate crops tolerate.
Therefore:
Same temperature≠same biological effect    
because genotype and acclimation matter.

59. Cold Stress and Frost Risk
Cold stress can progress from non-freezing stress toward freezing injury:
Cool→Chilling stress→Severe cold→Freezing→Frost injury       
The boundaries are crop-specific.

60. Cold Stress Management
Management can be divided into:

Avoidance
Avoid exposing sensitive stages to cold.

Protection
Protect plants during cold events.

Tolerance
Use varieties capable of withstanding low temperatures.

61. Variety Selection
One of the most effective long-term strategies is selecting:
- cold-tolerant varieties
- frost-tolerant varieties
- appropriate maturity types
- varieties suited to local climate.

62. Sowing-Date Adjustment
Sowing date affects crop developmental timing.
The objective is to avoid having sensitive stages coincide with severe cold.
Sowing date→crop phenology→cold exposure risk     

63. Protected Cultivation
Protected structures can reduce exposure to cold.
Examples:
- greenhouse
- polyhouse
- low tunnel
- high tunnel
- frost cloth/protective covering.
These can modify the crop microclimate.

64. Covering Plants
For some horticultural crops, protective coverings can reduce radiative and convective heat loss.
Examples:
- frost cloth
- row covers
- suitable insulating materials.
The objective is:
Plant heat loss↓→tissue temperature maintained    

65. Irrigation for Frost Protection
Under suitable conditions, water can provide heat during freezing:
Water freezing→latent heat release    
This principle can be used in carefully managed frost-protection systems.
However, this is a frost-protection technique, not a general treatment for all forms of cold stress.

## 66. Mulching
Mulching can help moderate soil temperature.
It may:
- reduce nighttime soil cooling
- conserve soil moisture
- reduce temperature fluctuations.
Materials include:
- straw
- crop residues
- organic mulch.

67. Soil Moisture Management
Adequate soil moisture can increase soil heat storage compared with very dry soil.
But irrigation timing must be appropriate.
Excess moisture can increase:
- waterlogging
- root disease
- nutrient loss.

## 68. Windbreaks
Windbreaks can reduce cold-air movement and convective heat loss in some situations.
But their effectiveness depends strongly on:
- frost type
- wind conditions
- landscape
- shelterbelt design.

## 69. Heating
For high-value crops, heating may be used during severe cold or frost events.
Methods can include:
- approved orchard heating systems
- greenhouse heating
- localized heating.
The goal is to maintain tissues above damaging temperatures.

70. Cold Stress and Crop Stage
Growth stage
Possible cold effects
Seed
Slow metabolism/germination
Germination
Delayed emergence
Seedling
Poor vigor and growth

Growth stage
Possible cold effects
Vegetative
Reduced leaf/root/stem growth
Flower initiation Delayed or altered flowering
Flowering
Pollen/reproductive injury
Pollination
Poor pollen function
Fertilization
Reduced reproductive success
Grain/fruit set
Abortion/reduced set
Grain filling
Reduced filling rate
Maturity
Delayed maturity
Senescence
Altered or stress-induced senescence

71. Cold Stress and Yield
Cold can reduce yield through several pathways:
Cold→ Germination↓Growth↓Photosynthesis↓Flowering disruptedFertility↓Grain/fruit set↓Filling↓ xed      \\  
 \\   \\  \\  
 \\   \\    
Therefore:
Cold stress→yield↓    

72. Cold Stress and Quality
Cold can affect:
- grain quality
- seed quality
- fruit quality
- oil accumulation
- starch accumulation
- protein composition
- maturity timing.
The exact response is crop-specific.

73. Measuring Cold Stress
Cold stress can be assessed through:
- air temperature
- soil temperature
- leaf temperature
- canopy temperature
- survival percentage
- growth rate
- electrolyte leakage
- chlorophyll fluorescence
- photosynthetic rate
- membrane stability
- pollen viability
- yield.

74. Cold Injury Measurement
A simple survival measure is:
Survival (%)=Plants survivingPlants exposed×100  =         100  
A conceptual injury measure is:
Injury (%)=Damaged tissueTotal tissue×100  =   
     100  
Actual experimental methods vary by crop and research protocol.

75. Cold Stress and Electrolyte Leakage
Damaged membranes release ions.
Therefore electrolyte leakage can be used as an indicator of membrane injury.
Conceptually:

Cold injury↑→membrane leakage↑     

76. Cold Stress and Canopy Temperature
Canopy temperature can differ from air temperature.
Under cold conditions, the difference depends on:
- radiation
- wind
- humidity
- canopy structure
- plant water status.
Therefore:
Tcanopy≠TairT     T    
and both can be useful in crop stress monitoring.

77. Important Cold-Stress Mechanisms
The major mechanisms can be grouped into four categories:

## 1. Metabolic
Enzyme activity↓  ## 2. Membrane
Membrane rigidity↑  ## 3. Oxidative
ROS↑ROS ## 4. Freezing
Ice formation→cell dehydration   78. Cold Stress vs Heat Stress

79. Cold Stress vs Chilling Stress vs Frost
Term

Cold stress

The terminology can vary somewhat between disciplines, but this framework is useful for crop science.

80. Key Formulas

81. Complete Cold-Stress Mechanism
The entire process can be summarized as:
Low temperature→enzyme activity↓+membrane rigidity↑+root activity↓+photosynthesis↓→metabolic
imbalance    +
  +   +  
   
Then:
Metabolic imbalance→ROS↑→cellular damage→growth/reproductive impairment   ROS    
Under freezing:
Low temperature→ice formation→cell dehydration→membrane damage→tissue death       

82. Cold Stress Management Framework
The complete agricultural approach is:
Forecast→Risk assessment→Crop/calendar selection→Avoidance→Protection→Post-stress assessmen
t        

Before cold
- select appropriate cultivar
- adjust sowing date
- monitor weather
- maintain suitable soil moisture
- use protective structures where appropriate.

During cold
- protect sensitive crops

- use appropriate covering/heating/irrigation methods when justified
- minimize unnecessary disturbance.

After cold
- assess tissue injury
- evaluate stand establishment
- monitor recovery
- avoid excessive fertilizer or irrigation unless agronomically justified.

83. Easy Memory Framework
Remember COLD as:

C — Cellular membranes
Cold changes membrane properties.

O — Oxygen/oxidative balance
Cold can disturb metabolism and increase ROS.

L — Low metabolic activity
Enzymes, growth and nutrient uptake slow.

D — Development and reproduction
Flowering, pollen, fertilization, grain filling and maturity can be affected.

84. Exam-Ready Definition
Cold stress is the physiological and biochemical disturbance caused by exposure of a plant to
temperatures below its optimal range for sufficient intensity and duration. It can reduce enzyme
activity, alter membrane fluidity, inhibit photosynthesis and respiration, reduce root and nutrient
uptake, increase oxidative stress, impair flowering and pollen fertility, delay development, and
under freezing conditions cause ice formation, cellular dehydration and tissue death.

85. Final Conceptual Takeaway
The central idea is:

Cold Stress=Low-temperature exposure that exceeds the plant’s ability to maintain normal function ed    =    
Its major pathway is:
Cold→Metabolism↓+Membrane dysfunction+Photosynthesis↓+Root activity↓+ROS↑    +   +  
 +   +    
leading to:
Growth↓+Reproductive success↓+Development delayed+Yield/quality↓ 
 +   +   +
   
And in severe freezing conditions:
Cold→Ice formation→Cell dehydration→Membrane damage→Tissue death 
     
The key distinction to remember is:
Cold stress is the broad category   
Chilling = harmful non-freezing cold   
Frost/freezing = cold severe enough to cause freezing-related injury   
While vernalization is different: it is a specific, regulated cold exposure that can promote flowering in
suitable plants rather than being inherently a stress response.

Drought
## 1. Introduction
Drought is a prolonged period during which water availability is insufficient to meet the normal water
requirements of crops, ecosystems, people, or other systems.
In agriculture, drought is one of the most important abiotic stresses because water is required for:
- seed germination
- cell expansion
- photosynthesis
- nutrient transport
- transpiration
- flowering

- pollination
- fertilization
- fruit/grain development
- seed filling
- biomass production.
The basic relationship is:
Water availability↓→plant water status↓→physiological stress→growth/yield↓        
However, drought is not simply "no rain." A crop can experience drought because of insufficient
rainfall, high evaporative demand, poor soil-water storage, limited irrigation, or a combination of these
factors.

2. Definition of Drought
General definition
Drought is a sustained period of abnormally low water availability relative to the normal
requirements of a particular system.

Agricultural definition
Agricultural drought occurs when available soil water becomes insufficient to meet crop water
requirements, resulting in reduced crop growth, development, yield, or quality.
A useful conceptual expression is:
Drought stress=f(water supply,water demand,soil storage,crop demand,duration)  = f( , , , ,
 )  

3. Drought vs Dry Weather
These terms should not be confused.

Dry weather
Simply means relatively little rainfall or low atmospheric moisture.

Drought
Means water availability is insufficient relative to demand for a sustained period.

Therefore:
Low rainfall≠automatically agricultural drought   
For example, a short dry period may not cause drought if the soil contains enough stored water.

4. Drought vs Heat Stress
Drought and heat stress are different, but frequently occur together.

Drought
Primarily involves insufficient water availability.

Heat stress
Primarily involves excessive temperature.
They interact strongly:
Heat→ET↑→soil water depletion↑→drought stress   ET     
and:
Drought→transpiration↓→leaf cooling↓→Tleaf↑→heat stress       T    
   
This is called compound heat–drought stress.

5. Types of Drought
The major types are:
1. Meteorological drought
2. Agricultural drought
3. Hydrological drought
4. Socioeconomic drought
5. Ecological drought.

6. Meteorological Drought
Meteorological drought occurs when precipitation is substantially below the normal level for a region
and time period.
It is generally the earliest form of drought to develop.
Rainfall↓→meteorological drought    
The definition depends on:
- location
- season
- historical climate
- duration.

7. Agricultural Drought
Agricultural drought occurs when available water is insufficient for crop requirements.
It can result from:
- low rainfall
- long dry spells
- poor soil-water storage
- high evapotranspiration
- inadequate irrigation.
The key relationship is:
Available soil water<crop water requirement <   

8. Hydrological Drought
Hydrological drought involves reduced water availability in:
- rivers
- reservoirs
- lakes

- groundwater
- streams.
It often develops after prolonged meteorological drought.
Rainfall deficit→soil-water deficit→stream/reservoir/groundwater decline     

9. Socioeconomic Drought
Socioeconomic drought occurs when water scarcity begins to affect:
- food supply
- livelihoods
- industry
- energy
- water services
- economic activity.

10. Ecological Drought
Ecological drought refers to conditions where water deficits cause significant stress to ecosystems and
ecological functions.
It can affect:
- vegetation
- wetlands
- wildlife
- aquatic ecosystems
- soil biological processes.

11. Drought Is a Water-Balance Problem
A crop field can be viewed through a simplified soil-water balance:
ΔS=P+I−R−D−ET 
where:

- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage
- PP = precipitation
- II = irrigation
- RR = runoff
- DD = drainage
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
Drought occurs when water losses and demand exceed available water inputs and storage over time.

12. Soil Water
Plants obtain most of their water from the soil.
Important soil-water states include:

Saturation
Most soil pores contain water.

Field capacity
Approximate water content after excess gravitational water has drained.

Permanent wilting point
Soil-water condition at which plants can no longer extract enough water to recover from wilting.

Available water
Approximately:
AW≈FC−PWP 
The exact values depend on soil type and measurement method.

13. Plant-Available Water
The crop can extract only part of the water stored in the soil.
Conceptually:
Available water=water accessible to roots  =    
When soil water declines:

Soil water↓→plant water potential↓    14. Soil Water Potential
Water moves according to gradients in water potential.
A simplified concept is:
Soil→Root→Xylem→Leaf→Atmosphere       
under suitable conditions.
As drought develops, the soil and plant water potentials become increasingly negative.

15. Plant Water Potential
Plant water status can be represented as:
Ψw 
where Ψw  w is water potential.
Under drought:
Ψw↓ 
meaning water becomes less energetically available.
Water potential can be conceptually divided into components such as:
Ψw=Ψs+Ψp+Ψm+Ψg 
where:
- Ψs  s = solute/osmotic potential
- Ψp  p = pressure potential
- Ψm  m = matric potential
- Ψg  g = gravitational potential.

16. Drought Stress Mechanism
The major sequence is:

Rainfall/soil water↓→soil water potential↓→root water uptake↓→plant water status↓         
Then:
Water deficit→stomatal closure→CO2 uptake↓→photosynthesis↓ 
  CO 2     

17. Stomatal Closure
Stomata regulate:
- CO₂ entry
- water-vapor loss.
During drought:
Stomatal conductance↓   
This helps conserve water but reduces CO₂ entry.
Therefore:
Drought→stomatal closure→photosynthesis↓      

18. ABA and Drought
Abscisic acid (ABA) is a major hormone involved in drought responses.
Water deficit can increase ABA signaling.
The simplified pathway is:
Soil drying→ABA signaling→stomatal closure→water loss↓       
ABA also affects:
- gene expression
- root growth
- seed dormancy
- stress responses.

19. Drought and Transpiration
Transpiration is water loss from plant surfaces, mainly through stomata.
ET=E+T 
where:
- EE = evaporation
- TT = transpiration.
Under drought, plants often reduce transpiration by closing stomata.

20. Drought and Evapotranspiration
Evapotranspiration represents:
ET=Evaporation+Transpiration +   
When atmospheric demand is high:
ET↑ET and soil water can be depleted faster.
Thus drought severity depends not only on rainfall but also on atmospheric demand.

21. Drought and VPD
Vapor Pressure Deficit is:
VPD=es−ea 
High VPD means strong atmospheric drying demand.
Therefore:
High VPD→transpirational demand↑→water stress risk↑       

22. Drought and Root Growth
Roots are central to drought adaptation.
During moderate drought, some plants may increase root allocation to deeper soil layers.
Possible responses include:

- deeper roots
- greater root branching
- increased root-to-shoot ratio
- root elongation toward moist soil.
However, severe drought can eventually inhibit root growth.

23. Root-to-Shoot Ratio
A useful indicator is:
R:S=Root biomassShoot biomass        
Under some drought conditions:
R:S↑R:S because shoot growth is inhibited more strongly than root growth.
But this is not universal.

24. Deep Rooting
Deep roots can access water stored deeper in the soil profile.
Thus:
Deep roots→access to deeper water→greater drought avoidance     
This depends on:
- soil depth
- soil layers
- root architecture
- compaction
- water distribution.

25. Drought and Leaf Area
Plants may reduce leaf area under drought.
Mechanisms include:

- reduced leaf expansion
- leaf rolling
- leaf shedding
- premature senescence.
The purpose is often to reduce water loss.
Leaf area↓→transpirational surface↓     
But reduced leaf area also reduces photosynthesis.

26. Leaf Rolling
Leaf rolling reduces exposed leaf area.
It can therefore reduce water loss:
Leaf rolling→exposed surface↓→water loss↓       
However, severe rolling also reduces light interception and photosynthesis.

27. Drought and Photosynthesis
Drought reduces photosynthesis through multiple mechanisms.

Early stage
Stomatal closure limits CO₂ entry.

Severe/prolonged drought
It can additionally cause:
- chloroplast damage
- enzyme impairment
- oxidative stress
- reduced photosynthetic machinery.
Therefore:
Drought→photosynthesis↓     

28. Drought and Respiration
Respiration responses depend on:
- tissue
- severity
- duration
- temperature
- metabolic state.
Severe drought can disrupt normal respiration and energy metabolism.
Roots under severe drought may have reduced metabolic activity because of reduced growth and
substrate availability.

29. Drought and Carbon Balance
Crop biomass depends on the balance between carbon gain and losses.
Conceptually:
Net biomass≈photosynthetic carbon gain−respiratory/other losses   -    
Drought reduces photosynthetic carbon gain.
Therefore:
Drought→carbon assimilation↓→biomass↓       

30. Drought and Nutrient Uptake
Water is essential for:
- nutrient movement
- diffusion
- root activity
- mass flow.
Under drought:
Water availability↓→nutrient transport/uptake↓     

Nutrient deficiencies may therefore become more pronounced.

31. Drought and Nitrogen
Nitrogen uptake depends strongly on root activity and water movement.
Drought can reduce:
- nitrate movement
- root growth
- nitrogen uptake
- nitrogen assimilation.
Therefore:
Drought→N uptake↓   N   

32. Drought and Phosphorus
Phosphorus moves primarily through diffusion in soil.
Dry soil can reduce phosphorus mobility.
Therefore drought can cause:
Soil drying→P diffusion↓→P availability to roots↓   P   P   

33. Drought and Potassium
Potassium plays an important role in:
- osmotic regulation
- stomatal function
- enzyme activation.
Adequate potassium nutrition can support water regulation, but potassium fertilizer does not
automatically eliminate drought stress.

34. Drought and Flowering
Drought during flowering can be highly damaging.

It may cause:
- flower abortion
- reduced pollen viability
- reduced stigma receptivity
- poor fertilization
- reduced fruit/seed set.
Thus:
Drought during flowering→reproductive success↓     

35. Drought and Pollination
Water stress can reduce reproductive performance through:
- reduced flower production
- reduced pollen viability
- altered flowering synchrony
- reduced pollinator activity in some environments
- flower abortion.
Therefore:
Drought→pollination/fertilization success↓     

36. Drought and Fruit Set
In fruit crops:
Drought→flower/ovary stress→fruit set↓      
Severe water stress may result in fruit drop.

37. Drought and Grain Set
In cereals and grain crops, drought around flowering can reduce:
- spikelet fertility

- grain number
- kernel formation.
This can have a large effect because grain number is a major yield component.

38. Drought and Grain Filling
Drought during grain filling reduces:
- photosynthesis
- assimilate supply
- grain filling duration
- grain filling rate.
Thus:
Drought→grain filling↓→grain weight↓       

39. Drought and Seed Development
Water deficit can affect:
- embryo development
- endosperm development
- seed filling
- seed size
- seed quality.
Therefore:
Drought during seed development→seed weight/quality↓     

40. Drought and Maturity
Severe drought can accelerate senescence and sometimes shorten effective grain-filling periods.
Therefore:
Drought→premature senescence→filling duration↓      

However, drought responses differ among crops and developmental stages.

41. Drought and Senescence
Water deficit can accelerate leaf senescence.
The sequence can be:
Drought→water deficit→oxidative/hormonal signals→senescence      
Consequently:
Green leaf area↓→photosynthesis↓    42. Drought and Reactive Oxygen Species
Drought can disturb photosynthesis and electron transport.
This can increase:
ROS 
Excess ROS can damage:
- membranes
- proteins
- pigments
- DNA.
Therefore:
Drought→ROS↑→oxidative stress   ROS   

43. Antioxidant Defense
Plants activate antioxidant systems such as:
- superoxide dismutase
- catalase
- ascorbate peroxidase
- glutathione systems.

Non-enzymatic antioxidants include:
- ascorbate
- glutathione
- carotenoids
- tocopherols.

44. Osmotic Adjustment
One major drought adaptation is osmotic adjustment.
Plants accumulate compatible solutes such as:
- proline
- soluble sugars
- glycine betaine
- certain amino acids.
This can help maintain cell turgor.
Conceptually:
Drought→osmolyte accumulation→osmotic adjustment→water retention 
    

45. Turgor and Cell Expansion
Cell expansion depends strongly on turgor pressure.
Under drought:
Cell water↓→turgor↓    Therefore:
Drought→cell expansion↓→leaf/stem growth↓       
This is often one of the earliest visible effects of water deficit.

## 46. Wilting
Wilting occurs when cells lose enough water and turgor that leaves and other tissues lose their normal
rigidity.
The sequence is:
Water deficit→turgor↓→wilting      
Wilting can be temporary or permanent depending on severity and duration.

47. Temporary vs Permanent Wilting
Temporary wilting
Plant wilts during periods of high evaporative demand but recovers when water demand decreases or
water becomes available.

Permanent wilting
Plant cannot recover because soil water is too low for sufficient uptake.
The permanent wilting point is a soil-water concept associated with this condition.

48. Drought Resistance
"Drought resistance" is a broad term covering the ability of plants to maintain survival and/or
productivity under water limitation.
It can involve:
- drought escape
- drought avoidance
- drought tolerance
- recovery capacity.

49. Drought Escape
A plant completes its life cycle before severe drought becomes intense.
For example:

Early flowering→early maturity→drought avoided     
This is particularly useful in environments with predictable seasonal drought.

50. Drought Avoidance
The plant maintains relatively favorable water status despite dry conditions.
Mechanisms include:
- deep roots
- reduced water loss
- early stomatal regulation
- efficient water uptake
- leaf rolling.

51. Drought Tolerance
The plant maintains physiological function despite low tissue water status.
Mechanisms can include:
- osmotic adjustment
- antioxidant protection
- membrane stability
- protective proteins.

52. Drought Recovery
Some plants can recover after water becomes available.
Recovery depends on:
- severity
- duration
- tissue damage
- crop stage
- genotype.

Thus:
Drought resistance=escape+avoidance+tolerance+recovery  =
  +   +   +    
These mechanisms may overlap.

53. Drought and Water-Use Efficiency
Water-use efficiency (WUE) can be expressed in different ways.
A broad production definition is:
WUE=Biomass or yieldWater used        
At the physiological level, instantaneous WUE may be related to:
WUEi≈AE  E   
where:
- AA = photosynthetic CO₂ assimilation
- EE = transpiration.
Definitions must always specify the time scale and measurement method.

54. Water Productivity
Water productivity is commonly expressed as:
WP=Crop yieldWater consumed or supplied        
The exact definition varies according to whether the denominator is irrigation water,
evapotranspiration, or total water input.

55. Drought and Rainfall Distribution
Drought is strongly influenced by rainfall distribution, not just total annual rainfall.
For example, the same total rainfall can produce different outcomes:

Well distributed
Rainfall→regular soil-water replenishment   Poorly distributed
Long dry spell→soil-water depletion→crop stress    Therefore:
Rainfall amount + timing + intensity + distribution   
determine crop water availability.

56. Dry Spell
A dry spell is a period with little or no effective rainfall.
Its agricultural importance depends on:
- duration
- crop stage
- soil water storage
- root depth
- atmospheric demand.
A dry spell during seedling establishment may have a very different effect from one during maturity.

57. Drought During Different Crop Stages
Stage

Major drought effects
Seed
Poor germination
Germination
Delayed/failed emergence
Seedling
Poor establishment
Vegetative
Reduced leaf/root growth
Flower initiation
Altered development
Flowering
Flower abortion
Pollination
Reduced reproductive success
Fertilization
Poor seed/fruit set
Grain/fruit formation Reduced number
Grain filling
Reduced weight
Maturity
Premature senescence
Harvest
Reduced yield/quality

58. Critical Growth Periods
Every crop has periods when water stress causes disproportionately large yield losses.
Often important periods include:
- flowering
- pollination
- fertilization
- early fruit/seed formation
- grain filling.
But exact critical periods differ by crop.

59. Drought and Yield Components
A simplified yield relationship is:
Yield≈Plants/area×Reproductive units/plant×Weight/unit      
Drought can reduce:
- plant establishment
- reproductive units
- grain/fruit/seed weight.
For cereals:
Grain yield≈Plants/area×Grains/plant×Grain weight      

60. Drought and Soil Type
Soil texture strongly affects drought risk.

Sandy soil
- rapid drainage
- low water-holding capacity
- drought can develop quickly.

Clay soil
- high total water-holding capacity
- but some water may be strongly held
- drainage can be slow.

Loam
Often provides a favorable balance of:
- water storage
- aeration
- drainage.

61. Soil Organic Matter and Drought
Organic matter can improve:
- soil structure
- water-holding capacity
- infiltration
- aggregation
- biological activity.
Therefore:
Organic matter↑→soil-water storage potential↑     
The magnitude depends on soil type and management.

62. Mulching for Drought Management
Mulching reduces evaporation from soil.
Mulch→soil evaporation↓→soil moisture conserved      
It can also moderate soil temperature and suppress weeds.

63. Conservation Agriculture
Practices that can improve drought resilience include:
- residue retention
- reduced soil disturbance
- cover crops
- crop rotation
- mulching
- improved soil organic matter
- water harvesting.
These can improve soil-water infiltration and storage, although results are site-specific.

64. Rainwater Harvesting
Rainwater harvesting captures water for later use.
Examples include:
- farm ponds
- check dams
- contour bunds
- field bunding
- storage structures.
The basic concept is:
Rainfall→capture/storage→later crop use     

65. Irrigation Management
Efficient irrigation can reduce drought risk.
Methods include:
- drip irrigation
- sprinkler irrigation
- furrow irrigation

- deficit irrigation
- precision irrigation.
The objective is to supply water according to crop demand and soil-water status.

66. Drip Irrigation
Drip irrigation delivers water close to the root zone.
Potential benefits include:
- reduced evaporation
- reduced runoff
- targeted application
- improved water-use efficiency.
But performance depends on:
- system design
- maintenance
- filtration
- pressure
- soil properties
- scheduling.

67. Deficit Irrigation
Deficit irrigation deliberately applies less than full crop water requirement during selected periods.
The goal is to:
Save water while limiting yield loss   
It requires knowledge of crop sensitivity at different stages.

68. Drought-Tolerant Varieties
Breeding can target traits such as:
- deep rooting
- efficient water use

- osmotic adjustment
- heat tolerance
- stable reproduction
- stay-green traits
- rapid recovery.
No single trait guarantees drought tolerance under all environments.

69. Stay-Green Trait
Some genotypes maintain green leaves longer during drought or terminal stress.
This can help sustain:
Photosynthesis 
during grain filling.
However, "stay-green" can be:
- functional
- cosmetic.
Functional stay-green is more agriculturally useful because photosynthetic activity remains functional.

70. Drought and Heat Together
This deserves special emphasis.
Under combined drought and heat:
Drought→stomatal closure→transpiration↓→leaf cooling↓        
while:
Heat→evaporative demand↑→water demand↑       
Thus:
Heat + drought→stronger physiological stress    

71. Drought and Wind
Wind can increase water loss by removing humid air around leaves.
Therefore:
Drought+strong dry wind→water loss↑  +      
This can accelerate soil-water depletion and leaf dehydration.

72. Drought and Humidity
Low relative humidity generally increases VPD.
Thus:
RH↓→VPD↑→atmospheric drying demand↑   
When combined with drought:
Low soil water+high VPD→high plant water stress  +  
  

73. Drought and Temperature
Temperature affects drought severity because it influences atmospheric water demand.
Higher temperature generally increases saturation vapor pressure.
Therefore:
T↑→VPD↑→ET demand↑T  VPD  ET  when other conditions remain similar.

74. Drought and Climate Change
Climate change can affect drought through:
- changes in rainfall
- higher temperatures
- increased evaporative demand
- changing rainfall distribution

- longer or more frequent dry periods in some regions
- compound heat-drought events.
The exact direction and magnitude vary by location and season.

75. Drought Indices
Drought can be monitored using indices such as:

Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI)
Based primarily on precipitation anomalies over a selected time scale.

Standardized Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI)
Incorporates precipitation and atmospheric evaporative demand.

Palmer Drought Severity Index (PDSI)
Uses a water-balance framework.

Soil-moisture indicators
Measure or estimate available soil water.

Vegetation indices
Satellite-based indicators such as NDVI can detect vegetation stress.
Each index measures a somewhat different aspect of drought.

76. Drought Monitoring
Modern drought monitoring combines:
- rainfall observations
- soil moisture
- temperature
- ET
- remote sensing
- crop models
- groundwater data
- reservoir levels.

The basic principle is:
Weather+Soil+Plant+Hydrology→Drought assessment  +   +
  +     

77. Drought and Remote Sensing
Satellite data can monitor:
- vegetation greenness
- canopy temperature
- vegetation stress
- land-surface moisture indicators.
Examples include:
## • Ndvi
- EVI
- thermal infrared observations.
These can help detect spatial patterns of crop stress.

78. Drought and Crop Phenology
Drought can change:
- flowering time
- maturity time
- senescence
- leaf emergence
- grain filling.
Therefore:
Water availability+temperature+photoperiod+genotype→crop phenology  +   +   +     

79. Drought and Crop Life Cycle
The complete pathway is:

Seed→Germination→Seedling→Vegetative growth→Flowering→Pollination→Fruit/Grain formation
→Filling→Maturity           
Drought can affect every stage, but its consequences vary.

80. Important Drought Adaptation Strategies
Genetic
- drought-tolerant varieties
- deep rooting
- early maturity
- stable reproduction.

Agronomic
- appropriate sowing time
- mulching
- conservation tillage
- crop rotation
- residue management
- optimized plant population.

Water management
- irrigation scheduling
- drip irrigation
- rainwater harvesting
- farm ponds
- efficient irrigation.

Soil management
- organic matter
- reduced erosion
- improved aggregation
- improved water infiltration.

81. Drought Management Framework
A complete strategy is:
Forecast→Risk assessment→Water conservation→Efficient water use→Crop adaptation→Recovery - xed          

Before drought
- choose suitable crops
- select drought-tolerant cultivars
- conserve soil moisture
- improve organic matter
- plan irrigation
- harvest rainwater.

During drought
- prioritize critical growth stages
- irrigate efficiently
- control weeds
- minimize unnecessary water loss
- monitor soil moisture and crop stress.

After drought
- assess crop recovery
- manage nutrition appropriately
- control secondary pests/diseases
- evaluate whether replanting is required.

82. Key Drought Indicators in Plants
Common visible signs include:
- wilting
- leaf rolling

- reduced leaf expansion
- darker or dull foliage
- premature senescence
- leaf shedding
- reduced flowering
- flower/fruit abortion
- poor grain filling
- reduced plant height.
However, visual symptoms often appear after physiological drought stress has already begun.

83. Physiological Indicators
More quantitative indicators include:
- leaf water potential
- relative water content
- stomatal conductance
- photosynthetic rate
- transpiration
- canopy temperature
- chlorophyll fluorescence
- electrolyte leakage
- root-to-shoot ratio.

84. Relative Water Content
A common plant water-status measurement is relative water content (RWC):
RWC(%)=FW−DWTW−DW×100   TW-DW   100  
where:
- FWFW = fresh weight
- TWTW = fully turgid weight
- DWDW = dry weight.

Lower RWC generally indicates greater tissue dehydration.

85. Crop Water Requirement
Crop water requirement is the water needed to meet evapotranspiration and other crop needs under
specified conditions.
A common relationship is:
ETc=KcET0 
where:
- ETcET c = crop evapotranspiration
- KcK c = crop coefficient
- ET0ET 0 = reference evapotranspiration.
Drought occurs when effective water supply is insufficient relative to crop demand and available soil
storage.

86. Irrigation Requirement
A simplified conceptual relationship is:
IR≈ETc−Peffective    
but actual irrigation scheduling also considers:
- soil-water storage
- runoff
- drainage
- irrigation efficiency
- allowable depletion
- root-zone depth.

87. Drought and Harvest
Drought before harvest can cause:
- reduced yield
- smaller grain/fruit

- poor seed quality
- premature maturity
- increased harvesting difficulty in some crops.
Therefore drought can influence both production and final product quality.

88. Drought and Food Security
Agricultural drought can reduce:
- crop production
- livestock feed
- farm income
- food availability.
Therefore drought management is important for:
Farm productivity+livelihood security+food security  +
  +
