91. Complete Drought Mechanism
The entire process can be represented as:
Rainfall/irrigation↓→soil water↓→root water uptake↓→Ψw↓→stomatal closure         w   
Then:
Stomatal closure→CO2 entry↓→photosynthesis↓→biomass accumulation↓   CO 2       
Meanwhile:
Drought→ROS↑+osmotic stress+nutrient uptake↓→cellular stress    +   +     
During reproduction:
Drought→flower/pollen stress→fertilization↓→fruit/seed set↓        

Finally:
Drought→grain/fruit filling↓→yield and quality↓       

92. Most Important Concept
Drought is not simply a rainfall problem.
A better representation is:
Drought stress=Water supply−Water demand−available storage effects  =
  -   -    
More conceptually:
Rainfall+Irrigation+Soil storage−Runoff−Drainage−Evapotranspiration=Change in soil water  +   +   -   -     =    
When available water becomes insufficient:
Plant water stress begins   

93. Exam-Ready Definition
Drought is a prolonged period of insufficient water availability relative to the requirements of
crops or ecosystems. Agricultural drought occurs when soil-water availability becomes
inadequate to satisfy crop water demand, causing reduced cell expansion, stomatal closure,
reduced photosynthesis, impaired nutrient uptake, altered flowering and reproduction,
accelerated senescence, reduced grain/fruit filling, and ultimately reduced crop yield and quality.

94. Final Takeaway
Remember drought through the chain:
DROUGHT→Soil water↓→Plant water status↓→Stomatal closure→CO2 uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓→
Growth/reproduction↓→Yield↓        CO 2         
The four major plant strategies are:
Drought escape+Drought avoidance+Drought tolerance+Recovery  +
  +   +    

And the most important agricultural principle is:
Drought severity depends not only on how little rain occurs, but on the balance
between water supply, soil-water storage, atmospheric demand, root access to water,
crop water requirement, duration of the deficit, and the developmental stage at which
the crop experiences it.

Excess Rainfall
## 1. Introduction
Excess rainfall occurs when the amount or intensity of rainfall exceeds the soil's infiltration, storage,
drainage, or crop-use capacity, causing problems such as waterlogging, flooding, runoff, erosion,
nutrient loss, root oxygen deficiency, disease, lodging, and crop damage.
The important point is:
More rainfall≠more crop productivity   
Rainfall is beneficial when it supplies water within the crop's requirement. Beyond the soil–crop
system's capacity, additional rainfall can become harmful.
A simplified relationship is:
Excess rainfall→Runoff/ponding/waterlogging→Root-zone oxygen deficiency→Physiological stress→
Growth and yield loss       

## 2. Definition
Excess rainfall is rainfall occurring in an amount, intensity, duration, or frequency greater than
the capacity of the soil, drainage system, or crop ecosystem to safely absorb, store, drain, or
utilize it.
It can result from:
- very high rainfall amount
- intense rainfall over a short period
- prolonged rainfall
- poorly drained soils
- high water table
- low infiltration
- inadequate drainage

- low-lying fields
- flooding.

3. Excess Rainfall vs Heavy Rainfall
These are related but not identical.

Heavy rainfall
Refers primarily to high rainfall intensity or amount over a particular period.

Excess rainfall
Refers to rainfall that becomes excess relative to the capacity of the soil, drainage system, or crop.
For example:
A sandy soil may absorb a particular rainfall event without serious ponding, while the same event on a
compacted clay soil may cause waterlogging.
Therefore:
Effect of rainfall=f(amount,intensity,duration,soil,topography,drainage,crop)  = f( , , , , ,
 , )  

4. Rainfall Water Balance
A simplified field water balance is:
ΔS=P+I−R−D−ET 
where:
- ΔS S = change in soil-water storage
- PP = precipitation/rainfall
- II = irrigation
- RR = runoff
- DD = drainage/deep percolation
- ETET = evapotranspiration.
When rainfall is greater than the soil–crop system can accommodate:
P>infiltration/storage/drainage capacity   
the excess water may become:

- surface runoff
- standing water
- ponding
- waterlogging
- flooding
- deep drainage.

5. Main Forms of Excess Rainfall
Excess rainfall can affect agricultural land through several pathways.

1. Surface ponding
Water accumulates on the soil surface.

## 2. Waterlogging
The soil becomes saturated for sufficiently long that root-zone aeration is seriously reduced.

## 3. Flooding
Water covers agricultural land, sometimes deeply and for extended periods.

## 4. Runoff
Water flows across the soil surface instead of infiltrating.

5. Deep drainage
Water moves below the effective root zone.

## 6. Erosion
Fast-moving water removes soil particles.

6. Rainfall Intensity
Rainfall intensity is:
I=Pt  t   
where:
- II = rainfall intensity
- PP = rainfall amount

- tt = duration.
For example, 60 mm falling in one hour is very different from 60 mm spread over several days.
High intensity can cause:
Rainfall intensity>infiltration capacity→runoff  >     

## 7. Infiltration
Infiltration is the movement of water from the soil surface into the soil.
When rainfall intensity is greater than infiltration capacity:
Irain>finfiltration→surface runoff  >f       
Factors affecting infiltration include:
- soil texture
- soil structure
- compaction
- organic matter
- soil moisture
- crusting
- slope
- rainfall intensity
- vegetation cover.

## 8. Waterlogging
Waterlogging occurs when excess water saturates the soil and reduces the availability of oxygen to
plant roots.
This is one of the most important agricultural consequences of excess rainfall.
The basic sequence is:
Excess rainfall→soil saturation→O2↓→root respiration↓→root stress 
  O 2     

9. Why Oxygen Becomes Limited
Air-filled soil pores are important for oxygen diffusion.
When water fills these pores:
Air-filled porosity↓→O2 diffusion↓   O 2   
Oxygen diffuses through water much more slowly than through air.
Therefore saturated soil can quickly become oxygen-deficient.

10. Root Respiration Under Waterlogging
Roots require oxygen for aerobic respiration.
Under waterlogging:
O2↓→aerobic respiration↓   
Plants may increase anaerobic metabolism/fermentation.
For example:
Glucose→ethanol+CO2  +  2
in many plant tissues under oxygen limitation.
However, prolonged anaerobic metabolism cannot fully substitute for normal aerobic respiration.

11. Root Damage
Waterlogged roots may experience:
- reduced energy production
- impaired nutrient uptake
- reduced root growth
- membrane damage
- root death
- reduced root hydraulic conductivity.
Consequently:
Healthy root system↓→water/nutrient uptake↓     

An interesting consequence is that a plant can have too much water around its roots but still
experience physiological water stress, because damaged roots cannot supply water efficiently.

12. Waterlogging and Nutrient Uptake
Excess rainfall can reduce nutrient availability and uptake.
Important mechanisms include:
- leaching
- runoff
- denitrification
- reduced root activity
- altered nutrient chemistry.
Thus:
Excess rainfall→nutrient losses + root dysfunction→nutrient deficiency risk     

13. Nitrogen Loss
Nitrogen is particularly vulnerable.

Leaching
Nitrate can move below the root zone with drainage.
NO3−→deep drainage→root-zone N loss    

Denitrification
Under oxygen-limited conditions, microorganisms can convert nitrate into gaseous nitrogen forms.
Conceptually:
NO3−→N2O→N2NO 3 -  N 2O  N 2
This can reduce plant-available nitrogen.

14. Phosphorus Loss
Excess rainfall can cause phosphorus loss through:

- erosion
- runoff
- sediment movement.
Phosphorus is often strongly associated with soil particles.
Therefore:
Runoff + erosion→P loss   P   

15. Potassium Loss
Potassium can be lost through:
- leaching
- runoff
- erosion,
with the extent depending strongly on soil type and rainfall conditions.

16. Soil Erosion
High-intensity rainfall can detach soil particles.
The sequence is:
Raindrop impact→soil particle detachment→runoff→soil transport→erosion       
Forms include:
- splash erosion
- sheet erosion
- rill erosion
- gully erosion.

17. Raindrop Impact
Individual raindrops can strike bare soil with considerable energy.
This can:

- break soil aggregates
- detach particles
- create surface crusts.
Therefore crop residues and vegetation cover are important for protecting soil.

18. Soil Crusting
Repeated intense rainfall can break down surface aggregates.
Fine particles can block soil pores and form a crust.
Consequences include:
Crusting→infiltration↓→runoff↑       
It can also interfere with seedling emergence.

19. Excess Rainfall and Germination
Moderate water availability is essential for germination, but excessive water can be harmful.
The problem is often not simply "too much water" but lack of oxygen.
Excess water→O2↓→seed respiration↓→germination/emergence problems   O 2     

20. Seedling Damage
Young seedlings are particularly vulnerable.
Excess rainfall can cause:
- waterlogging
- root damage
- poor anchorage
- nutrient deficiency
- damping-off
- soil crusting
- physical burial

- lodging.

21. Excess Rainfall During Vegetative Growth
During vegetative growth, excessive rainfall can cause:
- reduced root growth
- reduced nutrient uptake
- yellowing
- weak stems
- excessive vegetative growth in some situations
- disease development
- lodging.
The actual response depends on crop and soil.

22. Excess Rainfall During Flowering
Rainfall during flowering can interfere with:
- pollen release
- pollen movement
- pollinator activity
- stigma receptivity
- flower retention.
Heavy rain can physically damage flowers.
Therefore:
Excess rainfall during flowering→pollination success↓ 
   

23. Excess Rainfall and Fertilization
Poor pollination can subsequently reduce fertilization.
The chain is:

Heavy rain→pollination problems→fertilization↓→seed/fruit set↓ 
      
This effect is crop- and weather-dependent.

24. Excess Rainfall During Fruit Formation
Excess moisture can increase:
- fruit cracking
- fruit drop
- fungal diseases
- poor fruit quality
- nutrient imbalance.
For example, rapid changes in fruit water status can contribute to cracking in susceptible fruits.

25. Excess Rainfall and Grain Formation
In cereals, excessive rainfall can affect:
- flowering
- pollination
- grain set
- grain filling
- lodging
- disease.

26. Excess Rainfall During Grain Filling
Excess rainfall can reduce crop performance through:
- low radiation/cloudiness
- root-zone oxygen deficiency
- nutrient losses
- disease

- lodging.
Therefore:
Excess rainfall→photosynthesis/plant function↓→grain filling↓ 
     

27. Excess Rainfall and Sunlight
Heavy rainfall often occurs with cloud cover.
Therefore radiation reaching the crop can decline:
Cloud cover↑→solar radiation↓→photosynthesis↓       
This is an important indirect effect.

28. Excess Rainfall and Photosynthesis
Excess rainfall can reduce photosynthesis through several pathways:
1. Cloud cover reduces light.
2. Waterlogging damages roots.
3. Nutrient uptake declines.
4. Stomatal behavior changes.
5. Disease can damage leaves.
Therefore:
Excess rainfall→multiple constraints→photosynthesis↓      

29. Excess Rainfall and Respiration
Waterlogging reduces oxygen availability to roots.
Thus:
Waterlogging→O2↓→aerobic root respiration↓   O 2    
Anaerobic metabolism may increase temporarily.

30. Excess Rainfall and Disease
High humidity and prolonged leaf wetness can strongly favor many plant diseases.
Conditions favorable to disease may include:
- high relative humidity
- leaf wetness
- warm temperatures
- poor air circulation
- dense canopy.
Therefore:
Excess rainfall→humidity/leaf wetness↑→disease risk↑       

31. Fungal and Oomycete Diseases
Wet conditions can favor many fungal and oomycete pathogens.
Potential problems include:
- leaf spots
- blights
- rots
- damping-off
- downy mildews
- root rots.
The exact pathogen depends on the crop and environment.

32. Excess Rainfall and Weeds
Rainfall can promote weed emergence and growth when temperature and other conditions are suitable.
Thus:
Rainfall + suitable temperature→weed emergence/growth↑     
This increases competition for:

- nutrients
- light
- water
- space.

33. Excess Rainfall and Pest Dynamics
Rain can have different effects on insects:
- wash insects from plants
- reduce some insect activity
- increase plant susceptibility through stress
- alter pest population dynamics.
Therefore excess rainfall does not universally increase every pest population.

34. Excess Rainfall and Lodging
Waterlogged soil can weaken root anchorage.
Heavy rain and wind can also mechanically push plants over.
Thus:
Waterlogged soil+strong wind→lodging risk↑  +  
   
Lodging is particularly important in cereals such as:
- wheat
- rice
- maize
- barley
- sorghum.

35. Root Lodging vs Stem Lodging
Root lodging
The root-soil system fails and the plant leans or falls.

Stem lodging
The stem bends or breaks.
Excess rainfall can increase root lodging risk by weakening soil anchorage, while wet conditions and
wind can contribute to stem lodging.

36. Excess Rainfall and Soil Structure
Repeated intense rainfall can cause:
- aggregate breakdown
- surface sealing
- compaction under machinery when soil is wet
- erosion
- nutrient movement.
Therefore soil management is crucial.

37. Soil Type and Excess Rainfall
Sandy soil
Usually:
- high infiltration
- rapid drainage
- lower water-holding capacity.
It may experience less prolonged waterlogging but can still suffer from runoff under intense rainfall or
from nutrient leaching.

Clay soil
Usually:
- slower infiltration
- high water-holding capacity
- greater waterlogging risk.

Loam
Often provides a balance between:

- infiltration
- storage
- drainage.

## 38. Topography
Topography strongly affects excess-water accumulation.

Low-lying areas
More likely to accumulate water.

Slopes
More likely to experience runoff and erosion.
Thus:
Slope→runoff/erosion    
while:
Depression→water accumulation    

## 39. Drainage
Drainage is the removal of excess water from the root zone.
Good drainage helps maintain:
- soil aeration
- root respiration
- nutrient uptake
- healthy root growth.
Poor drainage increases waterlogging risk.

40. Surface Drainage
Surface drainage removes standing water from the field.
Methods include:
- field channels
- shallow drains

- graded fields
- surface ditches.

41. Subsurface Drainage
Subsurface drainage removes excess water below the soil surface.
Systems may use:
- buried drainage pipes
- drainage tiles
- engineered drainage networks.
It is particularly relevant where high water tables or poorly drained soils persist.

42. Raised Beds
Raised beds can improve drainage around the root zone.
Conceptually:
Raised bed→root zone elevation↑→waterlogging risk↓       
Suitability depends on crop, soil and rainfall conditions.

43. Excess Rainfall and Nutrient Management
After heavy rainfall, fertilizer losses can occur.
Therefore simply applying more fertilizer is not always the correct response.
Management should consider:
- nutrient deficiency symptoms
- soil testing
- crop stage
- drainage
- fertilizer source
- previous application
- expected rainfall.

44. Nitrogen Management
Nitrogen losses after excess rainfall can occur through:
Leaching+Denitrification+Runoff  +   +    
Split nitrogen application can sometimes reduce loss risk compared with applying a large amount at
- nce, but the appropriate strategy is crop- and site-specific.

45. Excess Rainfall and Harvest
Excess rainfall near harvest can be particularly damaging.
Problems include:
- delayed harvesting
- wet grain
- lodging
- grain sprouting
- fungal growth
- fruit cracking
- soil compaction from machinery
- field losses.

46. Pre-Harvest Sprouting
In some cereal crops, prolonged wet conditions around maturity can cause grains to begin germinating
while still attached to the plant.
This can reduce:
- grain quality
- baking/processing quality
- seed quality.

47. Grain Moisture
Excess rainfall can maintain high grain moisture.

Consequently:
High grain moisture→drying requirement↑→storage risk↑ 
     
Proper drying is essential before safe storage.

48. Fruit Cracking
Some fruits are susceptible to cracking when water uptake and fruit expansion exceed the ability of the
fruit skin to accommodate the change.
Conceptually:
Rapid water uptake→fruit expansion→skin stress→cracking 
    
Genotype, nutrition, temperature and previous water status also matter.

49. Excess Rainfall and Post-Harvest Quality
Wet conditions can increase:
- fungal contamination
- microbial deterioration
- storage losses
- drying costs
- mechanical damage.
Therefore:
Harvest→rapid drying→safe storage     
becomes particularly important after wet weather.

50. Beneficial vs Harmful Rainfall
Moderate/appropriate rainfall
Recharges soil water
Supports germination
Supports photosynthesis
Supplies water for growth

Excess rainfall
Waterlogging
Poor oxygen availability
Root stress
Nutrient leaching

Moderate/appropriate rainfall
Excess rainfall
Supports nutrient transport
Denitrification
Supports yield
Runoff
Reduces irrigation need
Soil erosion
Supports crop development
Disease risk
Maintains water balance
Lodging
Supports reproductive development Harvest/post-harvest problems

51. Excess Rainfall and Crop Growth Stages
Crop stage

Major effects of excess rainfall
Seed
Rot, poor oxygen availability
Germination
Poor emergence
Seedling
Root damage, damping-off
Vegetative
Waterlogging, nutrient loss
Flower initiation
Developmental disturbance in sensitive crops
Flowering
Pollination problems
Fertilization
Reduced reproductive success
Fruit/grain formation Poor set, disease
Filling
Reduced assimilate accumulation
Maturity
Sprouting, quality deterioration
Harvest
Delays and field losses
Post-harvest
Drying and storage problems

52. Management of Excess Rainfall
Before heavy rainfall
Prepare by:
- maintaining drainage
- cleaning drainage channels
- avoiding unnecessary soil compaction
- maintaining soil cover
- using suitable varieties
- managing field slope
- creating water-harvesting/storage structures where appropriate.

During excess rainfall
Priorities include:
- removing standing water where feasible
- maintaining drainage channels
- avoiding machinery on saturated soils
- monitoring nutrient loss
- monitoring diseases
- protecting vulnerable crops.

After excess rainfall
Assess:
- root damage
- nutrient status
- disease
- lodging
- erosion
- crop stand
- soil compaction.
Then make management decisions based on actual crop and soil conditions.

53. Conservation Practices
Useful practices can include:

Crop residues
Reduce raindrop impact.

Cover crops
Protect soil from erosion.

Contour farming
Slows runoff on slopes.

Terracing
Reduces slope runoff and erosion.

Bunding
Helps manage runoff and retain soil/water.

Vegetative barriers
Slow water movement.

Improved soil structure
Can improve infiltration and reduce runoff.

54. Excess Rainfall and Rainwater Harvesting
Excess rainfall does not necessarily have to become a loss.
Where technically and environmentally appropriate:
Excess rainfall→capture→storage→future irrigation      
Examples:
- farm ponds
- reservoirs
- recharge structures
- check dams.

55. Important Relationship: Excess Rainfall and
Drought
These may appear opposite, but they are connected.
Excess rainfall can occur during one period and drought later.
For example:
Heavy rainfall→runoff+poor storage→water lost   +     
Later:

Dry period→soil moisture deficit→drought stress     
Therefore effective water management should focus on capturing and storing water, not simply
receiving rainfall.

56. Excess Rainfall and Climate
Climate variability can produce alternating:
Excess rainfall↔dry spells    
Therefore agriculture increasingly needs management strategies that handle both extremes.

57. Important Indicators of Excess Rainfall
Damage
Field indicators include:
- standing water
- saturated soil
- yellowing leaves
- wilting despite wet soil
- root browning/decay
- poor emergence
- leaf disease
- lodging
- nutrient deficiency symptoms
- erosion
- fruit cracking
- grain sprouting.
A particularly important diagnostic clue is:
Wilting in a waterlogged field does not necessarily mean the soil lacks water; the roots
may be unable to absorb water because of oxygen deficiency or root damage.

58. Excess Rainfall vs Waterlogging vs Flooding
Term
Meaning
Excess rainfall Rainfall exceeds the system's capacity to safely handle/use it
Ponding
Water temporarily accumulates on soil surface
Waterlogging Root zone remains saturated enough to restrict aeration
Flooding
Water covers land, often with deeper/longer inundation
Runoff
Water flows over soil surface
Drainage
Removal of excess water
They are related but not synonymous.

59. Key Formulas
Rainfall intensity
I=Pt  t   

Water balance
ΔS=P+I−R−D−ET 

Evapotranspiration
ET=E+T 

Available water
AW≈FC−PWP 

Crop evapotranspiration
ETc=KcET0 

Conceptual runoff condition
Irain>finfiltration⇒runoff  >f       

60. Complete Excess-Rainfall Mechanism
The complete agricultural chain is:
Excess rainfall   
↓
Runoff + ponding + soil saturation   
↓

Root-zone oxygen↓   
↓
Root respiration + root activity↓   
↓
Water/nutrient uptake↓   
↓
Photosynthesis + growth↓   
At the same time:
Excess rainfall→leaching + denitrification + erosion→nutrient losses 
   
and:
Excess rainfall→humidity + leaf wetness↑→disease risk↑       
Finally:
Excess rainfall→reproductive stress + lodging + harvest losses→yield/quality↓      

61. Exam-Ready Definition
Excess rainfall is rainfall occurring in an amount, intensity, or duration greater than the capacity
- f the soil, drainage system, and crop to absorb, store, drain, or utilize it safely. It can cause
runoff, waterlogging, flooding, soil erosion, nutrient losses, root-zone oxygen deficiency, disease
development, lodging, reproductive failure, harvest delays, and reduced crop yield and quality.

62. Final Takeaway
Remember excess rainfall through this chain:
EXCESS RAINFALL→Runoff/Waterlogging/Flooding→O2↓→Root function↓→Nutrient uptake↓→P
hotosynthesis/Growth↓→Reproduction/Filling↓→Yield & Quality↓    O 2            
The most important principle is:

Excess rainfall becomes a crop stress when water input exceeds the capacity of the soil
–plant system to infiltrate, store, drain, or utilize that water. Its impact depends on
rainfall intensity and duration, soil type, drainage, topography, crop species,
developmental stage, and the condition of the crop.

Flooding
## 1. Introduction
Flooding is the temporary or prolonged covering of agricultural land with water, or the excessive
accumulation and movement of water over land, to a degree that disrupts normal soil, plant, and
ecosystem functions.
In agriculture, flooding is an important water-related abiotic stress. It can damage crops through:
- oxygen deficiency
- root suffocation
- reduced respiration
- nutrient losses
- soil erosion
- physical damage
- disease
- lodging
- reduced photosynthesis
- reproductive failure
- delayed harvest
- crop death.
The basic mechanism is:
Flooding→Soil saturation/inundation→O2↓→Root dysfunction→Physiological stress→Growth/
yield↓    O 2      

## 2. Definition
General definition
Flooding is the temporary or prolonged inundation of normally dry land by water.

Agricultural definition
Agricultural flooding occurs when excess water covers crop land or saturates the crop's root zone
sufficiently to interfere with normal crop growth, development, and production.
Flooding can be caused by:
- excessive rainfall
- rivers overflowing
- reservoirs or dams overflowing
- storm surges
- cyclones
- poor drainage
- high groundwater
- irrigation failure
- snowmelt in some regions
- drainage-channel blockage.

3. Flooding vs Excess Rainfall
These concepts are related but not identical.

Excess rainfall
Rainfall exceeds the capacity of the soil–crop system to absorb, store, drain, or use it.

Flooding
Water actually inundates or covers land, often because of rainfall, river overflow, drainage failure,
storm surge, or other causes.
Therefore:
Excess rainfall≢flooding    
Excess rainfall can cause flooding, but flooding can also occur without extreme rainfall directly over
the field.

4. Flooding vs Waterlogging
This distinction is very important.

Feature
Waterlogging
Flooding
Main condition Root zone saturated
Land covered/inundated by water
Water location Mainly soil/root zone
Soil surface and/or above-ground plant parts
Main problem Oxygen deficiency in roots Oxygen deficiency + physical inundation
Depth
Often shallow/subsurface Can range from shallow to deep
Duration
Can be prolonged
Can be temporary or prolonged
Crop exposure Roots primarily affected
Roots, stems, leaves and entire plant may be affected
They can occur together:
Flooding→Waterlogging→O2↓    O 2  

5. Flooding vs Ponding
Ponding
Temporary accumulation of water on the soil surface, usually relatively shallow.

Flooding
Generally involves more extensive inundation and can involve substantial water depth and movement.
Thus:
Ponding is not necessarily flooding   

6. Types of Flooding
Flooding can be classified according to its source and behavior.

1. Riverine flooding
Occurs when rivers and streams overflow their banks.

2. Flash flooding
Rapid flooding caused by intense rainfall, sudden runoff, cloudbursts, or similar events.

3. Pluvial flooding
Occurs when rainfall overwhelms local drainage systems, even without river overflow.

4. Coastal flooding
Caused by:
- storm surge
- high tides

- cyclones
- sea-level-related events.

5. Urban/peri-urban flooding
Poor drainage and impervious surfaces can cause water to accumulate and subsequently affect nearby
agricultural areas.

6. Irrigation/drainage-induced flooding
Can occur because of excessive irrigation, blocked drains, or drainage-system failure.

7. Temporary vs Prolonged Flooding
Temporary flooding
Water remains for a short period and drains quickly.
Some crops may recover if damage is limited.

Prolonged flooding
Water remains for an extended period.
This greatly increases:
- oxygen deficiency
- root injury
- anaerobic metabolism
- nutrient loss
- disease
- plant death.
Therefore:
Flooding damage=f(depth,duration,temperature,crop,growth stage)  =
f( , , , , )  

8. Flood Depth
Flood depth strongly affects damage.

Shallow flooding
May primarily affect roots and lower stems.

Deep flooding
Can submerge:
- leaves
- flowers
- fruits
- stems
- entire seedlings.
Complete submergence is generally much more stressful for many crops because gas exchange and
photosynthesis are severely restricted.

9. Flood Duration
Duration is one of the most important factors.
A crop may tolerate:
hours 
- f waterlogging but suffer severe injury after:
several days 
- f prolonged inundation.
Therefore:
Flood severity≠depth alone    
Duration matters greatly.

10. Soil Oxygen Deficiency
The most important physiological problem caused by flooding is often oxygen deficiency.
When soil pores fill with water:
Air-filled pores↓→O2 diffusion↓   O 2   
Because oxygen diffuses much more slowly through water than air, roots rapidly experience hypoxia
- r, under severe conditions, anoxia.

Hypoxia
Low oxygen availability.

Anoxia
Extremely low or effectively absent oxygen.

11. Root Respiration
Roots require energy for:
- ion transport
- nutrient uptake
- maintenance
- growth
- cellular repair.
Normally:
Glucose+O2→CO2+H2O+ATP +O 2  CO 2+H 2O+ 
During flooding:
O2↓→aerobic respiration↓→ATP↓ 
  ATP  
This is one of the central causes of flooding injury.

12. Anaerobic Respiration
When oxygen becomes severely limited, plants can temporarily increase fermentation.
For example:
Glucose→pyruvate→ethanol+CO2    +  2
This allows limited ATP production through glycolysis.
But:
Anaerobic metabolism→much less efficient ATP production 
  
Prolonged dependence on this pathway can contribute to injury.

13. Root Energy Crisis
Flooding therefore creates:
O2↓→ATP↓→active transport↓→root function↓     
This affects nutrient uptake and ion balance.

14. Nutrient Uptake Under Flooding
Flooding can reduce uptake of:
- nitrogen
- phosphorus
- potassium
- sulfur
- micronutrients.
The reasons include:
- reduced root activity
- low ATP availability
- altered soil chemistry
- nutrient leaching
- denitrification
- reduced diffusion
- root damage.

15. Nitrogen Loss
Nitrogen is particularly vulnerable.

Leaching
Nitrate can move below the root zone.
NO3−→deep drainageNO 3 -  Denitrification
Under oxygen-limited conditions:
NO3−→N2O→N2NO 3 -  N 2O  N 2
Thus:
Flooding→N loss→N deficiency risk   N   N   

16. Soil Redox Potential
Flooding changes soil chemical conditions.
As oxygen is consumed:
Flooding→soil redox potential↓     
This can alter the chemical forms and availability of several elements.
Under strongly reducing conditions, elements such as:
- iron
- manganese
- sulfur
can undergo changes in oxidation state.
The consequences depend strongly on soil chemistry.

17. Toxic Compounds
Prolonged waterlogging can promote accumulation of potentially harmful compounds, including:
- reduced iron
- reduced manganese
- sulfide under strongly reducing conditions
- organic acids
- ethanol and other metabolites.
The actual toxicity depends on concentration, crop species, soil properties, and duration.

18. Root Death
Prolonged flooding can cause:
- root respiration failure
- membrane damage
- nutrient imbalance
- accumulation of toxic metabolites
- root decay.
Eventually:
Flooding→root death    

19. Flooding and Water Uptake
Interestingly, a flooded plant can experience physiological water stress even though there is abundant
water around it.
Why?
Because damaged roots cannot transport water efficiently.
Thus:
Flooded soil≠healthy water uptake    
This is a key agricultural concept.

20. Flooding and Photosynthesis
Flooding can reduce photosynthesis through several mechanisms:
- leaf submergence
- reduced light penetration
- stomatal changes
- root damage
- nutrient deficiency
- chlorophyll degradation
- reduced CO₂ availability under water.
Therefore:

Flooding→photosynthesis↓     

21. Submerged Leaves
When leaves are underwater:
- light availability decreases
- gas diffusion becomes extremely slow
- CO₂ availability changes
- O₂ exchange is restricted.
This can severely reduce photosynthesis in crops not adapted to submergence.

22. Flooding and Stomata
Flooding can alter stomatal behavior.
Root oxygen deficiency can generate chemical and hormonal signals that affect the shoot.
Possible responses include:
Root stress→hormonal signaling→stomatal conductance changes 
   

23. Flooding and Root-to-Shoot Signaling
Roots under flooding can produce signals involving:
- ABA
- ethylene
- reactive oxygen species
- other signaling molecules.
These signals can alter:
- stomatal behavior
- leaf growth
- senescence
- root architecture.

24. Ethylene Under Flooding
Ethylene is particularly important because its gaseous movement out of submerged tissues becomes
restricted.
As a result:
Flooding→ethylene accumulation    
Ethylene can contribute to responses such as:
- leaf senescence
- aerenchyma formation
- adventitious roots
- shoot elongation in some species.

## 25. Aerenchyma
Some flood-tolerant plants form aerenchyma, specialized internal air spaces that facilitate oxygen
movement from shoots toward roots.
Conceptually:
Shoot oxygen→aerenchyma→root zone     
This can improve root survival under waterlogged conditions.

26. Adventitious Roots
Flood-tolerant plants may produce adventitious roots near the soil/water surface.
These roots can:
- access better-aerated zones
- improve water/nutrient uptake
- help plants survive flooding.

27. Flood Escape
Some flood-adapted plants rapidly elongate shoots to keep leaves above floodwater.
This is called a flood-escape strategy.

The concept is:
Water level rises→rapid shoot elongation→leaves remain above water 
   
This strategy is highly species- and genotype-dependent.

28. Submergence Tolerance
Some plants survive temporary complete submergence by reducing metabolic activity and conserving
carbohydrates.
Thus two broad strategies can be seen:

Escape strategy
Grow upward rapidly.

Quiescence/tolerance strategy
Reduce growth and conserve resources until floodwater recedes.

29. Flooding and Plant Growth
Flooding can reduce:
- root growth
- shoot growth
- leaf expansion
- branching
- tillering.
Therefore:
Flooding→growth rate↓     

30. Flooding and Seed Germination
Flooding can interfere with germination because seeds require oxygen for normal metabolic activation.
Flooding→O2↓→seed respiration↓→germination/emergence↓   O 2      
However, some species have adaptations for germination under flooded conditions.

31. Flooding and Seedlings
Seedlings are often highly vulnerable because they have:
- small energy reserves
- poorly developed roots
- limited photosynthetic capacity.
Flooding can cause:
- seedling death
- poor establishment
- damping-off
- nutrient deficiency.

32. Flooding During Vegetative Growth
During vegetative growth, flooding can cause:
- reduced root activity
- leaf yellowing
- reduced tillering/branching
- nutrient deficiency
- disease
- reduced biomass.

33. Flooding During Flowering
Flooding around flowering can reduce:
- flower production
- pollen viability
- pollen release
- pollination
- fertilization
- fruit/seed set.

Thus:
Flooding during flowering→reproductive success↓ 
   

34. Flooding and Grain Formation
In cereal crops, flooding can reduce:
- spikelet fertility
- grain number
- grain filling
- grain weight.
The effect depends strongly on:
- crop
- genotype
- water depth
- duration
- temperature
- developmental stage.

35. Flooding and Fruit Crops
Fruit crops may suffer from:
- root asphyxiation
- flower drop
- fruit drop
- fruit cracking
- nutrient disorders
- root diseases
- reduced fruit quality.
Perennial plants may experience damage that persists after the flood has disappeared because roots can
remain injured.

36. Flooding and Senescence
Flooding can accelerate leaf senescence.
A simplified pathway is:
Flood stress→root dysfunction+hormonal/ROS changes→chlorophyll loss→senescence   +      

37. Flooding and Reactive Oxygen Species
When flooding ends and oxygen rapidly returns, plants can experience oxidative stress.
This is sometimes called reoxygenation stress.
The sequence is:
Flooding→low O2→metabolic disruption→reoxygenation→ROS↑    2    ROS  
Antioxidant systems help protect tissues.

38. Flooding and Disease
Flooded conditions often favor:
- root rots
- damping-off
- fungal diseases
- oomycete diseases
- bacterial diseases.
High humidity and prolonged leaf wetness can further increase disease risk.
Flooding→humidity/leaf wetness↑→disease risk↑       

39. Flooding and Weeds
Flooding can alter weed populations.
Some weeds are highly sensitive to flooding, while others are flood tolerant.

After floodwater recedes, disturbed soil can also create favorable conditions for weed emergence.

40. Flooding and Soil Erosion
Fast-moving floodwater can cause severe erosion.
The process is:
Floodwater velocity↑→soil particle detachment→transport→soil loss      
It can remove:
- topsoil
- organic matter
- nutrients
- seeds
- seedlings.

## 41. Sedimentation
Floodwater can deposit sediments onto agricultural fields.
This can have both positive and negative effects.

Possible benefits
- deposition of fine mineral material
- nutrient addition in some floodplain systems.

Possible problems
- burial of seedlings
- altered soil texture
- poor infiltration
- contamination
- blocked drainage.

42. Flooding and Soil Structure
Flooding followed by drying can alter soil structure.
Potential problems include:
- compaction
- aggregate disruption
- surface crusting
- reduced infiltration.
Machinery operation on saturated soil can make compaction particularly severe.

43. Do Not Enter Saturated Fields With Heavy
Machinery
When soil is very wet:
Heavy machinery→soil compaction↑     
Compaction can reduce:
- pore space
- root penetration
- infiltration
- soil aeration.
Therefore field traffic should generally be minimized until soil has sufficiently drained.

44. Flooding and Nutrient Loss
Flooding can cause:
Leaching+Runoff+Denitrification+erosion  +   +
  +    
leading to nutrient loss.
However, flooding can also change nutrient availability rather than simply reduce every nutrient.

45. Flooding and Nitrogen
The major mechanisms are:
N loss=Leaching+Denitrification+Runoff  =   +
  +    
Nitrogen deficiency may appear after floodwaters recede.

46. Flooding and Phosphorus
Phosphorus can be transported with:
- eroded soil
- sediments
- runoff.
Flooding can also alter phosphorus chemistry in reduced soils.
Therefore phosphorus behavior is complex and soil-dependent.

47. Flooding and Potassium
Potassium can be lost through:
- leaching
- runoff
- erosion.
The extent depends on:
- soil type
- cation exchange capacity
- rainfall/flood duration
- soil chemistry.

48. Flooding and Soil Microorganisms
Flooding changes the microbial environment.
As oxygen disappears:

Aerobic microbes↓   
while anaerobic microbial processes become more important.
These processes influence:
- carbon cycling
- nitrogen cycling
- sulfur cycling
- iron/manganese chemistry.

49. Flooding and Greenhouse Gases
Waterlogged soils can promote production of gases such as:
- methane (CH4CH 4)
- nitrous oxide (N2ON 2O) under particular conditions.
The exact emissions depend on:
- soil
- temperature
- organic matter
- redox conditions
- fertilizer
- duration.
Thus flooding can have implications beyond crop yield.

50. Flooding and Temperature
Flooding injury often becomes more severe under warm conditions because:
- metabolism is faster
- oxygen is consumed faster
- microbial activity increases
- oxygen solubility in water decreases as temperature rises.
Therefore:

Warm flooding→potentially faster oxygen depletion    
This is one reason flood tolerance can vary with season and temperature.

51. Flooding and Light
Deep water and cloudy weather can reduce radiation.
Underwater leaves may receive very little light.
Therefore:
Flooding + cloudiness→light availability↓→photosynthesis↓ 
     

52. Flooding and Harvest
Flooding near harvest can cause:
- delayed harvesting
- lodged crops
- wet grain
- fruit damage
- soil compaction
- machinery access problems
- grain sprouting
- fungal contamination.

53. Flooding and Post-Harvest Quality
Wet harvested products may require rapid drying.
Problems include:
- fungal growth
- microbial spoilage
- reduced seed viability
- reduced processing quality

- storage losses.
Therefore:
Flood→wet harvest→drying/storage challenge     

54. Crop Tolerance to Flooding
Crops differ greatly in flood tolerance.
Some crops are relatively sensitive, while others have specialized adaptations.
Examples of adaptation mechanisms include:
- aerenchyma
- adventitious roots
- shoot elongation
- carbohydrate conservation
- anaerobic metabolism
- submergence tolerance.
Rice is a classic example of a crop with substantial adaptation to flooded conditions, but not all
rice varieties tolerate deep or prolonged flooding equally.

55. Flood Tolerance Is Not the Same as Flood
Proof
A flood-tolerant crop can survive certain:
- depths
- durations
- temperatures
- developmental stages.
It does not mean it can survive every flood.
Therefore:
Flood tolerance=f(crop+genotype+depth+duration+stage)  =
f( + + + + )  

56. Flooding at Different Crop Stages
Crop stage

Major flooding effects
Seed
Poor oxygen supply, seed decay
Germination
Delayed/failed emergence
Seedling
High mortality
Vegetative
Root damage, nutrient deficiency
Tillering/branching Reduced growth
Flower initiation
Developmental disturbance
Flowering
Pollination problems
Fertilization
Reduced reproductive success
Fruit/grain formation Poor set
Grain filling
Reduced grain weight
Maturity
Sprouting/quality problems
Harvest
Access and yield losses
Post-harvest
Drying/storage problems

57. Flooding and Yield Components
A simplified yield equation is:
Yield≈Plants/area×Reproductive units/plant×Weight/unit      
Flooding can reduce all three.
For cereals:
Grain yield≈Plants/area×Grains/plant×Grain weight      

58. Flood Management
Flood management has three major stages:
Pre-flood→During flood→Post-flood     

59. Before Flooding
Important preparation measures include:

Drainage
Maintain:
- field drains
- channels
- outlets.

Field leveling
Appropriate leveling can improve water movement and reduce low spots.

Raised beds
Useful for suitable crops and soils.

Crop selection
Choose varieties appropriate for local flood risk.

Water storage
Farm ponds and other structures can sometimes reduce uncontrolled runoff and store water for later
use.

60. During Flooding
Priorities include:
- maintain drainage pathways
- avoid unnecessary field traffic
- monitor water depth and duration
- protect irrigation/drainage infrastructure
- monitor livestock and human safety
- avoid entering dangerous floodwater.
Agricultural decisions should prioritize safety before crop salvage.

61. After Flooding
Assess:
- crop survival
- root condition

- water depth/duration
- nutrient status
- disease
- erosion
- sediment deposition
- soil compaction
- crop maturity.
Then decide whether to:
- retain the crop
- replant
- provide nutrients
- manage disease
- improve drainage.

62. Replanting Decision
Replanting depends on:
Plant survival+remaining growing season+crop duration+soil condition+seed availability+expected yiel
d  +   +   +   +   +    
It should not be based solely on visible leaf damage.

63. Fertilizer After Flooding
Do not automatically apply large amounts of fertilizer immediately after flooding.
First assess:
- nutrient losses
- crop stage
- root recovery
- soil condition
- soil-test information

- remaining yield potential.
Nutrients, especially nitrogen, may have been lost, but the correct replacement depends on
circumstances.

64. Disease Management After Flooding
After floodwater recedes, monitor for:
- root rot
- stem rot
- leaf diseases
- bacterial diseases
- damping-off.
Disease management should follow crop-specific recommendations and applicable pesticide
labels/regulations.

65. Soil Management After Flooding
Allow soil to drain sufficiently before intensive cultivation.
Avoid:
Wet soil+heavy machinery +   
because this can create long-lasting compaction.
Once soil reaches a suitable moisture condition, management may include:
- controlled tillage where appropriate
- residue management
- erosion repair
- drainage improvement
- soil testing.

66. Flooding and Climate Resilience
Flood-resilient agriculture can include:

Crop level
- tolerant varieties
- appropriate planting dates
- diversified crops.

Field level
- drainage
- raised beds
- field leveling
- soil structure improvement.

Landscape level
- wetlands
- floodplain management
- watershed management
- water storage
- erosion control.

67. Flooding and Integrated Water Management
The goal is not simply:
"Remove all water."
Rather:
Capture+Store+Drain+Recharge+Use efficiently  +   +  
+   +    
This connects flood management with drought management.
Excess water during one period can potentially become a water resource during a later dry period if
safely captured and stored.

68. Important Flooding Indicators
Field indicators include:
- standing water

- saturated soil
- yellowing leaves
- wilting despite wet soil
- root browning
- root decay
- leaf chlorosis
- stunted growth
- lodging
- disease
- erosion
- sediment deposition.

69. Physiological Indicators
More quantitative measurements include:
- root oxygen status
- soil redox potential
- chlorophyll content
- photosynthetic rate
- stomatal conductance
- chlorophyll fluorescence
- root biomass
- survival percentage
- yield components.
A simple survival measure is:
Survival %=Plants survivingPlants exposed×100  =         100  

70. Important Relationships
Flooding → oxygen deficiency
Flooding→O2↓   O 2  

Oxygen deficiency → root respiration decline
O2↓→ATP↓   

Root damage → nutrient uptake decline
Root function↓→nutrient uptake↓     

Flooding → disease
Flooding→humidity/leaf wetness↑→disease risk↑       

Flooding → erosion
Fast-moving water→soil detachment→erosion     

71. Flooding vs Drought
These are opposite water extremes, but both can damage crops.

dehydrated

signals

72. Flooding vs Excess Rainfall vs Waterlogging
Concept
Core meaning
Excess rainfall Rainfall exceeds system capacity
Runoff
Water flowing over land
Ponding
Water temporarily standing on surface
Waterlogging Root-zone saturation causing poor aeration
Flooding
Land inundated by water
Drainage
Removal of excess water
These processes can form a chain:
Excess rainfall→Runoff/ponding→Flooding→Waterlogging→O2↓→Root stress      O 2   

73. Complete Flooding Mechanism
The entire process can be summarized as:
Heavy rainfall/river overflow/etc.   
↓
Land inundation   
↓
Soil saturation   
↓
Air-filled pore space↓   
↓
O2 diffusion↓   
↓
Root aerobic respiration↓   
↓
ATP↓ 
↓
Root growth + nutrient uptake↓   
↓

Photosynthesis + plant growth↓   
↓
Flowering/reproduction/grain or fruit development↓   
↓
